*{Imnah, and Ishuah, and Ishuai, and Beriah, and Serah their sister}* These went down with Jacob into Egypt, Gen. 46:17. These sons were heads of fair families called by their name, Num. 26:44.

**V. 31.** *{And the sons of Beriah, Heber, and Malchiel}* These two also went down to Egypt, Gen. 46:17, and were heads of families, Num. 26:44, 45.

*{who is the father of Birzavith}* Birzavith was a city, and Malchiel the founder or governor thereof. See ch. 2:42.

**V. 32.** *{And Heber begat Japhlet, and Shomer}* Or, *Shamer*, v. 34.

*{and Hotham, and Shuah their sister}* Such women as are brought into genealogies were of special note.

**V. 33.** *{And the sons of Japhlet, Pasach, etc.}* The memorandum, These are the children of Japhlet, shows that these were eminent persons.

**V. 34.** *{And the sons of Shamer}* Called Shomer, v. 32.

**V. 35.** *{And the sons of his brother Helem, etc.}* Helem is supposed to be the brother of Shamer, v. 34, and the same that is called Hotham, v. 32.

*{Zophah, etc.}* These four were all sons of Helem.

**V. 36.** *{The sons of Zophah, Suah, etc.}* Eleven in all.

**V. 37.** *{Jether}* Or, *Ithram*, v. 38.

**V. 38.** *{The sons of Jether}* Called Ithram, v. 37.

*{Jephunneh, etc.}* Caleb’s father was also of this name, ch. 4:15.

**V. 39.** *{And the sons of Ulla, Arah, etc.}* Ulla is not before mentioned: but the order of setting him down implies that he was either one of the sons of Jether, v. 38, or should have been added to those that are named before.

**V. 40.** *{All these were children of Asher}* All from v. 30 to this.

*{heads of their fathers’ house}* From whom several families received their denomination.

*{choice}* Not of ordinary parts, but very eminent persons, to be preferred before many others.

*{and mighty men of valour}* See ch. 5:24 & 2 King. 24:14.

*{chief of the princes}* Or, *heads of the princes*. There were some governors over sundry persons and places, and others that had oversight over such governors, to whom they were to give an account: such are here meant. See Dan. 6:1, 2.

*{and the number throughout the genealogy of them}* The number of the several members of distinct families.

*{that were apt to the war}* That had been trained up unto and well exercised in military exploits, and fitted for the war.

*{and to battle}* To stand face to face, foot to foot, in pitched battles against enemies.

*{was twenty and six thousand}* These were a great many, but came far short of the children of Issachar, v. 5.

## CHAPTER 8

**Verse 1.** *{Now Benjamin}* Benjamin’s genealogy was in brief set down ch. 7:6, 13. Here it is more largely expressed. Two especial reasons might move the penman hereunto: I. The first king of Israel was chosen out of this tribe; and by this large genealogy he comes to him, v. 33. II. This tribe held close to Judah when the other tribes revolted, 1 King. 12:23 and 2 Chron. 11:1. Now it was the special drift of this sacred historian to set forth the kingdom of Judah especially.

*{begat Bela his firstborn}* Gen. 46:21. Num. 26:38.

*{Ashbel the second}* Called Jediael, ch. 7:6. Ashbel is set in the third place Gen. 46:21, so is Jediael ch. 7:6, but Num. 26:38 Ashbel is set in the second place, as here. Seeing this term second is here expressly set down, we are to take Ashbel to be the second son born, though it may be Becher was in some dignity preferred before him.

*{and Aharah the third}* Called Ehi, Gen. 46:21, and Ahiram, Num. 26:38, so that he had three names.

**V. 2.** *{Nohah the fourth}* Called Becher, Gen. 46:21, ch. 7:6, and set in the second place.

*{and Rapha the fifth}* Called Rosh, Gen. 46:21. There are ten reckoned up as sons of Benjamin, Gen. 46:21, five of which are these here named: the other five were his grandchildren, namely Gera the son of Bela; Naaman, Muppim, called Shephuphan; Huppim, called Huram; and Ard, called Addar; all expressed v. 3, 4, 5.

**V. 3.** *{And the sons of Bela were Addar}* Or, *Ard*, Gen. 46:21.

*{and Gera, and Abihud, etc.}* Here are nine sons of Bela; yet it is said ch. 7:7 that his sons were five; and none of these five are reckoned here. But five of these here set down might be Bela’s grandchildren; as five of Benjamin’s grandchildren are reckoned among his sons, Gen. 46:21. Besides, it is very frequent in Scripture for the same persons to have divers names. See ch. 3:15, 16.

**V. 5.** *{And Gera}* This might be son of the former Gera, v. 3.

*{and Shephuphan}* Or, *Muppim*, Gen. 46:21, or, Shupham, Num. 26:39.

*{and Huram}* Or, *Huppim*, Gen. 46:21, or, Hupham, Num. 26:39. These two last named had each of them three names.

**V. 6.** *{And these are the sons of Ehud}* These has reference to v. 7, so as all the words of this verse after Ehud are to be included in a parenthesis. There is mention of Ehud the son of Gera a Benjamite, or *a son of Jemini*, who was the second judge of Israel, Judg. 3:15. If this be that Ehud, then questionless he was many degrees distant from Benjamin.

*{these are the heads of the fathers of the inhabitants of Geba}* Or, *Gibeah*, 1 Sam. 11:4. They which are mentioned v. 7, etc. were chief governors of Geba, and in that respect are called heads of the fathers of the inhabitants, or chief of their fathers’ families.

*{and they removed them}* The forenamed governors removed the inhabitants from Geba to a more spacious and commodious place, because they were so many as they could not well continue together. See the like Gen. 13:6 & 26:16.

*{to Manahath}* Manahath is the name of a man Gen. 36:23, ch. 1:40, but here, of a place, from whence the title Manahethites was taken, ch. 2:54.

**V. 7.** *{And Naaman}* If this have relation to Ehud, v. 6, the particle *and* may be translated *even*, and the words joined thus, *These are the sons of Ehud, even Naaman, and Ahiah, and Gera*. There is mention of three such names, v. 3, 4, but these haply were other persons than they.

*{he removed them}* Ehud showed himself provident herein, being careful to provide fit inheritances for his children.

*{and begat Uzza, and Ahihud}* Or, *after he had begotten*. The increase of his children made him the more provident.

**V. 8.** *{And Shaharaim}* Because no mention is made of Shaharaim before, he is taken to be one of Ehud’s children.

*{begat children in the country of Moab}* It is not probable his father would remove him out of Israel into Moab; but upon some other occasion being there he begot children in that land.

*{after he had sent them away; Hushim and Baara were his wives}* This clause is diversely taken; by some thus, *After he had sent away Hushim and Baara his wives*, by giving them a bill of divorce, he begat the said children: by others thus, *After Ehud had sent his sons away, Shaharaim begat children in Moab*. Of Hushim and Baara see ch. 7:12.

**V. 9.** *{And he begat of Hodesh his wife}* Some take Hodesh to be the same that was called Baara v. 8.

*{Jobab, etc.}* These seven, Jobab, Zibia, Mesha, Malcham, Jeuz, Shachiah, and Mirma, were the posterity of Shaharaim.

**V. 10.** *{These were his sons, heads of the fathers}* Prime men, and princes of families.

**V. 11.** *{And of Hushim}* This was Shaharaim’s first wife, v. 8.

*{he begat Ahitub, and Elpaal}* These were brothers to the forementioned princes by the father’s side, but not by the mothers.

**V. 12.** *{The sons of Elpaal, Eber, and Misham, and Shamed}* We read of other sons of Elpaal v. 18. But those were such as dwelt in Jerusalem, these dwelt elsewhere.

*{who built Ono, and Lod}* In the tribe of Benjamin, Neh. 11:35. See Ezra 2:33. Neh. 7:37. It is probable these sons of Elpaal dwelt in those cities.

*{with the towns thereof}* Heb. *daughters*. See ch. 7:28.

**V. 13.** *{Beriah also}* Of this name see ch. 7:23.

*{and Shema}* These two were brothers.

*{who were heads of the fathers of the inhabitants}* See v. 6.

*{of Aijalon}* Aialon see ch. 6:69. Here it must be taken for the country bordering on the city Aialon. A city called Ajalon was in the tribe of Dan, Josh. 19:42.

*{who drove away the inhabitants of Gath}* Some refer this to the history ch. 7:21, as if these princes of Benjamin, in revenge of that slaughter committed on the Ephraimites, had entered the country of the Philistims, and driven away the men of Gath. Others take it indefinitely, without relation to any other history, and say that these children of Benjamin expelled the Philistims from Gath, and dwelt there themselves. There is a third opinion, That these Benjamites after the captivity so far enlarged their dwellings, as they possessed some of the cities of Dan, and drove the enemies thence. But it cannot well stand with the series of this genealogy to put off this history to so long a time. The second opinion is the most probable.

**V. 14.** *{And Ahio, etc.}* From hence to v. 29 is set down a catalogue of such children of Benjamin as were willing to dwell in Jerusalem. These are distinguished by their several parentages. The first nine v. 14, 15, 16 were the sons of Beriah, v. 13.

**V. 17.** *{And Zebadiah, etc.}* The seven named v. 17, 18 were the sons of Elpaal. See v. 11, 12.

**V. 19.** *{And Jakim, etc.}* The nine named v. 19, 20, 21 were the sons of Shimhi, or Shema, v. 13.

**V. 22.** *{And Ishpan, etc.}* The eleven set down v. 22, 23, 24, 25 were sons of Shashak. The second man in this catalogue was named Shashak, v. 14, but they were different persons.

**V. 26.** *{And Shamsherai}* The six v. 26, 27 were the sons of Jeroham. There were men of other tribes of this name, ch. 6:27 & 27:22.

**V. 28.** *{These were heads of the fathers by their generations}* See v. 6 and ch. 7:2.

*{chief men}* Such as are described ch. 7:40.

*{These dwelt in Jerusalem}* See v. 14. Jerusalem was built within the tribe of Benjamin, Josh. 12:63. Judg. 1:21. People found such profit and delight in dwelling in the country, that they thanked such as were willing to dwell in Jerusalem, Neh. 11:12.

**V. 29.** *{And at Gibeon dwelt the father of Gibeon}* See 1 King. 3:4. This father of Gibeon is expressly said to be Jehiel, ch. 9:35. Here the title Gibeon is attributed to a place and person.

*{whose wife’s name was Maachah}* There was another Maachah the wife of Machir, who was certainly a woman of excellent parts, in that she is once and again named with her husband, ch. 7:15, 16.

**V. 30.** *{And his firstborn son Abdon}* There was a judge of this name, Judg. 12:13, but of another tribe. The particle and has not reference to first-born (for all of them could not be first-born) but indefinitely to the title son.

*{and Zur, etc.}* Here are nine set down to be the sons of Gibeon.

**V. 31.** *{And Zacher}* Or, *Zechariah*, ch. 9:37.

**V. 32.** *{And Mikloth}* This Mikloth is reckoned among the sons of Jehiel, ch. 9:37.

*{begat Shimeah}* Or, *Shimram*, ch. 9:38.

*{And these also dwelt with their brethren}* i.e. With such as were of the same tribe, Benjamites, which are reckoned up v. 14, etc.

*{in Jerusalem}* See v. 28.

*{over against them}* Some on one side, some on the other. Or this may have reference to such as dwelt at Gibeon, v. 29, which was over against Jerusalem.

**V. 33.** *{And Ner begat Kish}* Ner was also called Abiel, 1 Sam. 9:1. He had also another son besides Kish, of his own name, who was the father of Abner, 1 Sam. 14:50, 51. See ch. 9:36.

*{and Kish begat Saul}* Saul was the first king of Israel; for whose sake the genealogy of Benjamin is thus distinctly set out.

*{and Saul begat Jonathan}* Jonathan was in disposition far different from his father. Saul was most impious against God, and envious against David a man after God’s own heart; but Jonathan pious to God, and tender-hearted to David.

*{and Malchi-shua, and Abinadab}* These two together with Jonathan were slain in that battle wherein Saul himself lost his life, 1 Sam. 31:2.

*{and Eshbaal}* Called Ishbosheth, 2 Sam. 2:8, etc.

**V. 34.** *{And the son of Jonathan was Merib-baal}* Or, *Mephibosheth*, 2 Sam. 4:4. To him David showed great kindness for his father Jonathan’s sake, 2 Sam. 9:1, etc., and preserved him when the rest of Saul’s posterity were destroyed, 2 Sam. 21:6, 7. From hence to the end of the chapter the posterity of Jonathan is set down. The rest of Saul’s posterity was extinct: but God had respect to the piety of Jonathan, and preserved his posterity a long while, as the succeeding genealogy shows. For *the memory of the just is blessed*, Prov. 10:7.

**V. 35.** *{Tirea}* Or, *Tahrea*, ch. 9:41.

**V. 36.** *{Jehoadah}* Or, *Jarah*, ch. 9:42.

**V. 37.** *{Raphah}* Or, *Rephaiah*, ch. 9:43.

**V. 40.** *{And the sons of Ulam were mighty men of valour}* See ch. 7:40.

*{archers}* Heb. *drawing the bow*. Or, *treading the bow*. They used bows of steel, and treading upon them with their feet, employed the strength of both their arms to bring the bowstring up to the notch. We use crossbows so. They were very expert in shooting. See ch. 5:18.

*{and had many sons, and sons’ sons, an hundred and fifty}* This numerous progeny of Ulam is the rather here set down because he descended from Jonathan.

*{All these are of the sons of Benjamin}* Though all this tribe were almost utterly destroyed, Judg. 20:48, yet by God’s blessing on them they came again to be a great tribe.

## CHAPTER 9

**Verse 1.** *{So all Israel were reckoned by genealogies}* This is a conclusion of the former genealogies that were before the captivity, and a transition to the latter that were after the captivity, following in this chapter.

*{and behold}* This note of attention shows that such genealogies as are registered in sacred Scripture are matters remarkable. They give evidence of God’s providence in continuing his church generation after generation.

*{they were written in the book of the kings of Israel and Judah}* The book here mentioned must needs be another than that we now have of sacred Scripture. For many of the genealogies here set down, are not therein recorded. Yet had this historian great help from Scripture, especially in the most ancient genealogies.

*{who were carried away to Babylon}* Though the ten tribes were carried captive into Assyria, 2 King. 17:6, yet many of them remaining in their land, and associating themselves with Judah, 2 Chron. 30:11, were carried into Babylon: and in this respect it may be said that both Israel and Judah were carried into Babylon.

*{for their transgression}* Transgression is a collective word, and comprises under it many things. See 2 Chr. 36:14, etc.

**V. 2.** *{Now the first inhabitants}* By these are meant such as came to the land of Israel at the first return from captivity in the first year of Cyrus, Ezra 2:1, etc. There were others that afterwards returned with Ezra in the reign of Artaxerxes, Ezra 8:1, etc.

*{that dwelt in their possessions}* Their own inheritances which they enjoyed before the captivity.

*{in their cities}* Whether the great city Jerusalem, or other cities throughout the land. Every one took possession of what had formerly belonged to him.

*{were the Israelites, the priests, Levites, and the Nethinims}* These were four ancient orders whereby that people were distinguished. And this distinction continued all the time of the captivity, and afterwards. The first sort Israelites, were such as we call secular, civil, or laymen. The second, priests, were the posterity of Aaron. The third, Levites, were the other posterity of Levi. The fourth, Nethinims, were such as were given or dedicated unto God and to his service, or who subjected themselves thereunto, though they were not native Israelites. The Gibeonites, who did voluntarily yield to be *hewers of wood and drawers of water for the house of God*, Josh. 9:23, etc., are especially comprised under this title. See Ezra 2:58 & 8:20. Though these Nethinims were originally of the cursed Canaanites, and put to the most servile works, Josh. 9:21, yet because they submitted themselves to be servants of the Lord, and remained faithful to God’s people, the Lord much honored them by revenging the wrong done them, 2 Sam. 21:1, and here by putting them into the lasting catalogue of his people. To these are added *the children of Solomon’s servants*, Neh. 11:3.

**V. 3.** *{And in Jerusalem dwelt of the children of Judah, and of the children of Benjamin}* These two tribes had most right to dwell in Jerusalem, it being situated in them both; yet some of these tribes also dwelt abroad in other places of the land. See Neh. 11:1, etc.

*{and of the children of Ephraim and Manasseh}* Under these two others of the ten tribes are comprised by a synecdoche: for many of them joined themselves with Judah at the defection of Jeroboam, 2 Chron. 11:16, and others also after the ten tribes were carried captive into Assyria, 2 Chron. 30:11, 18. Yea, after Judah’s return from Babylon many of the ten tribes came in unto them. All these sorts of Israelites may be here intended.

**V. 4.** *{Uthai}* This catalogue is also set down, Neh. 11:4, etc., but with alteration of many names. Uthai is called Athaiah, Neh. 11:4.

*{the son of Ammihud}* Or, *Adaiah*, Neh. 11:5.

*{the son of Omri}* Or, *Zechariah*, Neh. 11:5. The other names that follow are also changed; and whereas there are here but five sons of Pharez in a lineal descent, there is one more added.

*{of the children of Pharez}* Called Perez, Neh. 11:4.

*{the son of Judah}* See Gen. 38:18, 29.

**V. 5.** *{And of the Shilonites}* The father of these is called Shiloni, Neh. 11:5, and taken to be Shelah, Gen. 38:5.

*{Asaiah the firstborn}* Called Maaseiah, Neh. 11:5.

*{and his sons}* Neh. 11:5. Six by name are set down by lineal descent from Shiloni to Asaiah, not here named; whereby it appears that this relative his has reference to Shiloni, whose sons they were.

**V. 6.** *{And of the sons of Zerah}* Zerah the great-grandfather of Achan, Josh. 1:1, may be here meant.

*{Jeuel, and their brethren}* Their has reference both to Zerah and Jeuel. Under the word brethren all manner of kindred and alliance, yea such as were of the same tribe, family, or office, are oft comprised in these genealogies, v. 9, 25, ch. 6:39, 44, 48 & 7:5.

*{six hundred and ninety}* All these were heads of families.

**V. 7.** *{And of the sons of Benjamin, Sallu the son of Meshullam, the son of Hodaviah}* This Hodaviah is called Joed, Neh. 11:7.

*{the son of Hasenuah}* Or, *Pedaiah*, Neh. 11:7.

**V. 8.** *{And Ibneiah, etc.}* To the four sons of Benjamin mentioned v. 7, here are added nine more, all of the posterity of Benjamin; so as there were thirteen in all by their generations.

**V. 9.** *{And their brethren}* Kindred, fellow citizens, etc., as v. 6.

*{according to their generations}* Their distinct families.

*{nine hundred and fifty and six}* In Neh. 11:8, where mention is made of the same sons of Benjamin the number is said to be but nine hundred twenty and eight. The reason of this difference may be this; there were some that by lot were appointed to dwell in Jerusalem, others willingly offered themselves to dwell there, Neh. 11:1, 2: of the one sort there might be nine hundred twenty and eight; and of the other, twenty-eight; both which sums make up nine hundred fifty and six.

*{All these men were chief of the fathers in the house of their fathers}* Both the forementioned numbers, of six hundred and ninety, and nine hundred fifty-six, are to be applied to those here called chief of the fathers; so as their whole number was one thousand six hundred forty and six. If there were so many principal heads, the number of those under them must needs be very great.

**V. 10.** *{And of the priests, Jedaiah, etc.}* Here he begins to set down the second order mentioned v. 2.

**V. 11.** *{And Azariah}* Or, *Seraiah*, Neh. 11:11.

*{the son of Hilkiah}* He whose history is recorded, 2 King. 12:4, etc.

*{the son of Meshullam}* Or, *Shallum*, ch. 6:12.

*{the son of Zadok, the son of Meraioth, etc.}* Meraioth is left out in the catalogue of priests, ch. 6:11, 12, and Ezra 7:2.

*{the ruler of the house of God}* This has reference to Azariah, who was the chief of those heads mentioned v. 13. He was next to the high priest; as Eleazar while Aaron lived, Num. 3:32. And as he was next in order to the high priest, so he had a special command over all the Levites, to order and dispose the things of the house of the Lord.

**V. 12.** *{And Adaiah the son of Jeroham}* There is a Jeroham of another tribe, v. 8.

*{the son of Pashur}* Three generations are here left out, Pelaliah, Amzi and Zechariah, who are mentioned Neh. 11:12, so as Jeroham was the fourth from Pashur.

*{the son of Malchijah}* After mention of Malchijah Neh. 11:12, these words are added, *And their brethren that did the work of the house, were eight hundred twenty and two*, which number is inserted to show God’s blessing on them, in preserving so many of them in captivity.

*{and Maasai the son of Adiel, the son of Jahzerah}* Or, *Amashai, the son of Azareel, the son of Ahasai*, Neh. 11:13.

*{the son of Meshullam}* This man is omitted Neh. 11:13.

*{the son of Meshillemith}* Or, *Meshilemoth*, Neh. 11:13.

**V. 13.** *{And their brethren}* See v. 9.

*{heads of the house of their fathers}* This shows their dignity.

*{a thousand and seven hundred and threescore}* This shows God’s providence over them, see v. 12.

*{very able men}* Heb. *mighty men of valour*. This shows their courage and strength.

*{for the work of the service of the house of God}* This shows wherein they exercised the courage of their mind, and to what they put the strength of their body, namely, the service of the Lord.

**V. 14.** *{And of the Levites}* These were the third degree, v. 2.

*{Shemaiah, etc.}* Here are four generations, Shemaiah, Hashub, Azrikam, Hashabiah, which under these names are also set down Neh. 11:15, and unto them a fifth is added, namely Bunni.

*{of the sons of Merari}* They were all of the posterity of Merari.

**V. 15.** *{And Bakbakkar, Heresh, and Galal}* These three are not mentioned under these names, Neh. 11:15, 16, but it may be they are there called Bunni, Shabbethai, and Jozabad. Of the two latter of these it is there said, that they were of the chief of the Levites, and *had the oversight of the outward business of the house of God*, i.e. their office was to take care of those things which were done out of the house of the Lord. See ch. 26:29.

*{and Mattaniah the son of Micah, the son of Zichri}* Or, *Zabdi*, Neh. 11:17.

*{the son of Asaph}* See ch. 6:39. Neh. 11:17. This Asaph is said to be *the principal to begin the thanksgiving in prayer*. He used to set tunes to psalms of praise, and to begin those tunes.

**V. 16.** *{And Obadiah the son of Shemaiah}* Or, *Abdah, the son of Shammua*, Neh. 11:17. Before these there is set down Bakbukiah, who is supposed to be Bakbakkar mentioned v. 15. And he is said to be *the second among his brethren*, namely, next unto Asaph the principal.

*{the son of Galal}* The order shows this was another Galal than he v. 15.

*{the son of Jeduthun}* Jeduthun was also a chief singer, with Asaph and Heman, ch. 25:1, 6. 2 Chr. 5:12 & 35:15.

*{and Berechiah the son of Asa, the son of Elkanah}* There was a famous king of Judah called Asa, 2 Chr. 20:1, etc. But we do not elsewhere read of a Levite of this name. Berekiah and Elkanah were doorkeepers, ch. 15:23.

*{that dwelt in the villages of the Netophathites}* See ch. 2:54. The villages of the Netophathites were in the tribe of Judah; out of which tribe were given cities to the sons of Aaron, who were priests, and to no other of the Levites, Josh. 21:19. But at the revolt of the ten tribes the Levites that were dispersed among those tribes came to Jerusalem, 2 Chron. 11:14, and habitations allotted them out of Judah and Benjamin. Thus might the Levites dwell in the villages of the Netophathites, though they were in Judah. Of the Levites before mentioned it is said Neh. 11:18, that they were in the holy city, and that they were two hundred fourscore and four. Of villages see ch. 4:32.

**V. 17.** *{And the porters were Shallum, etc.}* There were sundry offices whereunto set families of the Levites were deputed. Some were appointed to be keepers of the doors of the temple; whose divisions are distinctly set down, ch. 26:1, etc. Their generations continued all the time of the captivity. A catalogue of them we have here: four are named, Shallum, Akkub, Talmon, and Ahiman; but Neh. 11:19, only two. All the four were overseers of the rest, and therefore here mentioned.

*{and their brethren}* See v. 6. Of these there were an hundred seventy two, Neh. 11:19. See v. 12.

*{Shallum was the chief}* He was not only an overseer of ordinary porters, but chief of the overseers.

**V. 18.** *{Who hitherto}* Even after their return from captivity.

*{waited}* Held the office of a porter.

*{in the king’s gate}* Whereby the king was wont to go in and out, to and fro, from the temple. See 1 King. 10:5 & 2 King. 16:18.

*{Eastward}* At that end of the temple which was toward the east. What is said of this particular station and office appertaining to Shallum is to be included in a parenthesis.

*{They were porters}* This has reference to all mentioned v. 17.

*{in the companies of the children of Levi}* Among those Levites that were appointed to be porters.

**V. 19.** *{And Shallum the son of Kore, etc.}* Here is shown from which of the sons of Levi Shallum descended, namely from Kohath who was a progenitor of the priests, but by another descent, ch. 6:37, 38.

*{and his brethren of the house of his father}* This is added to show in what respect the title brethren is used. See v. 6.

*{the Korathites were over the work of the service}* For to be doorkeepers, was a ministry, and a service.

*{keepers of the gates}* Heb. *threshold*. See 2 King. 12:9.

*{of the tabernacle}* This may be taken both of the tabernacle which Moses made; for there were porters appertaining to it, while it stood, 1 King. 8:4, and also of the temple, which is oft styled tabernacle.

*{and their fathers being over the host of the LORD}* i.e. Their ancestors which encamped round about the tabernacle. They lay as a host about it, and marched as a host before it, and after it, when it removed from place to place. See Num. 1:50 & 2:15.

*{were keepers of the entry}* Of the passage into the house of the Lord.

**V. 20.** *{And Phinehas the son of Eleazar}* This shows that the fathers mentioned v. 19 were such as of old ministered about the tabernacle: for this Eleazar was Aaron’s son, ch. 6:3, 4.

*{was the ruler over them in time past}* This may be spoken of Phinehas while his father Eleazar lived. For he which was next to the high priest was chief over the Levites, Num. 3:32.

*{and the LORD was with him}* The Lord guided and assisted him in the courageous acts which he did, yea, and blessed him for the same, Num. 25:11, 12 & 31:6. Some explain this place thus, He was accepted of God, and beloved of God, yea, he found favor with God.

**V. 21.** *{And Zechariah the son of Meshelemiah}* This is meant of those who lived in David’s time, ch. 26:2.

*{was porter of the door of the tabernacle of the congregation}* He was the chief of the porters that kept the chief entrance into the tabernacle which was called the tabernacle of the congregation, because there people met together to worship God. See 1 King. 8:4.

**V. 22.** *{All these which were chosen to be porters in the gates were two hundred and twelve}* This is the number of the porters who came out of the captivity.

*{These were reckoned by their genealogy in their villages}* There were some that dwelt in Jerusalem; who were there reckoned up: others, in the villages; these are here meant. See ch. 4:32, 33.

*{whom David}* See ch. 26:1, etc.

*{and Samuel}* While he ruled he might settle the orders of the Levites himself, after he had settled the ark, 1 Sam. 7:1, 5. Or he might advise with David thereabout, while they two lived together; as in a like case Gad and Nathan did, 2 Chron. 29:25.

*{the seer}* 1 Sam. 9:9. See 1 King. 17:13.

*{did ordain}* Heb. *founded*. They first made an order that should in future times remain firm and stable.

*{in their set office}* Or, *trust*. The Hebrew word properly signifies *truth*, or *faith*, or *faithfulness*. It is here put for a perpetual office, in regard of that confidence which was put in their faithfulness, or because the administration of such offices required fidelity, 1 Cor. 4:2. The same is used again in this sense v. 26.

**V. 23.** *{So they and their children}* They has reference to such Levites as lived in Samuel’s and David’s time: and under their children are comprised their posterity, generation after generation, so long as the polity of the Jews remained.

*{had the oversight of the gates of the house of the LORD}* Had the chief charge of looking to the gates of the house of God, and also of taking care that other porters under them did their duty.

*{namely, the house of the tabernacle}* God is said to dwell in the tabernacle, Exod. 25:8, and therefore it is called an house, Judg. 18:31 & 20:18. Or this is an exposition of the former clause, and so both signify one and the same thing. Or by the house of the Lord is meant that place where the ark of the Lord was settled; and by the house of the tabernacle, the tabernacle that Moses built; which was at Gibeon, ch. 16:1, 37, 39. 2 Chr. 1:3.

*{by wards}* i.e. By three several courses; some at one time, some at another. See v. 25.

**V. 24.** *{In four quarters were the porters; towards the east, west, north, and south}* In Hebrew the quarters are called *winds*, because the winds come from all parts of the world, and the west is called *the sea*, because the great sea, or Mediterranean, is on the west of Palestine. There was no entrance neither into the tabernacle nor the temple at the west end, where the most holy place was situated; but the courts round about had gates in every of the four quarters before mentioned.

**V. 25.** *{And their brethren}* Their colleagues or fellow officers. See v. 6.

*{which were in their villages}* See v. 22.

*{were to come, after seven days, from time to time}* i.e. Week after week. For being divided into four and twenty courses, ch. 26:1, etc., some tarried at home, and others at that time did the services of the temple. See 2 King. 11:5.

*{with them}* This has reference to those four chief ones v. 17, who dwelt in Jerusalem, and so did their service in the temple continually. See v. 26.

**V. 26.** *{For these Levites, the four chief porters}* This verifies the last note on v. 25. For these four porters are those which are named v. 17.

*{were in their set office}* Or, *trust*. See v. 22.

*{and were over the chambers}* Or, *storehouses*.

*{and treasuries of the house of God}* See 1 King. 6:5. The porters had the charge not only of looking to the doors and passages, but also of all the treasures in the house of God. Treasuries are places where treasures are laid.

**V. 27.** *{And they lodged round about the house of God}* In certain chambers built about it. See 1 King. 6:5.

*{because the charge was upon them}* It became them well to discharge their charge: and that they might the better do it, they did not only wait at the doors all the day, but also lodged near thereunto in the nighttime.

*{and the opening thereof}* Of the doors of the temple.

*{every morning}* Heb. *even from morning to morning*. Betimes in the morning were the doors to be opened; for they were shut in the evenings.

*{pertained to them}* Belonged to their charge.

**V. 28.** *{And certain of them had the charge of the ministering vessels}* All sorts of instruments and utensils, as bowls, basins, dishes, and other things which were for sacred uses and holy services. See Exod. 27:3 & 37:16. 1 King. 7:45, etc.

*{that they should bring them in and out by tale}* Heb. *bring them in by tale, and carry them out by tale*. The priests had the charge of seeing all things set in order in the temple; but the Levites were to do the service of removing them from place to place, carrying them out, and bringing them in; and they were to give an account to the priests of what they carried out and of what they brought in: and therefore they carried the vessels out and brought them in by tale.

**V. 29.** *{Some of them also were appointed to oversee the vessels}* Some Levites had a charge over other Levites, and were appointed to oversee them in the disposal of such things as belonged to their charge. They who are mentioned v. 17 and others like them were overseers.

*{and all the instruments}* Or, *vessels*. All the movable things that were for use in the house of God are comprised under this last word translated *instruments*.

*{of the sanctuary}* Both the tabernacle, Exod. 25:8, and also the temple, 2 Chron. 20:8, were called a sanctuary: yea, and heaven too, Heb. 8:2 & 9:24: all holy places by reason of God’s special presence in them. Some properly expound the word, and translate it *instruments of holiness*, or *holy instruments*. See ch. 24:5 and 28:10.

*{and the fine flour}* This was for sundry meat offerings, Lev. 2:1, 4, 5, and for the showbread, Lev. 24:5.

*{and the wine}* For drink offerings, Lev. 23:13.

*{and the oil}* To mix with the fine flour in meat offerings, for sundry anointings, Exod. 29:27, and for the lamps, Exod. 35:24.

*{and the frankincense}* To be laid upon meat offerings, Lev. 2:1, 15, and upon the showbread, Lev. 24:7.

*{and the spices}* To make the incense, Exod. 30:34, etc.

**V. 30.** *{And some of the sons of the priests made the ointment of the spices}* Heb. *confectioners, or, makers of ointment*. Though the Levites were to provide, keep and prepare the spices, yet the priests were to compound and make the ointment. See Exod. 30:23, 24.

**V. 31.** *{And Mattithiah, one of the Levites}* Such Levites as were mentioned before.

*{who was the firstborn of Shallum the Korahite}* See v. 17, 19.

*{had the set office}* Or, *trust*. See v. 22.

*{over the things that were made in the pans}* Or, *flat plates, or slices*. These were for meat offerings. Lev. 2:5 & 6:21 & 7:9. Num. 11:8.

**V. 32.** *{And other of their brethren}* See v. 6.

*{of the sons of the Kohathites}* See ch. 6:33.

*{were over the shewbread}* Heb. *bread of ordering*. For the loaves that were called the showbread were set in order by rows before the Lord, Lev. 24:6.

*{to prepare it every sabbath}* Heb. *Sabbath, Sabbath*. See 1 King. 8:13. The Levites were to prepare them, and bring them to the priest; who set them in order every Sabbath, Lev. 24:8.

**V. 33.** *{And these are the singers}* Out of this order of Levites were all sorts of musicians chosen.

*{chief of the fathers of the Levites}* Principal heads, as v. 9.

*{who remaining in the chambers}* The chambers which were made on the sides of the temple, 1 King. 6:5. In these they lodged, v. 27.

*{were free}* They attending their singing according to the ordinance of David, ch. 25:1, etc., were freed from other services whereunto the forementioned Levites were put.

*{for they were employed in that work}* Heb. *upon them in that work*. i.e. It was upon them, or it belonged to them, to do the work before mentioned; namely all manner of music, instrumental and vocal, to be performed in the house of God.

*{day and night}* Early and late, morning and evening, when the ordinary sacrifices were offered; yea, and at other times, when extraordinary sacrifices were offered, or other solemn services performed. So Luke 2:37.

**V. 34.** *{These chief fathers of the Levites}* This has relation to those that were mentioned before.

*{were chief throughout their generations}* Not only they themselves in their own persons, and in their own days, but also the children which descended from them, were, generation after generation, prime and principal men, that were over other Levites.

*{these dwelt at Jerusalem}* Whereas other Levites dwelt in cities and villages in the country, these had their continual residence in Jerusalem. See v. 22.

**V. 35.** *{And in Gibeon}* This was a place in Benjamin. The historian having set down what he thought meet concerning the genealogies of the several tribes, returns to the tribe of Benjamin, and repeats a genealogy which he had set down ch. 8:29, etc., and that word for word, as a preparation to Saul’s history. For this genealogy leads to Saul and Jonathan his son.

*{Jehiel}* This name is added to show who was the father of Gibeon.

*{whose wife’s name}* Some copies read, *whose sister’s name*. Thus she was her husband’s wife and sister, as Sarah, Gen. 20:12.

**V. 36.** *{And Ner}* This name is omitted ch. 8:30. This Ner was not the father of Abner, 1 Sam. 14:50, 51, Saul’s uncle, and brother to Kish: but Ner the father of Kish, and Saul’s grandfather. See on 1 Sam. 9:1.

**V. 37.** *{And Zechariah}* Or, *Zacher*, ch. 8:31.

**V. 38.** *{Shimram}* Or, *Shimeah*, ch. 8:32.

**V. 41.** *{And Ahaz}* This name is here well added, for it is expressly set down, ch. 8:35.

**V. 42.** *{And Jarah}* Or, *Jehoadah*, ch. 8:36.

## CHAPTER 10

**Verse 1.** *{Now the Philistines}* The story of Saul’s death here set down is almost word for word so recorded 1 Sam. 31:1, etc. The sacred penman sets it here down, to make way to the history of David, his main scope. For all the chapters of this book, after this, set out the reign of David; and the other book sets out the history of his posterity succeeding him in the throne. Now the more to commend David’s reign, the tragical end of his predecessor is here briefly related. The Philistims here mentioned were the enemies that destroyed Saul and his sons. Of their original, see Gen. 10:14. They dwelt in the land of Canaan when Joshua came to conquer it; but were reserved to be scourges to the Israelites, Judg. 3:1, 3, and they always proved deadly enemies to them, Judg. 13:1, etc. It was a great blow that they gave Israel when they took Samson, Judg. 16:21, but that recorded in this chapter had been a greater, but that God raised up David to repair the damage and disgrace which was brought upon the Israelites by this their conquest over Saul.

*{fought against Israel}* The cause of the battle is not here set down: but if we consider the mortal enmity betwixt the two nations, we need not wonder that they should take all occasions to do what mischief they could. But the cause on God’s part is plainly expressed, v. 13, 14.

*{and the men of Israel}* Heb. *man of Israel*. Man is a collective word, and comprises all that are assembled together. Besides, here is a synecdoche, the singular number put for the plural. The Hebrew has an emphasis, and implies that every man fled.

*{fled from before the Philistines}* Heb. *from the face of the Philistims*. The Philistims did not only stand stout in the battle, but also fiercely pursued the Israelites.

*{and fell down slain}* Or, *wounded*. Heb. *thrust through*. By their wounds they were slain.

*{in mount Gilboa}* See 1 Sam. 28:4.

**V. 2.** *{And the Philistines followed hard after Saul, and after his sons}* They supposed that if these chief commanders in the war and chief governors of the state were taken away, there must needs be much confusion through the whole land of Israel. So they here used that policy which long after the king of Syria used, 1 King. 22:31.

*{and the Philistines slew Jonathan}* Jonathan was a pious man, and approved not the evil courses of his father, 1 Sam. 19:2 & 20:9, etc., and 23:16, 17, 18, but pious men are not exempted from outward judgments, as appears by what befell Eli, 1 Sam. 4:18, Josiah, 2 King. 23:29, and many others. For I. They are subject to many sins, for which God has a just quarrel against them; as he had against Eli, 1 Sam. 3:12, etc. II. They may unadvisedly cast themselves into danger; as Josiah did, 2 Chron. 35:22. III. God may take them thereby from the evil to come, Isa. 57:1. IV. God may prevent inconveniences thereby, which would fall out if they lived longer; as in this case of Jonathan. Though he himself might have been willing to have yielded the kingdom to David, 1 Sam. 23:17, yet they that set up Ishbosheth, 2 Sam. 2:8, 9, might much more have set up him. V. By Jonathan’s death God might aggravate the judgment on Saul: for he lived to see both his heir and also two other of his sons slain.

*{and Abinadab}* Or, *Ishui*, 1 Sam. 14:49.

*{and Malchi-shua, the sons of Saul}* These were not all the sons he had, but all that went forth with him to battle: for Ishbosheth, another son of his, was left to govern the kingdom in his father’s absence, and set up by Abner on the throne after his father’s death, 2 Sam. 2:8, 9.

**V. 3.** *{And the battle went sore against Saul}* For the enemy had a special eye on him, and followed hard after him.

*{and the archers}* Heb. *shooters with bows*. See ch. 5:18.

*{hit him}* Heb. *found him*. As when hounds fasten upon a deer in the midst of a herd, they are said to find him, so these archers fastening their arrows on Saul, being amongst many other soldiers, are here said to find him.

*{and he was wounded of the archers}* Heb. *he was grieved by the archers*. The piercing of the arrows could not but much vex him. In Hebrew they who are wounded are said to be *made sick*, 1 King. 22:34.

**V. 4.** *{Then said Saul to his armourbearer}* See 2 Sam. 31:4.

*{Draw thy sword, and thrust me through therewith}* He saw there was no ordinary means of escaping, but that he must die, and therefore he desires to be speedily dispatched by his own servant, and thinks not of gaining time to make peace with God.

*{lest these uncircumcised come}* He could think basely of those that were uncircumcised in the flesh, and yet not consider that he was worse, being uncircumcised in heart. See Ezek. 44:7, 9.

*{and abuse me}* Heb. *mock me*. He feared they might apprehend him alive, and make him a laughingstock, as they did Samson, Judg. 16:21, etc., and after put him to some shameful and painful death; yea, and when they had killed him, insult over him, and boast in his disgrace. In 1 Sam. 31:4 it is thus set down, *Lest these uncircumcised come, and thrust me through and abuse me*.

*{but his armourbearer would not}* Herein his armor-bearer had done well, if he had done it for conscience’ sake. For it is not lawful for one man to kill another to rid him out of present anguish. David caused such a one to be put to death, because he said he had slain Saul, 2 Sam. 1:10, 15.

*{for he was sore afraid}* Some apply this to a reverent respect that this armor-bearer bare his master. It may also be applied to an inward horror that might seize upon him by reason of the horribleness of the fact. To take away a king's life cannot but cause great terror of conscience, in a subject especially. See 2 Sam. 1:14; yea also he might fear some dreadful revenge to follow thereupon.

*{So Saul took a sword, and fell upon it}* Saul was in a desperate case, and had a desperate mind. He had forsaken the Lord, and here the Lord forsook him in his great extremity. Finding none that would speedily take away his life, he violently takes it away from himself. For he so fell upon his sword as it pierced him through and slew him: thus he ended his days in a capital and damnable sin. He committed murder, and the worst kind of murder that can be, self-murder, a sin against the light of nature, the letter of the law, the rule of charity, which is a man's love of himself, Matth. 22:39. It is the highest pitch of tempting God that can be, and a presumptuous usurpation of God's prerogative, *to whom belong the issues from death*, Psal. 68:20. It is also a most preposterous prevention of the place, time and means of repentance. No kind of departure out of this world gives greater evidence of the eternal perdition of the soul than this. They who in Scripture were left hereunto, are deemed reprobates, as this Saul, Ahithophel, 2 Sam. 17:23, and Judas, Matth. 27:5. Samson is not to be reckoned in the number of self-murderers, because he did what he did by divine instinct; and his aim was not simply to murder himself, but to take the opportunity of destroying the enemies of God and of his church, and that by virtue of his place and calling, which was to be a judge: and he did it with true devotion, and invocation of God's name, without any inordinate passion, which puts on self-murderers to destroy themselves: and as in other things, so in this especially, that by his death he destroyed the church's enemies, he was a type of Christ.

**V. 5.** *{And when his armourbearer saw that Saul was dead}* This shows that Saul made a speedy dispatch of himself.

*{he fell likewise on the sword, and died}* Herein he might show some respect to his lord, but he showed no respect to God, nor to his own soul.

**V. 6.** *{So Saul died, and his three sons, and all his house died together}* Under Saul's house all that in especial manner belonged unto him are comprised, especially those that were of his court, his special servants, his lifeguard, and his children accompanying him to the war. For such as tarried at home cannot be here meant. Besides this general particle all must be taken synecdochically for the greatest part. For Abner the captain of his host and divers others escaped. His armor-bearer in particular is added to these of his house, 1 Sam. 31:6.

**V. 7.** *{And when all the men of Israel that were in the valley}* The battle was fought in mount Gilboa, v. 1. But they that were below in the valley, which was at the bottom of the mountains of Gilboa, soon espied what was done. These that are here said to see this were on both sides of Jordan, 1 Sam. 31:7.

*{saw that they fled}* Namely, that the armies of Israel were put to rout, and all the soldiers fled.

*{and that Saul and his sons were dead}* There were in the army that quickly brought this heavy tidings, in these particulars, to them that were below in the valley; as the like heavy message was quickly spread abroad 1 Sam. 4:12, etc.

*{then they forsook their cities, and fled}* Such a fear seized upon them, as made them leave their habitations, and what else they could not readily carry away.

*{and the Philistines came and dwelt in them}* They first surprised them, then put garrisons therein, and afterwards brought inhabitants to possess them, that so they might the better retain them, and not lose them again.

**V. 8.** *{And it came to pass on the morrow}* On the day of battle they pursued the victory, till the night kept them from pursuing any farther; thereupon they had not leisure to view the dead corps till the next day.

*{when the Philistines came to strip the slain}* This has always been the practice of conquerors, especially of the common soldiers, to take their first opportunity to strip those that are slain, for their apparel, money, and what else they can find about them.

*{that they found Saul and his sons fallen in mount Gilboa}* By this it appeared that they who carried the news of Saul's and his sons' death to the inhabitants in the valley, were some of Saul's army, seeing the enemy knew it not till the next day. Hereby also it appears that the army of the Israelites was totally routed, and every one fled, in that there were none to carry off the dead bodies of Saul and his sons.

**V. 9.** *{And when they had stripped him}* Questionless they stripped others also; but Saul only is mentioned, because of that ignominious handling of him which after follows.

*{they took his head}* The Hebrew word properly signifies *the brain-pan*. But that may synecdochically be put for the whole *head*, and so well translated head.

*{and his armour}* For it was royal armor, and such as by the show of it declared it to be King Saul's.

*{and sent into the land of the Philistines round about}* They might send his very head and armor in triumph everywhere up and down: or they might send trumpeters openly to publish what was done throughout the whole land. This latter seems the more probable by reason of that which next follows.

*{to carry tidings unto their idols}* The word translated idols signifies such things as affright and terrify men. And some of their idols were made in such ugly and horrible shapes as many were affrighted with the very sight of them. That superstitious conceit which the heathen had of their idols wrought in men's minds a dreadful awe of them. Here they publish such matters of rejoicing to their dumb idols as if they had been living gods.

*{and to the people}* Such as had not been in the battle, nor near the plain, but were far off dispersed; so as they would have all of all sorts take notice thereof.

**V. 10.** *{And they put his armour in the house of their gods}* Idolaters and superstitious persons have always set up their trophies and monuments of victory in their temples; whereby they have showed little respect to the service of their gods. For such monuments are a means of drawing men’s eyes and minds from that service whereunto they should be attentive. The general word here translated *their Gods* is particularly named *Ashtaroth*, 1 Sam. 31:10. See 1 King. 11:5.

*{and fastened his head in the temple of Dagon}* Dagon was an idol proper to the Philistims. The root from whence the word is drawn, namely *דג* *dag*, signifies *a fish*. Others derive the word Dagon from *דגן* *Dagan*, which signifies *all manner of fruit*, and so make their idol to be a god, or goddess of fruits, as Ceres.

**V. 11.** *{And when all Jabesh-Gilead}* This was that city which Saul rescued, 1 Sam. 11:1, etc. And the place is here put for the inhabitants therein, 1 Sam. 31:11.

*{heard all that the Philistines had done to Saul}* Ill news quickly flies abroad far and near, and that in the particular circumstances thereof.

**V. 12.** *{They arose}* This phrase implies a readiness and speed in doing what they did. Certainly gratitude for the kindness which Saul showed them, 1 Sam. 11:11, stirred them up to do what they did. It is added 1 Sam. 31:12, that *they went all night*, that they might not be descried. So did Saul for them, 1 Sam. 11:11.

*{all the valiant men}* For they attempted a dangerous exploit, which required valiant men to perform it.

*{and took away the body of Saul, and the bodies of his sons}* For all these were fastened to the wall of Beth-shan. The enemy thought it not enough to put Saul's body to open shame; they did also the like to his sons' bodies. But these Gileadites had a care to free them all from that open shame. And this was another reason why they rose and went all night, that they might more speedily prevent the intended disgrace.

*{and brought them to Jabesh}* This was their own city, and the safest place whereunto they could bring them.

*{and buried their bones}* It is said, before this, 1 Sam. 31:12, that they burnt their bodies. It was usual among the heathen to burn the bodies of their deceased friends, and to keep the ashes thereof in a pitcher; for though they had some glimpse of the immortality of the soul, yet they believed not the resurrection of the body. But God's church in all ages, which has believed the resurrection, used to lay the bodies of their friends departed decently into a grave, as into a bed, out of which they should rise again. It was therefore extraordinary that the Gileadites burnt these bodies. The reason might be, because they were much hacked and slashed by the enemy, began to putrefy; or, that they might prevent the enemy from doing the like dishonor to those bodies again. Yet they buried their bones, because they would afford them, even in that kind, all the honor they could in those sad times. See 1 King. 2:10.

*{under the oak in Jabesh}* This was some famous tree, which they would make to be as a monument for those worthies there buried. So Jacob buried Deborah, Gen. 35:8. Besides by this oak it might in future ages be known where they were interred. See 2 Sam. 21:12, etc.

*{and fasted}* To testify their sorrow for that heavy judgment that had befallen the church of God in general, and Saul and his sons in particular.

*{seven days}* They did forbear all manner of sustenance all that while, but fasted each of these days from evening to evening, and at the evening had but small refreshing. See Dan. 10:2, 3.

**V. 13.** *{So Saul died}* This with all that follows to the end of the chapter about the causes of Saul's death, is added over and above that which is recorded 1 Sam. 31.

*{for his transgression}* As death first entered by sin, Rom. 5:12, so such kinds of death as God inflicts for special judgments are for special sins, as is here exemplified in this kind of Saul's death. Three kinds of sin are here expressly set down; 1. Disobedience against God's express charge, 2. Consulting with the devil, 3. Forsaking the Lord.

*{which he committed}* Heb. *transgressed*. For every sin is a transgression, 1 Joh. 3:4.

*{against the LORD}* Every transgression of any commandment either of the first or second table is against the Lord. For there is one law-giver, Jam. 2:11.

*{even against the word of the LORD which he kept not}* This has a particular relation to that word which God in special gave him, 1 Sam. 15:3. God's word was in express terms by a prophet made known to him, and that backed by weighty reasons; which made his transgression the greater.

*{and also for asking counsel of one that had a familiar spirit}* This was the second great sin. It has relation to 1 Sam. 28:7, etc. Of this sin see 2 King. 21:6.

*{to enquire of it}* He pretended to inquire of Samuel, but because he used the help of her whom he knew to work by the devil, (for therefore he had destroyed such, 1 Sam. 28:9.) he is here said to inquire of such a spirit as she and others accounted to be the devil.

**V. 14.** *{And enquired not of the LORD}* This was his third sin, that he forsook the Lord, manifested by not inquiring after him. But it may be said that he did inquire after the Lord, 1 Sam. 28:6. Indeed he made pretense of such a matter, but neither in truth nor in faith. Thus of Asa it is said, that *in his disease he sought not to the Lord*, 2 Chron. 16:12. And the apostle says, that such an one may not think that *he shall receive any thing of the Lord*, Jam. 1:7, and that men *ask, and receive not, because they ask amiss*, Jam. 4:3. Besides, he used not those means which God had warranted and sanctified for inquiring of him. For he had destroyed the Lord's priests, 1 Sam. 22:18, by whom he should have inquired; and he provoked the Lord to take away his Spirit from him, 1 Sam. 16:14. That therefore which is recorded, 1 Sam. 28:6, of his inquiring of the Lord, is meant of an enforced, sinful, faithless, hypocritical wish, that the Lord would some way or other foretell the issue of that great preparation which the Philistims made against him. This in truth was as no inquiring of the Lord. It was like Balaam's wish, Numb. 23:10, and Israel's howling, Hos. 7:14. Besides, when God answered not Saul upon that inquiry, Saul did not humble himself, search out his sins, confess them, turn from them, and pray for pardon of them; he used no means to regain God's favor, and obtain a gracious answer.

*{therefore he slew him}* This relative he has reference to the Lord. Now the Lord is said to slay him, in that he withdrew his protection from him, and gave him over to his own reprobate sense. See 1 King. 12:15, and on 1 Sam. 31:4.

*{and turned the Kingdom}* So much had God threatened against Saul, 1 Sam. 13:14 and 15:28.

*{unto David the son of Jesse}* Heb. *Isai*. See 1 King. 12:16. Mention is here meant of turning the kingdom unto David, as a transition to the following history of David and the continuance of his kingdom.

## CHAPTER 11

**Ver. 1.** *{Then}* Abner, after Saul's death, made Ishbosheth king over a great part of Israel, 2 Sam. 2:8, 9. By reason whereof there was long war between the house of Saul and the house of David: but at length David prevailed, and Abner and Ishbosheth were both slain, 2 Sam. 3:27, and 4:7. All that troublesome time this historian here passes over, and beginning with those days of David wherein he was established king, and in this chapter sets down how he was established, from v. 1 to v. 10, and by whom, from v. 10 to the end of the chapter. The nine first verses are recorded 2 Sam. 5:1–10.

*{all Israel}* Not every particular person, but such a multitude, as one looking upon them might suppose that every one was come thither: and indeed all met representatively in the heads of their tribes and families. See 1 King. 8:1, 2. The express number of every tribe here assembled see ch. 12:23, etc.

*{gathered themselves to David}* Voluntarily assembled, with one mind and heart, to settle him king over all the tribes. See ch. 12:23.

*{unto Hebron}* See v. 3.

*{saying, Behold, we are thy bone and thy flesh}* Three reasons are rendered by the Israelites to move them to establish David upon the throne. The first was the consanguinity betwixt them and David, in this metaphor of bone and flesh. This has reference to old Israel, who was their and his common father. This reason is rendered by Adam as the ground of the near union betwixt him and Eve, Gen. 2:23. It is also alleged by Laban, to assure Jacob that he should be dealt with courteously by him, Gen. 29:14, 15, and by Judah, to restrain his brethren from wronging Joseph, Gen. 37:27. Abimelech renders it as a reason to draw the men of Shechem to be on his side, Judg. 9:2, and David, to draw Israel and Amasa to come in to him, 2 Sam. 19:12, 13. Yea, it is pressed as a ground of Christ's love to his church, Eph. 5:30. In this place these Israelites might have an eye to the direction which God gave his people about choosing a king, that he should be from among their brethren, Deut. 17:15.

**V. 2.** *{And moreover in time past}* Heb. *both yesterday and the third day*. In that Hebrew phrase there is a synecdoche, a set and definite time put for an indefinite. In Hebrew yesterday is oft put for *time past*. See 2 King. 13:5.

*{even when Saul was king}* This is premised as an amplification of that which follows. For if when another was king, and he such an one as envied David, yet David did so and so for the good of this people, how much more will David do good for them, being himself king over them? This has relation to 1 Sam. 18:14, 15, 16, 27, 30.

*{thou wast he that leddest out and broughtest in Israel}* This is a phrase that sets forth the office and practice of a chief commander in war, which is both to go before his soldiers into the field, and to direct them what to do there, and also to show when they are to return home again. This is the second reason whereby they were moved to set the crown on David's head, namely that experience they had of his valor, skillfulness and faithfulness in defending the kingdom.

*{and the LORD thy God}* See 1 King. 1:17. God had given many evidences unto David of his favor and fatherly respect unto him; and David had given many evidences of his strong confidence in God, and faithful obedience to him: and therefore they use this phrase, *thy God*: in which there is a kind of propriety, but no exclusive: for they did also acknowledge the Lord to be their God.

*{said unto thee}* Though we do not read where God said these very words, yet we may from hence infer, that when God said that he had provided a king among the sons of Jesse, and when David was brought he bid Samuel anoint him, that he also said that David should be ruler over his people, 1 Sam. 16:12.

*{Thou shalt feed}* Or, *rule*. A metaphor taken from a *shepherd*, who provides good pasture for his sheep, brings them into their fold, lets them out of the same, defends them against the wolf, cures their sores, and every way takes care of them. Thus ought kings to do for their people; and therefore they are oft called *pastors*, Jer. 12:10, and *shepherds*, 1 King. 22:17, Esa. 44:28. The same Hebrew word is translated *pastor* and shepherd. And where it is said of Christ that *he shall be ruler in Israel*, Mic. 5:2, by the evangelist it is thus expressed, *he shall feed Israel*, Matth. 2:6.

*{my people Israel}* See 1 King. 6:13.

*{and thou shalt be ruler}* To feed and to rule do here intend one and the same thing. To be a ruler sets out a kingly function; to feed sets out a kingly practice or work.

*{over my people Israel}* God approves that relation, to make Israel rest more confidently on him, and to show that the honor done to David in setting him over God's people, and the care taken for Israel in setting David over them, were both very remarkable. This was the third reason whereby they were moved to settle David on the throne, God's own deputing him thereto.

**V. 3.** *{Therefore came all the elders of Israel}* See 1 King. 8:1. By the phrase *all the elders of Israel* we may know what was meant by *all Israel*, v. 1.

*{to the king to Hebron}* Of Hebron see 1 King. 2:11. To Hebron did David go so soon as he heard of Saul's death, and that by God's appointment, 2 Sam. 2:12, and there he continued his first seven years, 2 Sam. 5:5. Thither therefore came they all unto him.

*{and David made a covenant with them in Hebron}* This was a mutual covenant betwixt him and the people, for him to perform the duties that belonged to a prudent and loving sovereign, and for them to perform the duties of loyal and faithful subjects. And without question they all jointly together entered into a covenant with God, to avouch him for their God, and to walk in his ways, as Deut. ch. 26, v. 17.

*{before the LORD}* This implies a solemn ratification of their covenant, as in the presence of God, by solemn and sacred oath, and that before the ark of God, offering up sacrifices upon the altar of God.

*{and they anointed David king over Israel}* Of anointing a king, see 1 King. 1:34. This is the third time that David was anointed king. First by Samuel, 1 Sam. 16:13. Secondly, by the men of Judah, 2 Sam. 2:4. Thirdly, by all Israel, in this place. Solomon was anointed twice, 1 King. 1:39 and 1 Chr. 29:22.

*{according to the word of the LORD}* See 1 King. 12:24 and 14:10, 15.

*{by Samuel}* Heb. *by the hand of Samuel*. See 1 King. 8:53. This has reference to 1 Sam. 16:13, 18.

**V. 4.** *{And David, and all Israel}* See v. 1. A consequence inferred upon the establishing of David on his throne, follows; which was the destroying of such enemies, as had long remained among the Israelites, and building a royal palace, v. 4, 5, etc. David takes the opportunity of the multitude of the Israelites; and among them, many valiant soldiers assembled together, to vanquish the foresaid enemies, which none of his predecessors, for many generations before him, could do.

*{went to Jerusalem}* He had continued seven years at Hebron, 1 King. 2:11, but now being established king over all Israel, he goes to the chiefest city of all, which was Jerusalem. Jerusalem was situate, part in Benjamin, (Josh. 18:28; Judg. 1:21.) and part in Judah, Josh. 15:63; Psal. 78:68, 69. In this respect, Judah and Jerusalem are oft joined together, 2 Chron. 34:3, 5. It is that which of old was called Salem, Psal. 76:2. Hereof Melchizedek was king, Gen. 14:18. Heb. 7:1. Some say, that the word, *Jereh*, was prefixed, in memorial of God's providence in preserving Isaac, Gen. 22:12, 14. Abraham called the name of that place *Jehovah jireh*, that is, *the Lord will see*, or *provide*. *Jireh* sets out God's providence. Jereh prefixed before Salem makes Jerusalem, which signifies *to provide peace*: and so did the Lord for this city, especially in David's time. See more of Jerusalem 1 King. 3:15.

*{which is Jebus}* Called Jebusi, Josh. 18:16, 28. It was an ancient city inhabited by the Jebusites, who were of those cursed nations which God determined to root out, Gen. 15:21.

*{where the Jebusites were the inhabitants of the land}* Many of the Jebusites were rooted out together with other cursed nations: but such as dwelt in Jebus the children of Judah could not cast out, Josh. 15:63, so as they remained among the Israelites, Judg. 19:11, 12, and that till David's time, 2 Sam. 5:6.

**V. 5.** *{And the inhabitants of Jebus said to David}* By this it appears that David and his men had begun to set upon this city, but the Jebusites trusting in their high and strong walls and forts, feared not the loss of it: thereupon with much scorn they deride his attempt, and say as follows.

*{Thou shalt not come hither}* Make what preparations you will, and do what you can, you shall never be able to win this city. It is further added, 2 Sam. 5:6, *Except thou take away the blind and the lame*: which phrase much aggravates their scoffing scorn. The blind and the lame may here properly be taken for blind and lame men. Thus it implies, that they were so confident in the strength of that place as they placed upon the walls and in the towers blind and lame men, as if there needed no other to defend their forts against all David's forces. And thereupon this clause is added, *Thinking David cannot come in hither*, 2 Sam. 5:6. In this sense it is a most egregious scoff. Others apply it to their gods, whom the Israelites accounted blind and lame, in that they could neither see nor go, Psal. 115:5, 7. Thus it implies confidence in their gods; *q. d.* You must take away our gods, in whom, though you count them blind and lame, we place assured confidence; you must take away our gods, or else you can never prevail against us. Now they thought it impossible to take away their gods, and by consequence impossible that their city should be taken.

*{Nevertheless David took}* Notwithstanding their vile scorn and bold confidence God gave such success to David's enterprise as he took the city and utterly destroyed them all. The more insolent and confident in vain means enemies are, the nearer they are to their ruin.

*{the castle}* Jebus, which is oft called a city, Judg. 19:11, 12; Josh. 18:21, 28, is here called a castle, because it was as strong and well fortified as a castle.

*{of Zion, which is the city of David}* See 1 King. 2:10 and 3:1.

**V. 6.** *{And David said}* Or, *had said*. For that which follows was spoken before Zion was taken.

*{Whosoever smiteth the Jebusites first}* He that shall first adventure to take the castle, and by his valor draw on others to follow him, so as the Jebusites therein may be destroyed. It seems there was some narrow passage by which water ran down as by a gutter, through which, though with much difficulty and danger, a man might enter into the city: or there might be a gutter on the top of the fort, to which if a man could get, he might quickly surprise the fort: thereupon David said, *Whosoever getteth up to the gutter*, 2 Sam. 5:8.

*{shall be chief}* Heb. *head*. I. e. of the army.

*{and captain}* I. e. commander in chief of all the forces in Israel, whom we call generalissimo.

*{So Joab the son of Zeruiah}* See ch. 2:16.

*{went first up}* This and other stout acts of Joab show he was a man of great valor. Here with much hazard of his life he put forth his courage, both to gain that honorable place which David promised, and also to recover the reputation he had lost by his treacherous and cowardly murder of Abner, 2 Sam. 3:27.

*{and was chief}* Generalissimo. Joab was before this a chief commander in David's army, 2 Sam. 2:13, but now he was made chiefest of all. He held this place of captain general all the time of David's reign, which was full forty years. After David's death Benaiah was put in his room, 1 King. 2:35. It may be also that he commanded David's army before David was king.

**V. 7.** *{And David dwelt in the castle}* David made his royal palace there, and so dwelt therein.

*{therefore they called it}* I. e. Zion, 2 Sam. 5:7.

*{The city of David}* See v. 5.

**V. 8.** *{And he built the city round about}* Heb. *from the circuit, or compass*. I. e. everywhere. He did not only repair such places as were decayed, but also added new buildings, and made it a very fair city.

*{even from Millo round about}* See 1 King. 9:15. David himself at his own cost built all the city within.

*{and Joab repaired}* Heb. *revived*. Houses and walls, when decayed and ruined, seem to be as dead things, of no use; but when made up, repaired and beautified, they seem to have life put into them. See 2 Chr. 24:13.

*{the rest of the city}* That was without the walls. This Joab did to regain David's favor which he had lost by murdering Abner.

**V. 9.** *{So David waxed greater and greater}* Heb. *went in going and increasing*. After he was settled in the throne, he increased in wealth, honor, number of valiant men, conquests over his enemies, and in all things that he undertook at home and abroad.

*{for the LORD of hosts}* See 1 King. 18:15.

*{was with him}* Blessed and prospered him, and gave good success to the great things that he enterprised. See Rom. 8:31.

**V. 10.** *{These also}* This particle also has reference in general to the multitude of people and to the elders that met to establish David in his kingdom; and in particular, to Joab, and to his valiant exploit, v. 6.

*{are the chief of the mighty men whom David had}* Who were more excellent than other valiant men, and did greater exploits, and also had command over others.

*{who strengthened themselves with him}* Or, *held strongly with him*. As they held on his side, and remained faithful with him, so they put forth their valor, strength, and skill to the uttermost that they could, as any occasion was offered.

*{in his kingdom}* Or, *for his kingdom*. To maintain the peace and welfare thereof against all manner of enemies, yea, and to maintain his right thereunto.

*{and with all Israel}* These prime warriors joined with the elders and people of Israel, of whom mention was made, v. 1, 3, 4.

*{to make him king}* Namely, over all Israel. See v. 3.

*{according to the word of the LORD}* See v. 3; 1 Sam. 13:14 and 15:28 and 16:1, 13.

*{concerning Israel}* Or, *over Israel*. This may either have reference to the making of David king, and then it sets forth the extent of his kingdom over the twelve tribes: or to the words of the Lord, and then it implies the end that God aimed at in appointing him to be king, the peace and prosperity of Israel.

**V. 11.** *{And this is the number of the mighty men}* Not only the number in general, but also the particular names are expressly set down.

*{whom David had}* This catalogue of worthies has reference to the beginning of David's reign here; or but to his continuance in his kingdom, 2 Sam. 23:8, etc.

*{Jashobeam}* Some interpret this word appellatively. For in the Hebrew, 2 Sam. 23:8, it is *Josheb-basshebeth*, which may be expounded, *sitting in the chair*, or *he which sits in the chair after the manner of a judge or senator*, or *one that gives counsel or advice to others*. Thus taken it sets out his office. Otherwise it is a proper name.

*{an Hachmonite}* Or, *son of Hachmoni*. Hachmoni signifies *a wise man*; so also does Hachmonite. The word taken appellatively sets forth his inward quality or endowment, that he was *a wise man*; but taken for a proper name, it sets forth his parentage. Of this man it is thus said, 2 Sam. 23:8, *the same was Adino the Eznite*. Adino signifies *delicate or acceptable*. Eznite signifies *one that carries a thing of wood*, as a spear-man. Together they signify *one that delights in brandishing a spear*. Or, Adino was his proper name, Eznite, the family whence he came. Jashobeam or *Josheb-basshebeth* (which are taken for one name, though somewhat different in letters and syllables) and Hachmonite or the Tachmonite (for these two set forth the same thing) are taken for one man; and Adino the Eznite for another. But many take these for one and the same, because it is thus said, *the same man was Adino the Eznite*, 2 Sam. 23:8. If they be taken for one man, then he had two names, Jashobeam and Adino: and the first epithet Hachmonite sets out his parentage; the other Eznite, his family. Sundry of the ancient Hebrews take all these as a description of David, whom they make the head and chief of all the worthies: as 1. That he sat on a seat or throne. 2. That he was very wise. 3. That he was lovely or beloved. 4. That he was skillful in using a spear, or any other warlike weapon.

*{he lift up his spear}* I. e. he stood in fight, or fought.

*{against three hundred}* Eight hundred are mentioned 2 Sam. 23:8. If the former description be of two men, there is no discrepance betwixt these two places: for one stood against three hundred, and another against eight hundred. Or if this be taken of two battles, then the same man at one time stood against three hundred, and at another time against eight hundred. See the next note.

*{slain by him at one time}* He so lift up his spear as he slew them. Now he might slay three hundred upon the ground, and the rest flying away, he might pursue them, and slay five hundred more; thus the number of all he slew be eight hundred. And this is said to be at one time, because he gave not over the pursuit till all were slain. Or if he slew three hundred, and put the rest to rout, and his soldiers followed and slew eight hundred, he might be said to slay them, because he was the occasion of their slaughter.

**V. 12.** *{And after him was Eleazar the son of Dodo the Ahohite}* The second worthy is described by his name, Eleazar; by his parentage, the son of Dodo. See v. 26, and by his family, the Ahohite. We read of Ahoah a Benjamite, ch. 8:4. Of that family might this Eleazar be; and also Ilai, v. 29. And this Dodo may be he that is called Dodai an Ahohite, ch. 27:4.

*{who was one of the three mighties}* The first order or rank of worthies is here understood: for here are mentioned two distinct degrees, in every of which were three eminent persons, each degree excelling the other; and all of them excelling those that are named after them.

**V. 13.** *{He was with David at Pasdammim}* Or, *Ephesdammim*, 1 Sam. 17:1.

*{and there the Philistines were gathered together to battle}* See ch. 10:1.

*{where was a parcel of ground full of barley}* It seems it was then harvest-time, and the Israelites having a field of barley ready to be mowed, the Philistims sought to take it away from them.

*{and the people fled from before the Philistines}* These people haply being ready to mow the barley, upon sight of the Philistims ran away, as afterwards other Israelites did upon sight of the Moabites, 2 King. 13:20, 21, and as the Israelites before this did, when Saul was slain, ch. 10:7.

**V. 14.** *{And they set themselves in the midst of that parcel}* Or, *stood*. Namely, against the Philistims. They is either synecdochically to be taken, the plural for the singular; or some of Eleazar's soldiers might stand stoutly with him; or Shammah may be here meant; and they two might stand in the field when the others fled. But it is said 2 Sam. 23:11 that the place where Shammah stood was *a piece of ground full of lentils*. Answer: If Eleazar and Shammah got two distinct victories, one might well be in a field of barley, the other in a field of lentils. If it be but one victory, one part of the field might be sowed with barley, another with lentils; and Eleazar might stand in that part which was full of barley, and Shammah in the other.

*{and delivered it}* From being spoiled by the enemy.

*{and slew the Philistines}* He did not only put them to rout, but also to slaughter, so as he followed the victory with great courage.

*{and the LORD saved them}* Though Eleazar were an especial instrument of that victory, yet God was the principal author.

*{by a great deliverance}* Or, *salvation*. There were many enemies, the Israelites were fled, there remained but one, or two, or a few to stand against the enemies; yet that one, or two, or few, destroyed those enemies. If Jashobeam, v. 11, and Adino, 2 Sam. 23:8, were one and the same man, there are but two worthies of the first degree here mentioned, namely Jashobeam, and Eleazar. Therefore many suppose that Shammah, 2 Sam. 23:11, was the third.

**V. 15.** *{Now three of the thirty captains}* Or, *three captains over the thirty*. Every one of the thirty were captains and had bands of soldiers under them; but the six worthies were as colonels over sundry captains and their companies. The words seem to imply, that the three here mentioned were of those thirty set down v. 26, etc. but the order of setting down this history, immediately after the famous works of the first three worthies, singly by themselves, implies that the exploit following was a joint work of all those three together. This reading in the margin, *three captains over the thirty*, will well stand with this application. See the last note on v. 19.

*{went down to the rock to David}* I. e. to which David was wont to resort in time of danger. See 1 Sam. 23:25. In rocks men used to shelter themselves, Judg. 20:45, 47.

*{into the cave of Adullam}* See 1 Sam. 22:1. This he did in the harvest-time, 2 Sam. 23:13, when water was most scarce.

*{and the host of the Philistines encamped in the valley of Rephaim}* This they did after Saul's death, 2 Sam. 5:18, but so they might do also in Saul's days, and that when he persecuted David.

**V. 16.** *{And David was then in the hold}* This was questionless the cave of Adullam fortified by David, and in that respect called an hold.

*{and the Philistines garrison was then at Bethlehem}* Though their main army were in the valley of Rephaim, yet they might set a garrison in Bethlehem. For they oft made inroads into the land of Israel, and placed garrisons in sundry places, 1 Sam. 10:5 and 13:3, 23.

**V. 17.** *{And David longed, and said}* Being sore athirst by reason of the hot season of the year, he earnestly desired some water to quench his thirst.

*{Oh that one would give me}* Heb. *Who will give me*. So Jer. 9:1. This is an usual Hebraism, testifying an earnest desire; Deut. 5:29.

*{drink of the water}* He much wished he might have some water; but his mind was not that any of his captains or soldiers should adventure their lives for it. Men oft wish for many things that they would not have at such and such a rate.

*{of the well at Bethlehem, that is at the gate}* There might be at Bethlehem a continual spring, clear and sweet: he might therefore in his greatest thirst have his mind upon it, because he knew that spring failed not when there was a drought in other places, and no water to be had; and because he knew that water was pleasanter than other waters.

**V. 18.** *{And the three brake through the host of the Philistines}* That encamped in the valley of Rephaim, v. 15. That valley lay betwixt the cave of Adullam, where David was, and Bethlehem. Hereby it only appears that they first brake through the army, and then outfaced the garrison, or else drove them away. This much amplifies their courage.

*{and drew water out of the well of Bethlehem that was by the gate}* Having made way by their valor, they might easily do that for which they came.

*{and took it, and brought it to David}* They showed great respect to David, in that to satisfy his longing they would venture their lives.

*{but David would not drink of it}* See the reason v. 19.

*{but poured it out to the LORD}* He did not in anger pour it on the ground, merely to waste it in such a time of drought; but he poured it out as a drink-offering to the Lord, and so consecrated it to him in thankfulness, for protecting his captains that so far endangered themselves.

**V. 19.** *{And said, My God forbid it me}* A phrase of utter refusal. *Q. d.* Though I much long for it, yet God keep me from it.
