*{on Alamoth}* The word signifies *young maidens or virgins*, Cant. 1:3 and 6:8; Psal. 68:20. Some take it here for that instrument which we call the virginals, as Psal. 46:1. Others, for a shrill voice, or that which we call the treble, or counter-tenor; for which a maiden's voice is fittest, by reason of the shrillness or sharpness of it.

**V. 21.** *{And Mattithiah}* This man with the four following are the very same that are mentioned v. 18.

*{and Azaziah}* This man is added to those reckoned up v. 18, or he was the same with Ben.

*{with harps}* See ch. 13:8.

*{on the Sheminith}* Or, *on the eighth*. *Sheminith* signifies *the eighth*, Lev. 25:22. It is also taken for an instrument with eight strings, Psal. 6:1. Some take it for the deepest kind of voice in singing, which we call the bass. Thus it is distinct from *Alamoth*, v. 20, one being the highest, and the other the deepest.

*{to excel}* Heb. *לנצח Lenatseah*; which some make to be another musical instrument, but most expound it *to excel*; namely, to make the music the more excellent and delightsome, or to make the persons to be accounted the more excellent.

**V. 22.** *{And Chenaniah chief of the Levites}* See ver. 12. Chenaniah was none of them mentioned before. He is called *the master of the song*, or singing-master, v. 27, one that was not only able to sing himself, but also to hear and instruct others.

*{was for the song}* Heb. *lifting up*. The word may be applied to the lifting up of a voice, as Ruth 1:14, or to the lifting up of a thing, and in that respect translated *a burden*, Isa. 46:1; Deut. 1:12. Or to *a charge* committed to one, Numb. 4:27. In regard of this various use of the word some apply this to singing, whereby the voice is lift up; others, to the carriages of the holy things about the sanctuary. I take the former to be the more proper here.

*{he instructed about the song}* In this respect he is said to be *the master of the song*, v. 27, who taught others to sing. Or he set the tune, or taught how to begin, how to rise, how to fall, how to rest, every way how to set or keep tune; as a skillful singing-master. They who take the word for a burden or carriage, read, *he was for the carriage, he instructed about the carriage*, i. e. he directed others about the manner of carrying the ark, with what pace to go, when to stand, and when to change bearers. See 2 Sam. 6:13.

*{because he was skilful}* He had good skill in music: or, he was very skillful about ordering the carriage of the ark.

**V. 23.** *{And Berechiah, and Elkanah}* See ch. 9:16.

*{were doorkeepers for the ark}* These two went before the ark, keeping off the people from rushing against it; and when it was brought to its tent, they kept the entrance thereinto.

**V. 24.** *{And Shebaniah, etc.}* These seven were all priests.

*{did blow with trumpets}* This belonged to the priests at removing of the ark, Num. 8:10, at feasts and sacrifices, Psal. 81:3; 2 Chr. 29:26, at anointing kings, 2 Chr. 23:13, and in war, Num. 10:9; 2 Chr. 13:14.

*{before the ark of God}* As it removed from one place to another.

*{and Obed-Edom and Jehiah were doorkeepers for the ark}* These two following the ark did the like office behind it that the other two did before it, v. 23. Or at least they interchanged courses with those that went before.

**V. 25.** *{So David and the elders of Israel}* See 1 King. 8:1.

*{and the captains over thousands}* See ch. 13:1.

*{went to bring up}* For David had called them together now this second time to that end, 2 Sam. 6:12, etc.

*{the ark of the covenant of the LORD}* See 1 King. 3:15.

*{out of the house of Obed-Edom}* Where it had been three months together, 2 Sam. 6:11.

*{with joy}* This properly sets forth the inward cheerfulness of the spirit, but it also implies an outward expression of their inward joy, as 2 Chr. 30:27; Ezra 6:16.

**V. 26.** *{And it came to pass when God helped the Levites that bare, etc.}* By giving them ready, willing and cheerful spirits; or, by manifesting his favor to them, so that they went on without interruption; which he did not before, ch. 12:10. Or, by ordering it so that when some Levites had carried a while, they rested, and other succeeded in their room, 2 Sam. 6:13.

*{that they offered seven bullocks and seven rams}* Seven is accounted a number of perfection. It makes the week, Gen. 2:2, sundry feasts, Lev. 23:3, 6, 15, 34, the years of rest and jubilee, Lev. 25:4, 8, the time of cleansing, Lev. 12:3 and 14:8, 9. Consecrating, Exod. 29:35, 37. Preparing, Lev. 22:27. Keeping the Lord's watch, Lev. 8:35, the stint of sprinkling blood, Lev. 16:14, and oil, Lev. 8:11. Washing, 2 King. 5:10. Fasting, 1 Sam. 31:13. Feasting, 2 Chron. 7:9, and sacrifices. God commanded Job's friends to offer up seven bullocks and seven rams, Job 42:8. Balaam had learned as much, Num. 23:1. David here, to make a perfect atonement, offered seven bullocks and seven rams. So Hezekiah, 2 Chron. 29:21. It is further added, 2 Sam. 6:13, *That when they that bare the ark of the Lord had gone six paces, he sacrificed oxen and fatlings*. After they had taken the ark upon their shoulders, and gone a little way with it, and observed God's gracious acceptance of their endeavors, they made a stand, and offered sacrifices unto God in testimony of their thankfulness. They did this at the end of six paces, because haply within such a space Uzzah was slain. And whereas it is said that he offered sacrifice, it is meant that David ordered it to be done by the priest. And no doubt but they made an altar there, according to the law, Exod. 20:24. Oxen were a usual extraordinary sacrifice, Num. 7:3. The other word fatlings implies other kinds, as sheep, or lambs. It may be that under oxen and fatlings, 2 Sam. 6:13, are comprised the seven bullocks and seven rams here. Besides by fatlings are meant choice sacrifices, not lean, but fat ones, as Gen. 4:4; Psal. 66:15.

**V. 27.** *{And David was clothed with a robe of fine linen}* Or, *David was girded with a linen ephod*, 2 Sam. 6:14, i. e. his robe of fine linen was in fashion like to the priest's ephod. He is said to be clothed with it, because he put it uppermost upon him; and to be girded with it, because he tied it about him with a linen girdle. See Jer. 13:1. The ephod and girdle were not those sacred ones made for the priest, but others like them. These David put on now, to testify his piety, sincerity and humility.

*{and all the Levites that bare the ark}* See v. 15, 26.

*{and the singers}* See v. 16.

*{and Chenaniah the master of the song}* Or, *carriage*. See v. 22.

*{with the singers}* Here that which is spoken of David is to be repeated, namely, that all these were clothed with robes of fine linen.

*{David had also upon him an ephod of linen}* Either this is added as an exposition of the former; or otherwise the former robe of fine linen is to be taken (as some expound it) for a robe of white silk, which may well become a king; and that which the Levites wore has relation to this ephod of linen. For we may not think that the Levites were clothed with royal robes. It is also said, 2 Sam. 6:14, that *David danced before the Lord with all his might*. His dancing was grave and comely, answerable to the religious music, and expressed his zealous, cheerful and joyful spirit. It was in those days usual to set out their thankful heart by dancing, Psal. 149:3 and 150:4 and 30:11; Exod. 15:20; Judg. 21:21. This David did before the ark, which was a sign of God's presence, and in that respect it is said *he danced before the Lord*. And such was his zeal and ardency herein as he is said to have done it *with all his might*. So ch. 13:8. See 2 King. 23:25.

**V. 28.** *{Thus all Israel, etc.}* They who are mentioned v. 3, 25.

*{with shouting}* This implies a common acclamation and applause of all that were present, such an one as is mentioned 1 Kin. 1:40; 1 Sam. 4:5; Ezra 3:11, 13.

*{and with the sound of the cornet}* A cornet is a musical instrument made of horn, not like the hunter's horn, half-round, but wreathed, and with holes in the side; and it makes a loud, various and delectable sound, if at least it be skillfully handled.

*{and with trumpets, and with cymbals}* See ch. 13:8.

*{making a noise}* This cannot imply a louder sound than was made with the forementioned instruments, there being no musical instruments louder than they; but it implies an addition of other kind of sounds, to make the better melody.

*{with psalteries and harps}* See ch. 13:8.

**V. 29.** *{And it came to pass as the ark of the covenant of the LORD came to the city of David}* See 1 King. 2:10 and 3:15. In this city David had prepared an especial place for it, v. 3.

*{that Michal the daughter of Saul}* David's wife, 1 Sam. 18:27.

*{looking out of a window}* Thus queens, ladies, and others at great solemnities used to do, Judg. 5:28; 2 King. 9:30.

*{saw King David dancing, and playing}* David was very skillful in playing on an harp, 1 Sam. 16:18, 23. See on v. 27.

*{and she despised him in her heart}* She did not know what spirit moved David so to demean himself, but considering only the external act, she thought it an uncomely thing for a king in a linen upper garment to dance before his people, and thereupon like a carnal woman, she scorned him. This scorn and disdain arising from her own corrupt heart was afterward manifested by her opprobrious speech, 2 Sam. 6:20. In the four last verses of that chapter are expressly set down, both her scornful exprobration of David for that fact, and also David's sharp and just reproof of her; and God's just judgment on her for it.

## CHAPTER 16

**Ver. 1.** *{So they brought the ark of God}* Namely, the priests and Levites, together with the rest of Israel there assembled, David being the principal of all, ch. 15:25.

*{and set it in the midst of the tent}* The word translated tent is the same that is ordinarily translated *tabernacle*; and it is so translated 2 Sam. 6:17.

*{that David had pitched for it}* See ch. 15:1.

*{and they offered burnt sacrifices and peace offerings}* See 1 King. 3:15. These used to be offered on all occasions of solemn rejoicings, in way of thanksgiving, as ch. 29:21; 1 King. 8:64.

*{before God}* I. e. before the ark. Or in the presence of God himself. See 2 Sam. 6:14; 2 King. 19:14.

**V. 2.** *{And when David had made an end of offering the burnt offerings and the peace offerings}* After he had performed his duties of piety to God.

*{he blessed the people}* See 1 King. 8:55. It was usual with good kings to bless their people at solemn meetings. This David here did as a king and as a prophet.

*{in the name of the LORD}* By invocating God's name, and praying for his blessing upon them, and blessing God for that blessing he had bestowed on them. See these particulars exemplified, 1 King. 8:56, etc.

**V. 3.** *{And he dealt to every one of Israel}* This he did that the spirits of every one might be the more quickened to praise God with him, as Neh. 8:10, and that none might repent him of his journey by returning hungry and empty: so did Christ, Matth. 14:16.

*{both man and woman}* Both male and female were commanded at solemn meetings to attend holy services, Deut. 29:11 and 31:12. Answerably they did so, Josh. 8:35; Neh. 8:3. In this respect David here puts no difference betwixt the sexes. And in relation to God and Christ there is neither male nor female; all are one, Gal. 3:28.

*{to every one a loaf of bread}* There is another Hebrew word used 2 Sam. 6:19, which is translated *a cake*. The word here signifies that which is round, and in that respect may well be rendered *a loaf*. It is translated *a piece of bread*, Prov. 6:26. It implies so much as might at least well satisfy for a meal.

*{and a good piece of flesh}* This epithet good is here to be applied especially to the quantity: it was answerable to the bread. The word *אשפר* *eshpar* is a compound, the first syllable whereof signifies *fire*, the other *an ox*. Hence some infer that roast beef was dealt to every one.

*{and a flagon of wine}* The word does also signify *a little bottle*, and contained so much as might be drunk by one person in a day. So much of bread, flesh and wine given to every particular person manifested exceeding great bounty, and testified David's great joy for settling the ark in his city, and quickened up the spirits of his subjects to rejoice with him, and knit their hearts more close to him. It is added, 2 Sam. 6:19, *That all the people departed every one to his house*. This implies that they received full content, and having now finished this public service went to their private employments.

**V. 4.** *{And he}* David.

*{appointed certain of the Levites}* Those mentioned ch. 15:18.

*{to minister}* To sing, keep the doors, and do other services.

*{before the ark of the LORD}* See 1 King. 3:15.

*{and to record}* Or, *to declare and publish*, namely, the great acts of the Lord, in singing, or otherwise.

*{and to thank and praise}* Publicly, solemnly, and melodiously.

*{the LORD God of Israel}* See 1 King. 8:15.

**V. 5.** *{Asaph the chief}* This has relation to those Levites hereafter mentioned. Heman is set before Asaph ch. 15:16, 17 and 6:33, 39.

*{and next to him Zechariah, etc.}* Here are eight of those that were named ch. 15:18 deputed to attend upon the ark.

*{and Jeiel with psalteries and with harps}* Heb. *with instruments of psalteries and harps*. See ch. 13:8. Jeiel was culled out from among those before named to play upon these instruments.

*{but Asaph made a sound with cymbals}* See ch. 13:8. With cymbals Asaph answered the other instruments in course and tune.

**V. 6.** *{Benaiah also and Jehaziel the priests, with trumpets}* See ch. 13:8.

*{continually}* Every day in the hours appointed, constantly. See Exod. 28:38; Luk. 24:53 and ch. 23:31.

*{before the ark of the covenant of God}* See v. 4.

**V. 7.** *{Then on that day}* When the ark was settled in his place.

*{David delivered}* David indited the psalms, and committed them to skillful musicians, to put a tune upon them, and to sing them from time to time.

*{first this psalm}* This was the first psalm that David appointed to be publicly sung in the service of God. He did afterwards enlarge this, and also appointed other psalms. In regard of that divine spirit he had in composing psalms he is styled *the sweet singer of Israel*, 2 Sam. 23:1. David's name is not set before this psalm in the book of Psalms, but certainly many psalms were penned by him which have not his name prefixed. Consider Act. 4:25 and 13:33. It is there implied that David was the inditer of the book of Psalms.

*{to thank the LORD}* This is the sum of the whole psalm; which is spent partly in exhortations to praise God, and partly in setting down reasons to enforce those exhortations.

*{into the hand of Asaph}* To tune and sing it.

*{and his brethren}* Of his tribe and order. See ch. 9:6.

**V. 8.** *{Give thanks unto the LORD}* The former part of this psalm, from this verse to v. 23, is part of the hundred and fifth psalm. This being a general form of praise, was sung when any weighty occasion was afforded to God's people. The word translated *give thanks*, signifies *confess*. Which implies an acknowledging and declaring of God to be what he is, and to do what he does; which is the best manner of praising that can be. And the psalmist by his many exhortations in this psalm stirs up others to do what he saw meet to be done.

*{call upon his name}* They who are careful to praise God for blessings received may with much confidence call upon him for such things as they need.

*{make known his deeds among the people}* This may be taken of manifesting God's works throughout all Israel, or otherwise declaring the same to other nations.

**V. 9.** *{Sing unto him}* Praise must be given with cheerfulness.

*{sing Psalms unto him}* A grateful heart cannot be satisfied with a single expression of God's praise, but will return to it again and again.

*{talk ye of all his wondrous works}* God's great works must be made known to others, Psal. 40:10, being such as justly cause much admiration.

**V. 10.** *{Glory ye in his holy name}* Heb. *in the name of his holiness*. The word translated *glory* implies a setting forth of one's self; but it must be so as God may be glorified therein, and his name, which properly is holiness itself, may be glorified.

*{let the heart of them rejoice}* They who are here intended have matter not only of outward rejoicing, but also of inward joy and comfort in their soul; and all their rejoicing ought to arise from the heart.

*{that seek the LORD}* That renouncing all false gods and vain hopes, endeavor to know the Lord, and trust on him. See ch. 28:9.

**V. 11.** *{Seek the LORD, and his strength}* By God's strength is in a special manner meant the ark of the covenant, which was the evidence of the presence of the mighty God among them. Therefore they would sometimes carry the ark into the war, to heighten their courage, and daunt the enemy, Josh. 6:6, etc.; 1 Sam. 14:18 and 4:3. See 2 Chr. 6:21.

*{seek his face}* This has relation also to the ark, because there God manifested his presence. These two metaphors may farther imply, that in calling upon God we ought to set before us his almighty power and the evidences of his favor to us, that we may more confidently rest upon his power, and trust to his readiness and willingness to hear us.

*{continually}* God is to be sought not once or twice, but so oft as we have occasion, time after time; yea, our mind must ever be ready to be raised unto him all the days of our life.

**V. 12.** *{Remember}* Those things which are once known of God must oft be meditated on and called to mind.

*{his marvellous works}* See v. 9.

*{that he hath done}* In former ages long before our time.

*{his wonders}* Such as made men to wonder at them, being very extraordinary in their kind.

*{and the judgments of his mouth}* Such judgments as he first threatened, and afterwards executed. David has an eye to God's wonderful deliverance of his people out of Egypt, and to his judgments on the Egyptians.

**V. 13.** *{O ye seed of Israel}* It is said Psal. 105:6 *O ye seed of Abraham*. Both are true: for the Israelites were the seed of the one and of the other, and the promises were made and renewed to them both: and in that respect they are styled the seed of Abraham, or the seed of Israel, to strengthen their faith.

*{his servant}* This epithet is given to Abraham, Psal. 105, and to Israel, Psal. 135:22, for honor's sake, and as a testimony of their faithfulness to God. See 1 King. 8:56, 66.

*{ye children of Jacob his chosen ones}* God chose Jacob before Esau his elder brother, Gen. 25:23; Matth. 1:2; Rom. 9:11, 12, 13, and the children of Jacob to be a peculiar people unto himself, Deut. 7:6 and 10:15.

**V. 14.** *{He is the LORD our God}* See ch. 13:2.

*{his judgments are in all the earth}* He has brought all sorts of men under his power, and exercises his judgments everywhere. Or, the judgments which he executed on his enemies are made known throughout the whole world, Josh. 2:9, 10. Here are two reasons rendered to enforce the former duties, one taken from his good respect to his people, the other from his judgments on transgressors.

**V. 15.** *{Be ye mindful always of his covenant}* Or, *he hath remembered his covenant for ever*, Psal. 105:8. That which is here set down implies a duty on our part, and withal the condition of that covenant whereunto we are bound, Gen. 17:9; Deut. 29:9. That which is noted in the psalm lays down a sure ground of faith, God's mindfulness of that covenant of grace which he made with his people, Deut. 7:9; 1 King. 8:25.

*{the word which he commanded}* This here has relation to us, and shows the extent of our duty, that we should be careful to observe whatsoever he enjoins: but Psal. 105:8 it has reference to God, and his faithfulness in rewarding whatsoever is done upon his command.

*{to a thousand generations}* Here is a synecdoche, a set number for an infinite: and it implies that we in our generations should serve God, and do what lies in us that our posterity, yea, all that come after us, to the world's end, should also in their generation serve him. In Psal. 105:8 it implies that God's word, promise and covenant shall never fail, but according to the condition thereof be made good throughout all generations.

**V. 16.** *{Even of the covenant which he made with Abraham}* This has a special relation to Gen. 17:1, etc. and 15:18 and 22:16, etc.

*{and of his oath unto Isaac}* Gen. 26:3, 4. We read of no oath made to Isaac: but in that God renewed the same covenant unto him which by oath he confirmed unto Abraham, Gen. 22:16, etc. he may be said to swear to Isaac.

**V. 17.** *{And hath confirmed the same to Jacob}* Gen. 28:13, etc. and 35:11, 12.

*{for a law}* That as a statute it might remain inviolable.

*{and to Israel}* To Jacob and his posterity.

*{for an everlasting covenant}* This has reference not only to Israel after the flesh, so long as they remained the church of God, but also to Israel after the Spirit, even all they that believe in Christ, eternally, in this world and the world to come.

**V. 18.** *{Saying, Unto thee will I give the land of Canaan}* This in the letter was made good to Israel after the flesh, Josh. 21:43, 44, 45. In truth it is and shall be made good to all the heirs of the kingdom of heaven.

*{the lot}* Heb. *the cord*. First lots were cast for distributing the land of Canaan amongst the Israelites, Josh. 13:6 and 14:2 and 15:1; Psal. 78:55, afterwards some special cities were measured out by line or cord, Isa. 34:17; Zech. 2:1, 2; Mic. 2:5.

*{of your inheritance}* That which by lot was cast upon a tribe or family belonged as a proper inheritance to them and their heirs for ever.

**V. 19.** *{When ye were but few}* Heb. *men of number*. For they who are but few may soon be numbered. See Deut. 7:7.

*{even a few}* This being remarkable is again repeated.

*{and strangers in it}* This is spoken of their ancient fathers, Abraham, Isaac and Jacob, Gen. 17:8 and 28:4 and 37:1; Heb. 11:13. These two circumstances much amplify God's mercy in settling the Israelites in Canaan. For a numerous people to conquer a land is no great matter, and for natives to get inheritance in their own land is ordinary; but for a few to dispossess many nations, and those few, strangers, is wonderful.

**V. 20.** *{And when they went from nation to nation}* So did Abraham, Gen. 11:31 and 12:1, 6, 10 and 13:3 and 20:1, so did Isaac, Gen. 26:1, 23, so Jacob, Gen. 26:19 and 32:1 and 33:17 and 46:1, etc.

*{and from one kingdom to another people}* From Ur of the Chaldees to Haran, from Haran to Canaan, Gen. 11:31 and 12:5. From Canaan to Egypt, Gen. 12:11, thence again to Canaan, Gen. 13:1, then to the Philistims, Gen. 20:1, after that again to Canaan, Gen. 21:32, then to Egypt again, Gen. 46:6. This their peregrination is here set down to amplify God's providence over them in the verses following.

**V. 21.** *{He suffered no man to do them wrong}* Strangers are oft much wronged: but God took such care of the patriarchs, as they were permitted to live quietly, without violence offered them by the inhabitants of the places where they dwelt. See Gen. 12:16, 20 and 20:15 and 26:11 and 31:29 and 35:5.

*{yea, he reproved kings for their sakes}* As Pharaoh, Gen. 12:17, Abimelech, Gen. 20:2, and that Pharaoh who made the children of Israel bondslaves in Egypt, Exod. 7:16, etc.

**V. 22.** *{Touch not}* He means an injurious touching, smiting, or any other way wronging them.

*{mine anointed}* I. e. such as he had chosen and consecrated to him as a peculiar people, and on whom he had so bestowed his Spirit as by reason of the gifts thereof it was as oil poured upon them.

*{and do my prophets no harm}* It pleased God after an extraordinary way to make his will known unto the patriarchs; as by dreams, Gen. 28:12 and 31:10, 11. Visions, Gen. 15:17 and 28:12, the ministry of angels, Gen. 28:12 and 32:2, and other ways: and therefore they were called prophets, Gen. 20:7. And as prophets they did foretell things to come, Gen. 27:18, 29, 40 and 40:3, etc. God takes special care of such as he sets apart for his own service, and whom he endues with special gifts. In Psal. 105 there is much more added, which questionless was added by the psalmist upon a review of this psalm, after he intended to enter it into a public sacred record.

**V. 23.** *{Sing unto the LORD}* See v. 9. Though that which here follows were at first joined with that which goes before; yet when the psalmist upon review added more matter to it, he cut off that which follows, and made another psalm of it, namely the ninety-sixth. In this part David rises higher than before, even from the type to the truth, i. e. from himself to Christ, and from the church of Israel to the Christian church spread throughout all nations.

*{all the earth}* All nations throughout the whole world. In this large sense is this phrase taken, 1 Kin. 10:24; 2 King. 5:15; Psal. 8:1 and 19:4 and 66:4; Rom. 9:17. It has reference therefore to the Gentiles to be called. In Psal. 96:1 this is premised, *Sing unto the Lord a new song*. In Scripture-phrase things appertaining to the time of the gospel are called new; as, *a new covenant*, Heb. 8:13, *a new testament*, 1 Cor. 11:25, *a new Jerusalem*, Rev. 3:12. *New heavens, and a new earth*, Isa. 65:17, *a new name*, Isa. 62:2, *a new commandment*, Jo. 13:34, *a new way*, Heb. 10:28, *a new heart*, Ez. 36:26, yea, and *all things new*, 2 Cor. 5:17; Rev. 21:5. Thus the new song here mentioned is that which shall sound forth God's praises from the end of the earth, Isa. 42:10. The sum of this new song was sung out by an heavenly choir at the birth of Christ; and was this, *Glory to God in the highest, and on earth peace, good will towards men*, Luk. 2:14.

*{shew forth from day to day}* As occasion is offered, time after time.

*{his salvation}* In the type this may imply deliverance from temporal enemies; but in the truth, that redemption and salvation which Christ has purchased for us.

**V. 24.** *{Declare his glory}* That glory which Christ has got and brought to his church by redeeming them from all their spiritual enemies. This was typified by that glorious redemption whereby God brought his people out of Egypt.

*{among the heathen}* The Gentiles to be called.

*{his marvellous works}* Such as were intended v. 12, and also such as were typified by them.

*{among all nations}* For none are exempted from the benefit of the gospel.

**V. 25.** *{For great is the LORD}* Great in his essence, majesty, power, wisdom; and all other excellencies.

*{and greatly to be praised}* Man's esteem of God must be according to that excellency wherein and whereby he makes himself known.

*{he also is to be feared}* All duties to be performed to God must arise from an inward awe and fear of him, and in that respect they are comprised under fear.

*{above all gods}* By gods he means idols, such as vain men accounted gods, 1 Cor. 8:5, 5. The phrase does not intend that any fear at all should be yielded to idols; but because idolaters feared their gods, we are called upon to show a greater respect to the true God than they could to their false gods. Comparative phrases in Scripture oft imply a direct opposition and negation; as where it is said that the publican was justified rather than the Pharisee, Luk. 18:14. It is intended that though the Pharisee justified himself, yet in truth the publican was justified before God, and not the Pharisee.

**V. 26.** *{For all the gods of the people}* All whom the heathen, that knew not God, account gods.

*{are idols}* Heb. *אלילים*, *quasi* *אל אלים*, *not gods*, but *vanities*, mere fantasies, conceits of things that are not, Lev. 19:4.

*{but the LORD made the heavens}* By heavens synecdochically all God's works are comprised. This is an argument whereby the Lord does oft prove himself to be the only true God, Neh. 9:6; Joh. 9:8; Psal. 102:25; Prov. 3:19; Isa. 42:5; Jer. 10:11.

**V. 27.** *{Glory and honour are in his presence}* Or, *honor and majesty are before him*, Psal. 96:6. This is meant of those glorious works wherein and whereby God does manifest himself. For though he be in his essence invisible, yet he causes such a glory to be where he is as every one may say, There is God.

*{strength and gladness are in his place}* Or, *strength and beauty are in his sanctuary*, Psal. 96:6. As God himself is infinite in power and excellency, so all strength, beauty, and whatsoever may make the heart of man glad, comes from him, even from that holy place where he is.

**V. 28.** *{Give unto the LORD ye kindreds of the people}* By *kindreds of the people* are meant the very same who were before comprised under these phrases, *all the earth*, v. 23, *the heathen*, and *all nations*, v. 24, *the people*, v. 26. The word translated kindreds signifies also *families* or *congregations*. In the verses before he exhorted others to make known God unto them; here he exhorts them to take notice of God, and to yield unto him his due, v. 29.

*{give unto the LORD glory and strength}* So acknowledge God's power as therein you may glorify him.

**V. 29.** *{Give unto the LORD the glory due unto his name}* All that glory that is rightly given unto God is most due unto him: and they who do not yield all glory unto him, defraud him of his right. That which is given to his name is so given as he has made himself known.

*{bring an offering}* This has reference to that which God ordained under his law. That which God institutes and ordains must be tendered to him.

*{and come before him}* Or, *into his courts*, Psal. 96:8, namely, to the place appointed for his worship, and where he manifested his presence. People assembled in God's courts to worship him. See on 1 King. 6:36.

*{worship the LORD}* This is the end of approaching unto God's presence.

*{in the beauty of holiness}* This is a description of the temple, which in the excellent ornaments thereof and the truths prefigured thereby was very beautiful. It is called holiness, by reason of the holy use thereof, and the holy services appertaining thereto. Under these legal rites evangelical duties are prescribed.

**V. 30.** *{Fear before him}* See v. 25.

*{all the earth}* See v. 23.

*{the world also shall be stable}* By the world he means the Christian church gathered out of the world. This church, by reason of the reign and government of Christ, shall be firmly settled, notwithstanding the rage and opposition of enemies against it, Matth. 16:18.

*{that it be not moved}* This is the end of Christ's governing his church, to keep it safe and sure against all the attempts of her enemies. The reason hereof is thus rendered Psal. 96:10, He shall judge the people righteously. Nothing more settles or establishes an estate than righteous judgment.

**V. 31.** *{Let the heavens be glad, and let the earth rejoice}* These are excellent hyperbolical expressions of the great joy that should be in the Christian church. Senseless creatures, as the heavens and the earth, are excited to be sensible thereof. Or the heavens and the earth may metonymically be put for the inhabitants thereof, angels and men.

*{and let men say among the nations}* It was a matter of such joy as they might not conceal it, but declare it one to another, yea the church must declare it to others, to move them to come in.

*{The LORD reigneth}* This is meant of the Lord Christ, and of the spiritual government of his church.

**V. 32.** *{Let the sea roar}* Heb. *thunder*. When the waves of the sea with violent tempest are tumbled together, or the waters violently rush out together at some great breach, the noise arising from thence will be as the roarings of many wild beasts, or as the thunder. Now because this kind of noise is all the expression that the waters of the sea can give, they are exhorted thus to manifest their rejoicing.

*{and the fulness thereof}* Under this phrase those great creatures that most domineer in the sea, and fill it up; as whales and other sea-monsters are comprised. Thus is this phrase used Psal. 24:1.

*{let the fields rejoice}* Such fields as bear fruit for man or beast. This hyperbole is used as the former v. 31.

*{and all that is therein}* Hereby cattle, and other creatures that live on the fruits of the earth, are meant. Some suppose men to be here meant.

**V. 33.** *{Then shall the trees of the wood sing out}* He continues his hyperbole; only he alters the manner of phrase, and that which before he set down by way of incitation, here he sets down by way of promise. In his enumeration he has left out nothing; for all creatures may be comprised under the heavens, earth, sea, fields, woods, and all things in them. They all show that the kingdom of Christ shall bring matter of great rejoicing to all creatures, as sin makes all creatures groan under the burden thereof, Rom. 8:22.

*{at the presence of the LORD}* When the Lord Christ shall show himself to the world.

*{because he cometh to judge the earth}* By judging the earth he means Christ's governing the church. For further amplification and confirmation of this reason the phrase is doubled Psal. 96:13, *For he cometh, for he cometh to judge the earth*: and the manner of his judging is added, *He shall judge the world with righteousness, and the people with his truth*. Nothing causes more matter of joy than true and righteous judgment.

**V. 34.** *{O give thanks to the LORD}* This verse is set down Ps. 107:1 and 118:1 and 136:1. See v. 8.

*{for he is good}* Nothing gives juster cause of praising God than his goodness.

*{for his mercy}* The word translated mercy signifies that fatherly kindness and tender compassion that God bears to his children: for misery is the proper object of mercy.

*{endureth for ever}* This shows the unchangeableness and perpetual continuance of God's mercy; and withal that it is as a spring that will not be dried up with his church's weaknesses, nor wasted with the continual exercise thereof. The latter part of this verse is the staff or cadence of many verses of David's psalms, and of every verse of the 136 psalm. And it is a point to be seriously and frequently meditated on by us wretched sinners. See v. 41; 2 Chr. 5:13 and 7:3, 6 and 20:21; Ezra 3:11.

**V. 35.** *{And say ye, Save us}* This is a phrase of incitation, whereby God's people provoke to pray mutually one for another. Or, it is a direction to use this form, Hos. 14:2; Luk. 11:2.

*{O God of our salvation}* Upon their knowledge and faith that it was God that had delivered them, and would still continue to deliver them, they call upon him to save them. This may be meant both of temporal deliverances and also of eternal salvation by Christ.

*{and gather us together}* This applied to those that were not yet come in, implies a bringing of them in to Christ; but applied to those that were come in, it implies a continuing still to unite them more and more, that they might not be divided, as they had been formerly, 2 Sam. 3:1. Or, prophetically he may pray for the gathering of God's people from their dispersion or captivity, Psal. 106:47. Yea, it may also be extended to Christ's gathering his church from among the Gentiles.

*{and deliver us from the heathen}* Heb. *nations*. He means strange or other nations, or all foreign enemies. All the world but Israel were accounted heathen.

*{that we may give thanks to thy holy name}* The chiefest end that God's people aim at in seeking deliverances, is, that thereby they may have matter and opportunity to praise the Lord, and that as he makes himself known to them. God's name is that by which he is made known unto us. See v. 29; ch. 29:16.

*{and glory in thy praise}* That we may account ourselves happy, and rejoice that we have such an occasion to praise you.

**V. 36.** *{Blessed be the LORD God of Israel}* See 1 King. 8:15. As he begins so he ends with praise, and here promises what he exhorted others to do, v. 8.

*{for ever and ever}* This shows what is due unto God, namely everlasting praise; and withal the extent of David's desire, that God may be blessed, not only once, or a few times, but for ever; and that not only by him while he lives, but also by all ages after him.

*{And all the people}* All that were present, of what rank or degree soever, Deut. 27:15; Ezra 8:6.

*{said, Amen}* Testified their consent to all that had been said and sung, and withal their true desire of blessing God. See 1 King. 1:36.

*{and praised the LORD}* Hereby they gave evidence that Amen came from their heart.

**V. 37.** *{So he left there}* Here is showed what David did after the forementioned psalm was sung.

*{before the ark of the covenant of the LORD}* See v. 6 and 1 King. 3:15.

*{Asaph and his brethren}* See v. 7.

*{to minister before the ark continually}* Namely, in that tent wherein the ark was set, v. 1. See v. 6.

*{as every day’s work required}* Or, *to do every day what he did on one day*. See 1 King. 8:59.

**V. 38.** *{And Obed-Edom with their brethren}* See ch. 13:13. Some here add Hosah, mentioned in the end of this verse, thus, Obed-Edom and Hosah, because the relative their is of the plural number. Hosah was of the children of Merari, ch. 26:10.

*{threescore and eight}* There were so many, that they might in courses be helpful one to another.

*{Obed-Edom also the son of Jeduthun}* This is added as an explanation of what went before, *q. d.* *Obed-Edom also, I say*. This Obed-Edom is the same that is mentioned in the beginning of this verse.

*{and Hosah}* This shows that this man was before understood.

*{to be porters}* As the former clause of this verse showed their station, so this their function. Hereof see ch. 26:1.

**V. 39.** *{And Zadok the priest}* See 1 King. 1:8.

*{and his brethren the priests}* He means hereby other sons of Aaron; for all the posterity of Aaron were priests.

*{before the tabernacle of the LORD}* That which Moses built. See 1 King. 8:4.

*{in the high place}* See 1 King. 3:4.

*{that was at Gibeon}* To this place was the tabernacle brought after the destruction of the priests in Nob, 1 Sam. 22:18, 19. See 1 Chr. 21:29 and 2 Chr. 1:3.

**V. 40.** *{To offer burnt offerings unto the LORD}* This office belonged to the priests alone, Lev. 1:5, 7, 8, etc.

*{upon the altar of the burnt offering}* I. e. that brazen altar made by Moses, Exod. 38:1, etc. for that purpose, and therefore called the altar of burnt-offering, Exod. 40:6.

*{continually morning and evening}* Heb. *in the morning and in the evening*, Exod. 29:38, etc.

*{and to do according to all that is written in the law of the LORD}* To observe all the rites in the law of Moses enjoined to the priests about burnt-offerings and other services in the tabernacle.

*{which he commanded Israel}* He means all the children of Israel that descended from Jacob, from the time that those ordinances were given, to the coming of the Messiah.

**V. 41.** *{And with them Heman and Jeduthun}* See ch. 25:1.

*{and the rest that were chosen, who were expressed by name}* David had a care to choose out skillful persons to sing praise to God, and to register their names, that they might know their task and course.

*{to give thanks to the LORD}* See v. 4.

*{because his mercy endureth for ever}* See v. 34.

**V. 42.** *{And with them Heman and Jeduthun}* Verse 41 their general function was noted, here the particular manner of executing it.

*{with trumpets and cymbals}* See ch. 13:8.

*{for those that should make a sound}* These were loud instruments.

*{and with musical instruments}* Such as made a less sound, but were more melodious, as the harp, psaltery, etc. See ch. 13:8.

*{of God}* This is added either to declare the end of them, to set forth the praise of God; or else to set out their excellency, as ch. 12:22.

*{and the sons of Jeduthun were porters}* Heb. *for the gate*. Namely to attend the entrance into the tabernacle, and to see that no unclean thing should be brought thereinto, and nothing carried out but what ought. See ch. 26:1.

**V. 43.** *{And all the people departed every man to his house}* After David had settled the ark, and finished all his holy solemnities thereabouts, the people went home.

*{and David returned to bless his house}* To pray for his family, as he had done for his people, v. 2, and to settle piety and other good orders in his own house, as he had done in the Lord's. As in this latter he showed himself a pious and faithful king, so in the former a pious and faithful governor of his family. See 2 Sam. 6:20.

## CHAPTER 17

**Ver. 1.** *{Now it came to pass as David sat in his house}* That which was built in his own city, ch. 14:1; 2 Sam. 5:11. It is added, 2 Sam. 7:1, that *the Lord had given him rest from all his enemies*. Under these enemies Saul, Ishbosheth, and others that took part with the house of Saul, the Jebusites that kept the fort of Zion, and the Philistims that first made war against him, are comprised, 1 Sam. 31:6; 2 Sam. 4:7 and 5:9, 20, 25. Being delivered from his enemies by God's mercy, he would do what he could for God's honor.

*{that David said to Nathan the prophet}* See 1 King. 1:8. Nathan being an extraordinary prophet given to Israel after Samuel's death, David would not enterprise any great matter without his advice, supposing that he would give him counsel from the Lord.

*{Lo, I dwell in an house of cedars}* Of cedars see ch. 13:1; 1 King. 5:6. Now David taking notice of his own fair palace, was not puffed up therewith, as Nebuchad-nezzar, Dan. 4:27, but his pious mind was on God: and the beholding of his own glorious house made him think it was more meet that the Lord should have a fair house for his service, than himself for his royal use. The Israelites returned from captivity were not of this mind, Hag. 1:4.

*{but the ark of the covenant of the LORD}* See 1 King. 3:15 and 6:19. This being the greatest evidence of God's presence that ever he ordained to Israel, is here in special mentioned.

*{remaineth under curtains}* The word translated curtains signifies such things as are used to keep another thing from the injury of wind and weather. Some take it for skins that would hold out rain. He means the tent, ch. 16:1, which though it were of the best kind that could be, yet was not comparable to a royal palace, such an one as David dwelt in.

**V. 2.** *{Then Nathan said unto David, Do all that is in thine heart}* The word all has reference to that which David motioned v. 1. This answer Nathan gave, not by divine revelation, but upon his own imagination; supposing the action, being commendable in itself, might be well done by David. It was his failing that in a matter of so great consequence as the place of God's worship, he being a prophet would give an answer without warrant from God.

*{for God is with thee}* Because God had prospered David in other things, and directed him to order matters, as ch. 14:10, 14, he thought that now he was moved by God to build him an house.

**V. 3.** *{And it came to pass the same night}* God suffered not these his servants long to continue in a resolution against his mind.

*{that the word of God came unto Nathan, saying}* It is probable that God made known his mind to Nathan in a dream, because it was in the nighttime: but sure it is that it was by vision, v. 15. See 1 King. 3:5.

**V. 4.** *{Go and tell David}* God would have the same man that informed David amiss, to discover his error.

*{my servant}* See 1 King. 3:6.

*{Thus saith the LORD}* This is premised to show that there was a better warrant for this answer than for the former.

*{Thou shalt not build me an house to dwell in}* This is directly contradictory to what David intended and Nathan approved. Men may have good intentions which are not agreeable to the secret will of God. And extraordinary prophets are oft ignorant of God's purposes. See 1 Sam. 16:6; 2 King. 4:27. God's answer is set down interrogatively, 2 Sam. 7:5, *Shalt thou build me an house for me to dwell in?* which adds great emphasis. He says not, *Shall an house be built?* but, *Shalt thou build?* which does not declare the intent itself to be unlawful, but only the person that intended it not to be meet for the work. That the thing purposed was good and commendable is evident by God's appointing it, 1 King. 8:18. The reasons why David was not fit to do it, were, I. Because he had shed much blood, ch. 22:8. II. Because there were many enemies to be subdued, so as he could not have that leisure that was meet for so great a work, 1 King. 5:3. Besides these, other reasons follow.

**V. 5.** *{For I have not dwelt in an house since I brought up Israel}* To wit, out of Egypt. And here is another reason; David need not be over-forward in building a sumptuous house for the Lord, because for many generations before he had been content to be without such an house.

*{unto this day}* That very time wherein David made the motion.

*{but have gone}* Heb. *have been*. This is meant of the ark.

*{from tent to tent}* This may be taken either of the many places to which the tabernacle, wherein the ark was set, was removed. See 1 King. 8:4. Or of the tents made for it, one by Moses, Exod. 26:33, another by David, ch. 16:1. Yea, and it may be another tent, besides those two, was made at Kiriath-jearim, where it abode twenty years, 1 Sam. 7:1, 2.

*{and from one tabernacle to another}* This is added to show what kind of tent he meant before, namely such a tent as was called the tabernacle.

**V. 6.** *{Wheresoever I have walked}* Or, *in all places wherein I have walked*, 2 Sam. 7:7. By walking he means the moving of the ark from place to place.

*{with all Israel}* For the ark was not appropriated to any one tribe, but was for all the children of Israel to worship before it.

*{spake I a word to any of the judges of Israel}* Or, *of the tribes of Israel*, 2 Sam. 7:7. Because the judges were chosen out of several tribes, one out of one tribe, and another out of another, the word tribes is set down, but judges are especially intended under it.

*{whom I commanded to feed my people}* Because governors are as shepherds, therefore this metaphor is used. See ch. 11:2.

*{saying, Why have you not built me an house of cedars?}* See v. 1. This interrogative shows that God never required any of the governors of Israel, from Moses to David, to build a temple for him: and therefore David should not have attempted such a work without warrant from God himself. So this is a further reason to demonstrate that it was not meet for him to build a temple, because God had not required it of him.

**V. 7.** *{Now therefore thus shalt thou say unto my servant David}* See v. 4.

*{Thus saith the LORD of hosts}* See 1 King. 12:24 and 18:15.

*{I took thee from the sheepcote}* Here God sets down sundry great benefits he either had done, or would do for David. First, he raised him from a very mean condition, from tending of sheep. Now because shepherds use to provide and set sheep-coats for their sheep, this phrase is here used. The word may also be taken for a mean cottage, such an one as shepherds use to abide in, Jer. 23:3.

*{even from following the sheep}* Heb. *from after the sheep*. For shepherds use to go after and follow their sheep. See 1 Sam. 16:11; Psal. 78:70.

*{that thou shouldst be ruler over my people Israel}* He means a supreme ruler, even a king. See ch. 11:2. This was a great advancement, of a shepherd to be made a king.

**V. 8.** *{And I have been with thee whithersoever thou hast walked}* This is a second benefit; God's providence to him, and protection over him. God manifested his gracious presence with David, when he enabled him to rescue a sheep out of the jaw of a lion and a bear, 1 Sam. 17:34, etc. when he overcame Goliath, 1 Sam. 17:49, when he escaped the manifold persecutions of Saul, 1 Sam. 18:9, 11, 27, when he was preserved in the land of the Philistims, 1 Sam. 21:10, etc. and when he prevailed against the house of Saul, 2 Sam. 3:1, etc. In regard of these and many other preservations, this phrase is added, *whithersoever thou hast walked*.

*{and have cut off all thine enemies from before thee}* This is a third benefit, and has reference to God's destroying of Saul, 1 Sam. 31:6, Ishbosheth, 2 Sam. 4:7, and the Philistims, 2 Sam. 5:17, etc.

*{and have made thee a name}* I have so ordered your affairs, and enabled you to do so great acts, as your fame is everywhere spread abroad. This is a fourth benefit.

*{like the name of the great men that are in the earth}* He means such as had obtained great victories, had much wisdom, treasured up store of wealth, or for any other matters were famous. These four benefits are reckoned up, not to upbraid David, as if he had carried himself unworthy of them, but rather to show that God, though he were not minded to have him to be the builder of his temple, yet had ever borne a good respect unto him. And that it might appear that God did not repent him of those his former favors, he manifests his purpose of continuing his favor unto him, in the verses following.

**V. 9.** *{Also I will ordain a place for my people Israel}* This is a fifth benefit, and has respect to the time to come. It is not so to be taken as if the people of Israel had not had before this a place appointed for them: for Canaan was ordained for them in Abraham's time, Gen. 12:7, and the possession thereof given unto them in Joshua's, Josh. 15:1, etc. But it is to be taken comparatively, in relation to former times, wherein enemies oft entered their land, and much oppressed them, Judg. 2:14. Yea, till a little before this the Jebusites possessed even that place which was now the city of David, ch. 11:6, 7. But God would now so settle Israel in that land as they should wholly possess it.

*{and will plant them}* As a tree planted in the ground takes root, and remains stable therein.

*{and they shall dwell in their place}* As a man dwells in his own house. This is made the end of God's planting and settling them in the land, that they may dwell in a place of their own, 2 Sam. 7:10.

*{and shall be moved no more}* From that their land; namely as they had been formerly. When Saul was slain, many of the Israelites left their cities, 1 Sam. 31:7. This phrase no more must be taken with the ordinary limitations of promises about temporal blessings, obedience to God's commandments, and forbearing such sins as cause public judgments, 1 Sam. 2:30; Jer. 10:9, 10. While the people observed those conditions, in David's, Solomon's, Asa's, Jehoshaphat's, and other good kings' times, they were not moved. If the phrase be simply taken, it is to be applied to David's and Solomon's time; or else to the spiritual estate of God's church.

*{neither shall the children of wickedness}* The enemies of God and of his church are called children of wickedness, because they wholly give themselves to wickedness. It is an emphatical Hebraism; as, *sons of Belial*, 1 King. 21:10, 13, *children of disobedience*, Eph. 2:2 and 5:6; Col. 3:6.

*{waste them any more}* Destroy their persons, and spoil their goods.

*{as at the beginning}* While they were in Egypt, Exod. 1:13, etc.

**V. 10.** *{And since the time that I commanded judges to be over my people Israel}* In the time of the judges they were exceedingly wasted, Judg. 3:8, so also in the days of Saul, 1 Sam. 13:6, etc.

*{Moreover, I will subdue all thine enemies}* This is the second thing promised, and the sixth benefit here mentioned: and it was so fully accomplished in David's time, as Solomon his son found no enemy left to annoy him, 1 King. 5:4.

*{Furthermore, I tell thee}* This phrase of assurance is premised, because the blessing following was great and sure. This asseveration was uttered in the name of God, therefore it is thus set down, *The Lord telleth thee*, 2 Sam. 7:11.

*{That the LORD will build thee an house}* This is the third thing promised, and the seventh benefit. By house is meant a family, or a posterity. By building it, a raising it up, and establishing it. This house was built by continuing David's posterity till the coming of the Messiah, who was of that house, Matth. 1:1, and makes it to be an everlasting house. See 1 King. 2:24. The blessing here promised is set forth in this phrase, *The Lord will build thee an house*, to testify God's approbation of David's intention of building an house for God.

**V. 11.** *{And it shall come to pass when thy days be expired}* His life is set out by days, to show the brevity of life.

*{thou must go to be with thy fathers}* Or, *sleep with thy fathers*, 2 Sam. 7:12. See 1 King. 2:10. His ancestors being departed out of this world, he is said to go to them when he dies.

*{that I will raise up thy seed after thee}* By seed he means such children as should proceed out of his bowels, 2 Sam. 7:12.

*{which shall be of thy sons}* David had many sons, but one of them is here specially intended, and that is Solomon in the type, and Christ in the truth.

*{and I will establish his kingdom}* See 1 King. 9:5.

**V. 12.** *{He shall build me an house}* Or, *he shall build an house for my name*, 2 Sam. 7:14. See 1 King. 5:3. Solomon built a material temple, 1 King. 6:1, etc. and Christ, a spiritual house, Eph. 2:20, 21; Heb. 3:3, 6.

*{and I will stablish his throne for ever}* This was verified in Solomon for his lifetime; and for the continuance of the polity of the Jews, till the captivity, yea, and till the coming of the Messiah, Matth. 16:16, but in Christ it is accomplished everlastingly, Luke 1:32, 33.

**V. 13.** *{I will be his father}* This is true of Solomon not only in a general respect as he had his being from God, but also in regard of the grace of adoption, and of God's especial fatherly favor to him, ch. 28:6. But it is true of Christ most properly, by reason of his eternal generation, Heb. 1:5, and of that entire fatherly respect that God bare unto him, Matth. 3:17.

*{and he shall be my son}* This follows by the rule of relation, and it implies both a dignity and a duty. There can be no greater dignity than to be God's son: and he who is God's son, ought to bear a filial respect to God; yea if he be a true son, he will bear such a respect to him. It is added, 2 Sam. 7:14, *If he commit iniquity, I will chasten him with the rod of men, and with the stripes of the children of men*. This properly is meant of Solomon: for he committed sin, and answerably God punished him, 1 King. 11:9, etc. But Christ never having committed any sin, Heb. 4:15 and 7:26, if it be applied to him, it must be as he was our surety, Isa. 5:36; 2 Cor. 5:21. *The rod of men*, and *stripes of the children of men* signify mild punishments, such as may be borne by men. The metaphor is taken from men as they are parents, who with moderation and compassion use to correct their children; or else from men as they are finite and weak creatures, and so cannot with infinite and eternal torments punish others. In this latter respect the rod and stripes of men are opposed to the almighty, infinite, and everlasting judgments of God, which he inflicts on such as he hates. See Isa. 31:3 and 47:3.

*{and I will not take my mercy away from him}* Some understand this only of a temporary and outward mercy concerning the continuance of Solomon's kingdom. But such a mercy Saul enjoyed all his days; for he died king of Israel, 2 Sam. 1:14. But here being noted a difference betwixt God's mercy to Saul and to Solomon, questionless spiritual grace is meant. God gave his Spirit to Saul, and took it away again, 1 Sam. 10:9 and 16:14, but he never took away his Spirit from Solomon. Indeed he sinned a great sin, 1 King. 11:1, etc. but the book of Ecclesiastes is an evidence of his repentance.

*{as I took it from him that was before thee}* I. e. from Saul. It is, *whom I put away before thee*, 2 Sam. 7:15. God rejected Saul in David's time, 1 Sam. 13:14 and 15:28, and he rejected him, that David might succeed him in the kingdom: in these respects Saul was put away from before David.

**V. 14.** *{But I will settle him in mine house}* The church is God's house. Israel till Christ's time was God's church; and after Christ's time, the Christian church was God's house. In Israel was Solomon settled, and Christ in his church.

*{and in my kingdom}* That which was called God's house, because in an especial manner he dwelt and abode therein, is also called his kingdom, because he reigns therein.

*{for ever}* See v. 12. It is in 2 Sam. 7:16, *and thine house and thy kingdom shall be established for ever before thee*. See v. 11, 12.

*{and his throne shall be established for evermore}* See 1 King. 2:45.

**V. 15.** *{According to all these words}* Which God had delivered to his prophet Nathan.

*{and according to all this vision}* See v. 3.

*{so did Nathan speak unto David}* Though this message were contrary to Nathan's advice, v. 2, yet he plainly declared it as it was given him in charge. This was an evidence of his faithfulness.

**V. 16.** *{And David the king came}* Or, *then went King David in*, 2 Sam. 7:18. After he had received that gracious message, he went into the tent that he had prepared for the ark, ch. 16:1.

*{and sat before the LORD}* Before the ark, the evidence of God's presence. This word sat is oft taken, not simply for a posture of the body, but indefinitely, for continuing some while in a place, as Gen. 29:19; Psal. 102:12. If it be properly taken for the gesture of David's body, it may be that at first he sat meditating on the message, and then stood or kneeled when he prayed. So Solomon in dedicating the temple, first stood, and then kneeled, 1 King. 8:22, 54. Or if David sat all the while upon a seat, his prayer might be made with much devotion, as Moses his was, Exod. 17:12.

*{and said, Who am I}* David having received gracious promises from the Lord, falls into an high admiration of God's goodness to him, and into a hearty supplication for the accomplishment of the promises, v. 23, etc. he begins with an humble acknowledgment of his own unworthiness, the more to magnify God's bounty. This phrase, *Who am I*, is an emphatical expression of the meanness of his own person.

*{O LORD God}* These two titles set out the sovereignty and grace of God. David joins them both together, to work his heart to reverence and confidence.

*{and what is mine house}* This sets out the meanness of the family or stock whence he came.

*{that thou hast brought me hitherto}* His high exaltation to be a king he acknowledges to be of God.

**V. 17.** *{And yet this was a small thing in thine eyes, O God}* Though his advancement were very high in itself, and in his own and other men's account, yet by the promises which God made of greater matters it appeared that God accounted it but small preferment.

*{for thou hast also spoken of thy servant’s house}* David has reference to the promise v. 10, *The Lord will build thee an house*. And he magnifies God's goodness in that it was not restrained to his person, but extended to his posterity.

*{for a great while to come}* Even after David's days, so long as the kingdom of Israel, yea, and the church of God, continue. See v. 10.

*{and hast regarded me}* He accounted promises concerning his posterity as a kindness to himself.

*{according to the estate of a man of high degree}* This has reference to David's first estate, when he was a shepherd: though he were of so mean estate, yet God was pleased to deal with him as if he had descended from the highest and royalest stock that ever was. And this does much amplify God's mercy to him. His admiration hereof is thus set out, 2 Sam. 7:19, And is this the manner of man? Does any man thus deal with another? God's dealing with him so freely, so bountifully, so magnificently as he did, infinitely exceeded all the goodness or kindness that man shows to man. Some apply this to the Messiah, that should come from David; which was indeed the highest evidence of grace and favor that could be.

*{O LORD God}* See v. 16.

**V. 18.** *{What can David speak more to thee}* The word speak is not in the original, but supplied out of 2 Sam. 7:20, and it implies, that David could desire no more of God than what he had freely of himself offered and promised unto him.

*{for the honour of thy servant}* God has so for the present exalted David, and for the future promised such a seed, as greater honor could not be given him. He adds this phrase, *thy servant*, in testimony of his humble acknowledgment of God's sovereignty, and to show the ground of his confidence in God.

*{for thou knowest thy servant}* What his outward state and condition is, what his inward mind and disposition was, and what was needful, useful, profitable and honorable unto him. Some take the meaning of this whole verse to be this, that David need not much trouble himself about words and phrases to express his thankfulness, because the Lord knew the heartiness of his thankful mind.

**V. 19.** *{O LORD, for thy servant’s sake}* This title servant was in the former verse twice spoken of David in relation to God. Being here so taken it has reference to the promise that God made to David, *q. d.* for that promise sake which you have made to your servant. See 1 King. 11:12. In 2 Sam. 7:21 it is *For thy word’s sake*; word being there put for promise, as it uses to be, (see Psal. 119:49.) it expounds the meaning of this place: and shows that under this phrase, *for thy servant’s sake*, is meant the word or promise that God made to David. Some apply word unto Christ, styled the Word, Joh. 1:1, as they do also this title servant; as Isa. 42:1; Matth. 12:18. In this sense David here pleads the merit and mediation of the Son of God, who should in regard of his human nature descend from him.

*{and according to thine own heart}* I. e. your free grace, goodwill, or good pleasure: for God doth all things according to the counsel of his own will, Eph. 1:11. See Matth. 11:26.

*{hast thou done all this greatness}* Under this phrase are comprised that high estate whereunto God had advanced David, the many deliverances out of troubles, victories over enemies, and other good things that he had given him, together with those precious promises that he had made of greater favors hinted v. 17. These are called greatness in the abstract, because they were very great in themselves, and far beyond any desert of his.

*{in making known all these great things}* Heb. *greatnesses*. In 2 Sam. 7:21 it is, *To make thy servant know them*. It did much heighten David's esteem of those future good things, in that God had not only purposed them for him, but also revealed them unto him for the strengthening of his faith and hope.

**V. 20.** *{O LORD, there is none like thee}* In this and the two next verses is set down David's magnifying God for God's magnifying him: and it is an excellent form of thanks. This phrase is premised 2 Sam. 7:22, *Wherefore thou art great, O Lord God*. Of great see ch. 16:25. In saying none is like to God, he prefers the Lord before all, and that so highly as no comparison can be made. See Deut. 3:24; 1 Sam. 2:2.

*{neither is there any God besides thee}* Howsoever idolaters have their gods in high esteem, yet they are but mere vanities; the Lord is the only true God, Deut. 4:35.

*{according to all that we have heard with our ears}* This has reference to the miracles and other great works which God had done in former times, and were made known to succeeding ages.

**V. 21.** *{And what one nation in the earth is like thy people Israel?}* In 2 Sam. 7:23 it is *like thy people, even like Israel*. As v. 20 God's excellency in himself is declared, so here his goodness to his church and people. Israel is styled God's people, because he had in an especial manner chosen them to be his church. See 1 King. 3:8 and 8:51. And in this respect Israel was the glory of all the world; no people like them, Deut. 4:6, 7, 8, 34.

*{whom God went to redeem}* Out of Egypt. While they were in bondage, God seemed to be absent from them; but when the time of their deliverance came, it is said that he went to them. Deliverance of bondslaves is properly called redemption, Exod. 6:6 and 15:13; Deut. 7:8. This word went may have reference to God's coming down, Exod. 3:8.

*{to be his own people}* The end of God's delivering them was to make them a peculiar people to himself, Exod. 19:4, 5, 6; Deut. 10:14, 15.

*{to make thee a name of greatness and terribleness}* The manner of God's redeeming his people by judgments brought on Egypt made other nations fear and tremble, Josh. 2:11.

*{by driving out nations from before thy people}* Those which Moses and Joshua conquered, Josh. 24:5, etc.

*{whom thou hast redeemed out of Egypt}* See 1 King. 8:9. This clause is added 2 Sam. 7:23, *From the nations and their gods*. He makes mention of their gods, because the heathen so trusted in their idol-gods, as they thought the God of Israel could not deliver them, 1 Sam. 17:43. Therefore their gods were destroyed, Exod. 12:12; Num. 33:4. See ch. 14:12.

**V. 22.** *{For thy people Israel didst thou make thine own people}* V. 21.

*{for ever}* This applied to Israel after the flesh is true till that church was ended in Christ; but applied to Israel in the spirit, it is to be extended to the end of the world. See 1 King. 10:9. In 2 Sam. 7:24 it is *Thou hast confirmed to thyself thy people Israel to be a people unto thee for ever*. This God confirmed by an everlasting covenant, Gen. 17:7, and by oath, Luk. 1:73. See ch. 16:16.

*{and thou, LORD, becamest their God}* Though he be sovereign over all, yet he is in an especial manner the God of his church, Deut. 10:14, 15.

**V. 23.** *{Therefore now, LORD}* Here David begins his supplication. See v. 16.

*{let the thing that thou hast spoken concerning thy servant and concerning his house}* This has reference to v. 9, 10, etc.

*{be established for ever}* His meaning is, that God would continue the kingdom in his posterity, and settle the Lord Jesus, that should come from him, upon his everlasting throne.

*{and do as thou hast said}* He did believe that God would make good his word, but because prayer is an ordinary means to be used on man's part for the accomplishing of God's promises, he here uses it, the rather, to strengthen his own faith. See Ezek. 36:36, 37.

**V. 24.** *{Let it even be established}* His apprehension of the greatness of the blessing, and his earnest desire of having it accomplished, makes him crave the accomplishment thereof again and again in the same words.

*{that thy name may be magnified for ever}* The chief end that moved him to be so instant in his prayer was God's glory: for nothing could bring such everlasting glory to God as the exhibition of his Son, which is the main substance of his promise to David.

*{saying}* Or, *by saying*. All sorts of people that should hear of the accomplishing of the forementioned promise, would say as follows, and so magnify God.

*{The LORD of hosts}* See 1 King. 18:15.

*{is the God of Israel}* See 1 King. 8:15.

*{even a God to Israel}* See v. 21. The Lord was the God of Israel in regard of his sovereignty over them and covenant with them: he was a God to Israel in regard of his goodness to them.

*{and let the house of David thy servant be established}* See v. 23.

*{before thee}* In your grace and favor, so as you may take delight therein, and be well pleased therewith, and never cast it off, 1 King. 11:35.

**V. 25.** *{For Thou, O my God}* See ch. 11:19.

*{hast told thy servant}* Heb. *hast revealed the ear of thy servant*. I. e. God plainly made known his mind in David's hearing, so as he well understood it. See 1 Sam. 9:15; Ruth 4:4.

*{that thou wilt build him an house}* See v. 10.

*{therefore thy servant hath found in his heart}* *In his heart* is not here in the Hebrew; but it is well taken out of 2 Sam. 7:27. It implies that David had seriously thought of the matter in hand, and found his heart ready and forward thereunto.

*{to pray before thee}* To testify so earnestly as he did his hearty desire to have the promise accomplished. See v. 16.

**V. 26.** *{And now, LORD}* This has reference to v. 27.

*{thou art God}* The only true God. See 1 King. 18:30. To this is added, 2 Sam. 17:28, *And thy words be true*; which he alleges to testify his faith in the faithfulness of God.

*{and hast promised this goodness unto thy servant}* That which is distinctly expressed v. 9, etc.

**V. 27.** *{Now therefore let it please thee}* Or, *it hath pleased thee*. The former reading is a prayer that God would be pleased to ratify his word; see v. 23. The latter an acknowledgment of the goodness intimated v. 26, and a testification of his faith.

*{to bless the house of thy servant}* Under this word bless all manner of good things are comprised; particularly, that building of his house mentioned v. 10. By house is meant David's kingdom and posterity. See v. 10. This title servant in relation to God David has ten several times applied to himself in this prayer. His frequent mention hereof argues that strong confidence he had by reason of that relation betwixt God and him.

*{for ever}* See v. 12.

*{for thou blessest, O LORD}* This shows a readiness in God to bless, and a delight that he takes therein, as Mic. 7:18, and it is the groundwork of the prayer that David made to God. This is the tenth time that he has used in this prayer such a compellation as this. Sometimes he says, *O Lord God*, v. 17, sometimes, *O my God*, v. 25, sometimes Lord, v. 22, sometimes, *O God*, v. 17. Neither was this frequent mention of God's title a taking of his name in vain, nor an idle repetition of the same thing, but an evidence of his holy familiarity with God, and such a warrantable boldness, as he durst go to God, and speak to him as it were face to face: yea, it was an evidence of his strong affiance in God, and of his great affection to him. See Ezra 9:13.

*{and it shall be blessed for ever}* This further testifies David's faith in God's power and goodness. Whatsoever God undertakes shall accordingly be effected. That which Isaac said to Esau concerning Jacob, was said in God's name, *I have blessed him, yea and he shall be blessed*, Gen. 27:33.

## CHAPTER 18

**Ver. 1.** *{Now after this it came to pass}* In this and some other chapters following an especial reason is rendered why David was not a man fit to build the temple. God had other work for him to do, namely, to secure the land from enemies: for this end sundry battles that he fought and victories that he obtained are here in order set down.

*{that David smote the Philistines}* See ch. 10:1.

*{and subdued them}* He did not only vanquish them in a battle, as Shamgar did, Judg. 3:3, and Samuel, 1 Sam. 7:11, and Saul, 1 Sam. 14:31 and 17:52, and himself, ch. 14:10, 16, but so subdued them as they could not make head again, but rather became tributaries to Israel.

*{and took Gath}* Gath was the chief city among the Philistims, one of their principalities, 1 Sam. 6:17. See 2 King. 12:17. This city is called *Metheg-Ammah* 2 Sam. 8:1, which signifies *the bridle of Ammah*. The bridle implies government; for thereby horses are ruled. After this notation he took away their government from the Philistims.

*{and her towns}* Such as belonged to Gath; which being a great city, had sundry towns under her.

*{out of the hand of the Philistines}* Who till this time possessed it.

**V. 2.** *{And he smote Moab}* The name of the first progenitor, Gen. 19:37, is given to the whole stock. They were always deadly enemies to Israel, Psal. 82:6. David smote them as he had smitten the Philistims, v. 1. It is added 2 Sam. 8:2, *and measured them with a line*, etc.

*{and the Moabites became David’s servants, and brought gifts}* They became tributaries, and yielded homage unto him, and brought gifts, both such as were by David imposed upon them, as 2 King. 4:4, and also voluntary presents, to gain, and retain his favor, as 2 Chr. 17:10, 11.

**V. 3.** *{And David smote Hadarezer}* Or, *Hadadezer*, 2 Sam. 8:3. See 1 King. 11:23. He is there said to be the son of Rehob. Rehob was also the name of a place, Numb. 13:21; Josh. 19:28; 2 Sam. 10:8.

*{king of Zobah}* A country of Syria, 2 Sam. 10:8.

*{unto Hamath}* See 1 King. 8:65.

*{as he went to stablish his dominion by the river Euphrates}* This may be referred either to David or to Hadarezer. If to David, it implies that he went to settle the out-coasts of his kingdom bordering upon Euphrates, and that Hadarezer, whose kingdom reached also to Euphrates, opposed him. If to Hadarezer, then he hearing of David's victories over others, went to stablish the out-coasts of his own dominion, to prevent David's entering thereupon; and David having intelligence hereof, prevented him, and vanquished him. This he did the rather, because God had promised to give to Abraham's seed the land reaching to the river Euphrates, Gen. 15:18; Deut. 1:7; Josh. 1:4.

**V. 4.** *{And David took from him a thousand chariots}* Chariots were of old in great use for war; men using to sit or stand in them, and so to fight with their enemies, or shoot against them. Thereby they might with speed set upon an enemy, or fly from him. They were also useful in times of peace, to set out a prince's majesty. Solomon had great store of them, 1 King. 10:28. See 1 King. 9:19.

*{and seven thousand horsemen, etc.}* There is mention made only of seven hundred horsemen, 2 Sam. 8:4, but some suppose that only the choice and principal horsemen are there intended, but all sorts here: see on 2 Sam. 8:4.

*{David also houghed all the chariot horses}* He cut those nerves by which they are enabled to make the greater speed, and so made them unserviceable. See the like Josh. 11:9.

*{but reserved of them a hundred chariots}* It is probable that he burnt all the chariots save the hundred here mentioned; for so did Joshua, Josh. 11:9.

**V. 5.** *{And when the Syrians of Damascus}* Heb. *Damesek*. Here such Syrians are meant as either dwelt in Damascus, or appertained thereunto. See 1 King. 11:24.

*{came to help Hadarezer king of Zobah}* The Syrians of Damascus were confederates with Hadarezer, and ready to help him in his need.

*{David slew of the Syrians two and twenty thousand men}* Though this were a very great slaughter, and might have deterred them from taking part with any against David, yet to their shame and damage they did again join with the Ammonites against him, ch. 19:6, 16.

**V. 6.** *{Then David put garrisons in Syria-Damascus}* To keep them in awe, and restrain them from gathering head against him again.

*{and the Syrians became David’s servants, and brought gifts}* See v. 2.

*{Thus the LORD preserved David whithersoever he went}* Protected him against his enemies, and gave him good success in all that he undertook.

**V. 7.** *{And David took the shields of gold that were on the servants of Hadarezer}* It seems this king had store of gold, in that he made shields thereof, not only for himself but also for his servants. See 1 King. 10:17.

*{and brought them to Jerusalem}* There was his treasure, and there he laid them up, as things dedicated to the Lord, v. 11, and that for the house which he intended. See ch. 22:14.

**V. 8.** *{Likewise from Tibhath, and from Chun}* Or, *Betah and Berothai*, 2 Sam. 8:8. For divers names use to be given to the same places. When David took them they might be called Betah and Berothai: but when Ezra wrote this book, Tibhath and Chun.

*{cities of Hadarezer}* David did not only rout the army of Hadarezer, but also so pursued the victory as he took his cities.

*{brought David very much brass}* As this booty was great in quantity, so it was very precious in the kind and quality; for it was very choice brass. So 1 King. 7:45.

*{wherewith Solomon made the brasen sea, and the pillars, and the vessels of brass}* See 1 King. 7:23, etc. and 2 Chr. 4:15, etc.

**V. 9.** *{Now when Tou}* Or, *Toi*, 2 Sam. 8:9.

*{king of Hamath}* Hamath bordered upon the dominion of Hadarezer: and this was the reason of the war betwixt him and Tou, whereof in the next verse.

*{heard how David had smitten all the host of Hadarezer}* The victories, or overthrows of princes, cannot but quickly come to their neighbors' ears, and much affect them one way or other.

*{king of Zobah}* See v. 3.

**V. 10.** *{He sent Hadoram}* Or, *Joram*, 2 Sam. 8:10. Either these two names were given to one person, or by continuance of time some letters and syllables were thus altered.

*{his son}* That his message might be the better accepted, he sends his own son, and it may be the heir of his kingdom, on the ambassage.

*{to king David to enquire of his welfare}* Or, *to salute him*. Heb. *to ask of him for peace*. Tou having good intelligence of David's valor and success, earnestly desired to be one of his confederates and to have peace with him. So did the Gibeonites with Israel, Josh. 9:3, etc.

*{and to congratulate him}* Heb. *to bless him*. To thank God for his success, and to wish that he might still prosper. The words may also be taken indefinitely for a royal congratulation, which is usual with neighboring kings.

*{because he had fought against Hadarezer, and smitten him}* See v. 3. The overthrow of one's enemy is a great advantage to him: therefore Tou makes this the occasion of his congratulatory message.

*{for Hadarezer had war with Tou}* Heb. *because a man of war Tou was with Hadarezer*. I. e. there were many wars betwixt them, and Tou was forced to defend himself by force of arms against Hadarezer.

*{and with him all manner of vessels}* *With him* is not in the Hebrew. Some, to make up the sense, read, *And in his hand were all manner of vessels*. The Hebrew text 2 Sam. 8:10 has it so. The meaning is, that Tou sent by and with his son Hadoram all manner of vessels. See 1 King. 10:21; 2 King. 7:15.

*{of gold and silver}* See 1 King. 10:25; 2 King. 5:5.

*{and brass}* See v. 8.

**V. 11.** *{Them also King David dedicated unto the LORD}* See 1 King. 7:5. David set them apart for the house of God, to be used about holy services for God's honor, and to be put to no other use.

*{with the silver and the gold which he brought from all these nations}* All which he subdued, 2 Sam. 8:11.

*{from Edom}* Syria is named instead of Edom, 2 Sam. 8:12. David might take from both places. Or Syria and Edom joining together against David, as Syria and Ammon did, ch. 19:6, etc. the army consisting of both nations being overthrown, that which was taken from one might also be said to be taken from the other. Of David's conquest over Edom see v. 12, 13.

*{and from Moab}* V. 2; 2 Sam. 8:2.

*{and from the children of Ammon}* These also did David vanquish, ch. 20:1. We read how God forbade the children of Israel to fight against Edom, Moab, and Ammon, Deut. 2:5, 9, 19. How then does David destroy them? R. That inhibition was for a time, while that brotherhood or kindred that had been betwixt the fathers of those nations was fresh in memory: Esau, the father of the Edomites was Jacob's brother; and Ammon and Moab were sons of Lot, who was Abraham's kinsman. But these three nations bearing an implacable hatred against Israel, and being their deadly enemies, Psal. 83:6, 7, and their hatred increasing more and more, generation after generation, they became at length unsufferable: and it is probable that they first set on David, as the Philistims did, ch. 14:8, and so gave the occasion of the war. The occasion which Ammon gave is expressly set down 2 Sam. 10:1, etc. The Edomites took part with the Syrians, and so brought destruction upon themselves. See v. 11, 12.
