The Jewish rabbins say that the king of Moab slew David's father and mother, whom he left in his land, 1 Sam. 22:3.

*{and from the Philistines}* See ch. 14:8, etc.

*{and from Amalek}* Amalek was Israel's most deadly enemy, and first set upon them, Exod. 17:8; Deut. 25:17, etc. God devoted them to utter destruction, Exod. 17:16; Deut. 25:19; 1 Sam. 15:3. David therefore had just cause to destroy and spoil them. To these whom David spoiled, is added Hadarezer son of Rehob king of Zobah, 2 Sam. 8:12. Of him see v. 3, etc.

**V. 12.** *{Moreover Abishai the son of Zeruiah}* See ch. 2:16 and 11:20.

*{slew of the Edomites}* See v. 11.

*{in the valley of Salt}* See 1 King. 14:7.

*{eighteen thousand}* Upon this slaughter it is said that *David gat him a name*. For what captains and generals in war do is attributed to the king under whom they fight. Yet it may be David himself was in the field when this was done, though Abishai with the troops under him, set upon the Edomites and slew them. This and other victories of David being made known far and near, his name and fame was spread abroad thereby, and he became famous. In this respect it is said that *he gat him a name when he returned from smiting of the Syrians*, 2 Sam. 8:13. They who are there called Syrians are here called Edomites, as before Edom and Syria were put one for another, v. 11. The title of the 60 psalm has reference to this story, where it is said that *David strove with Aram-Zobah, when Joab returned and smote of Edom in the valley of Salt twelve thousand*. There are two seeming differences betwixt that and this place. The first concerns the persons. There the conqueror is said to be Joab; here, Abishai: but both might have a hand in this victory, and so that difference is reconciled. The other difference is in the number of them that were slain. There mention is made only of twelve thousand, here of eighteen: but Abishai might first put the enemy to flight, and slay six thousand; and then Joab pursue the victory, and slay twelve thousand more; both which make up eighteen thousand. The slaughter of all is here attributed to Abishai, because he first put the enemy to flight. See on the title to Psal. 60.

**V. 13.** *{And he put garrisons in Edom, etc.}* I. e. David, mentioned v. 11. He dealt here with the Edomites, as he dealt with the Syrians v. 6. Here was first fulfilled the prophecy of Isaac, Gen. 27:29, 37. This subjection of Edom to Israel continued till the days of wicked Joram. See 2 King. 8:20, 22.

*{Thus the LORD preserved, etc.}* See v. 6.

**V. 14.** *{So David reigned over all Israel}* This has relation to ch. 11:3 and 12:38.

*{and executed judgment and justice among all his people}* As David's piety to God is set out ch. 13:3, etc. and 15:1, etc. and 16:1, etc. and 17:1, etc. and his valor against his enemies, ch. 14:8, etc. and v. 1, etc. so here, his justice in well ordering his state and kingdom. He neither waxed insolent nor secure upon the victories he had gotten: but having freed his kingdom from all his enemies round about, he sets himself well to govern his own people by executing justice and judgment. Judgment has respect to the law whereby the state was then governed, to order the affairs of his kingdom according to it: justice has respect to his distributing to every one according to his demerit, countenancing and encouraging the good and righteous, and punishing evil ones. This he did impartially to all without respect of persons.

**V. 15.** *{And Joab the son of Zeruiah was over the host}* See ch. 11:6.

*{and Jehoshaphat the son of Ahilud, recorder}* See 1 King. 4:3.

**V. 16.** *{And Zadok the son of Ahitub}* See ch. 6:8, 52.

*{and Abimelech}* Or, *Abimelech*, 2 Sam. 8:17.

*{the son of Abiathar}* Abiathar is said to be the son of Ahimelech, 1 Sam. 23:6, and Zadok and Abiathar are said to be priests, 2 Sam. 20:25. To reconcile these places, it is to be conceived that both these persons, father and son, were each of them called Abimelech and Abiathar; both of them had these two names. So the same person that is called Abimelech, (who was the father) 1 Sam. 21:1 and 1 Sam. 23:6, is called Abiathar, Mark 2:16, and here the son, that was called Abiathar 1 Sam. 23:6, is called Abimelech.

*{were the priests}* These two were of divers stocks: for Ahimelech or Abiathar was of the sons of Ithamar, and Zadok of the sons of Eleazar, ch. 24:3. Now to prevent contention, David gave them a kind of equal jurisdiction, that one should not be over the other; and in that respect they are both said to be priests. Abiathar descended from Eli, who had been judge, and accounted high priest in his time, 1 Sam. 14:3. Besides the father of Abiathar was slain in David's cause, 1 Sam. 22:16, and Abiathar himself was afflicted in all wherein David was afflicted, 1 King. 2:26. Therefore he was accounted the high priest till Solomon put him out, and set Zadok in his room, 1 King. 2:35.

*{and Shavsha}* Called Seraiah, 2 Sam. 8:17, and Sisa or Shisha, 1 King. 4:3.

*{was scribe}* Or, *secretary*. See 1 King. 4:3.

**V. 17.** *{And Benaiah the son of Jehoiada}* See 1 King. 4:4.

*{was over the Cherethites and the Pelethites}* See 1 King. 1:38.

*{and the sons of David}* This must be meant of David's elder sons, such as were born to him in Hebron, ch. 3:1, 2, 3. The other could not yet be old enough to manage such places.

*{were chief about the king}* Heb. *at the hand of the king*. In 2 Sam. 8:18 it is *were chief rulers*, or, *princes*. They had the highest places in the kingdom, next to the king himself. Which might be one occasion of the insolency of Absalom, 2 Sam. 15:10, and of Adonijah, 1 King. 1:5. The forementioned officers are expressly set down to commend David's prudence about ordering the affairs both of the state and church: of the state in time of war, in ordaining Joab to be general, and Benaiah over the host; and in time of peace, in appointing a recorder, a scribe, and sundry rulers; of the church, in appointing priests.

## CHAPTER 19

**Ver. 1.** *{Now it came to pass after this}* 2 Sam. 10:1, etc. This has relation to the former chapter, that sets down the victories that David had over all his enemies round about, and his settling the affairs both of church and state in good order. David might think that now he was freed from all fear of enemies abroad, and tumultuous persons at home. But God saw it meet yet further to exercise his valor, prudence, faith and other graces, and therefore minister fresh occasions of trying the same.

*{that Nahash the king of the children of Ammon}* This was that malicious enemy of whom mention is made 1 Sam. 11:1, etc.

*{died}* Kings are subject to death as well as others.

*{and his son}* His name was Hanun, v. 2 and 2 Sam. 10:1. There were Israelites of this name, Neh. 3:13, 30.

*{reigned in his stead}* It seems this kingdom was hereditary.

**V. 2.** *{And David said, I will shew kindness unto Hanun the son of Nahash}* Gratitude is to be manifested even to strangers.

*{because his father shewed kindness unto me}* It is not expressed in Scripture what that kindness was; but it may probably be supposed, that Saul and Nahash being utter enemies, Nahash might courteously entertain David when he fled from Saul, as Achish did, 1 Sam. 27:3.

*{And David sent messengers to comfort him concerning his father}* Kings that are not at enmity one with another use to send ambassadors, as to congratulate one another's good success, so to condole one another's losses.

*{So the servants of David came into the land of the children of Ammon}* Though the Ammonites had formerly been enemies to the Israelites, yet these servants of David, being sent on a friendly ambassage, suspected no such ill handling as they met with, v. 4.

*{to Hanun}* To him they were sent, and they rested not until they came to the court where he was, even into his presence.

*{to comfort him}* It is a comfort to such as have occasion of sorrow, that others are sensible of their grief. And the contrary is a great aggravation of one's sorrow, Lam. 1:12. It is added 2 Sam. 10:2, *by the hand of his servants for his father*: i. e. by their ministry. See 1 King. 8:53. What is done by a king's ambassadors, is accounted to be done by the king himself. This epithet *their Lord* is added 2 Sam. 10:3. See 2 King. 9:7.

**V. 3.** *{But the princes of the children of Ammon said to Hanun}* These were his courtiers and of his privy council, who set themselves to flatter him, and to put undue jealousies into his head.

*{Thinkest thou that David doth honour thy father}* Heb. *in thine eyes doth David honor thy father*. They suppose that howsoever others might take it, yet it could not appear to Hanun to be an honor.

*{that he hath sent comforters unto thee}* They question not the intended end, as if it were no honor for one king to seek to comfort another in his sorrow; but they question the truth of David's intent.

*{are not his servants come to thee for to search}* Namely, *to search the city*, as it is expounded 2 Sam. 10:3.

*{and to overthrow}* Having searched out some advantages for bringing in an army, they may seek to destroy the city.

*{and to spy out the land}* When they have gotten the city, they may go through the whole land, and spy out what spoils they can. Their evil surmises make them conceive the worst they could.

**V. 4.** *{Wherefore Hanun took David’s servants}* Being ambassadors, it was against the law of nations to disgrace and wrong them.

*{and shaved them}* 2 Sam. 10:4, Shaved off the one half of their beards. A palpable disgrace. To have half the beard on and half off does much disfigure the face of a man, making his countenance worse than a fool's.

*{and cut off their garments in the midst}* They were wont in those days to wear long side garments; so as they being cut off in the midst, their lower parts which used to be covered, were laid open, for others to laugh at them.

*{hard by their buttocks}* This phrase is added to aggravate the disgrace, because those parts especially use to be covered. See Isa. 20:4.

*{and sent them away}* This implies that he sent some along with them, to see that all the while they were in his dominion they should neither shave the other part of their beards, to make all alike, nor put on other apparel, but be a derision and scorn to all that should look upon them.

**V. 5.** *{Then there went certain, and told David how the men were served}* Some that pitied their case, as soon as they discerned how they were handled, made post-speed to David, that some course might be taken so soon as possible could be to take away their shame.

*{and he sent to meet them}* David being sensible of their disgrace, sends to them before they could come to him.

*{for the men were greatly ashamed}* Disgrace much works on those that suffer it, though they have no way deserved it.

*{and the king said, Tarry at Jericho}* Though Jericho lay at this time ruinated, (for it was not rebuilt till Ahab's reign, 1 King. 16:34.) yet there might be some houses and convenient places of abode.

*{until your beards be grown}* That could not be under two or three months.

*{and then return}* When they might come without such signs of dishonor and disgrace.

**V. 6.** *{And when the children of Ammon saw}* Or, *understood*, either by some that told them how ill David took their abuse of his messengers, and what just revenge he intended: or their own conscience convincing them of their villainy, they could not but fear that David would revenge that ungrateful and inhuman act.

*{that they had made themselves odious to David}* Heb. *to stink with, or, before, David*. So it is translated 2 Sam. 10:6. They thought that David could no more endure them than a filthy stinking thing, and thereupon would seek to destroy them.

*{Hanun and the children of Ammon}* King and subjects conspire together to their own ruin.

*{sent a thousand talents of silver}* See ch. 22:14. This was a fair bait to allure their neighbors to afford them aid.

*{to hire them chariots and horsemen out of Mesopotamia}* Heb. *Aram-Naharaim*. This was the place from whence Isaac had his wife, Gen. 24:10, and that country out of which God called Abraham, Act. 7:2. The Hebrew name shows that it was in Syria; and thereupon the Israelites in their confession were to acknowledge that a Syrian was their father, Deut. 26:5. The first king that oppressed the Israelites after Joshua's time was king of this place, Judg. 3:8, 10.

*{and out of Syria-Maacah}* Syria contained sundry dominions in it, whereof this was one.

*{and out of Zobah}* This also was another part of Syria. See ch. 18:3. The persons that inhabited the forementioned places are called *Syrians of Beth-Rehob*, and *Syrians of Zoba*, 2 Sam. 10:6. Beth-Rehob signifies *the house of Rehob*. Now Rehob was the father of Hadadezer before mentioned, 2 Sam. 8:3, 12. And Zobah was the place of which Hadadezer was king, 2 Sam. 8:5.

**V. 7.** *{And they hired thirty and two thousand chariots}* See ch. 18:4. It is added 2 Sam. 10:6, *twenty thousand footmen*. These were, besides such as rode in the chariots.

*{and the king of Maacah and his people}* This has reference to the verb hired. The Ammonites hired the king of Maacah and his people, which are said to be a thousand men, 2 Sam. 10:6. It is there also added, *and of Ishtob twelve thousand men*: these also were Syrians, 2 Sam. 10:8. All the men that were hired were thirty and three thousand, besides those that rode and fought in chariots. This was a very great number, that came out of their own country to help another king. But David having much spoiled the Syrians, and brought them under subjection, ch. 18:5, 6, it may be thereupon supposed that they came the more willingly to aid the Ammonites, as hoping now to break the yoke which David had laid upon their necks, and to revenge themselves of him.

*{who came and pitched before Medeba}* Medeba was in the country of Moab, Numb. 29:30; Isa. 15:2, near which there was a large plain, Josh. 13:9, 16.

*{And the children of Ammon gathered themselves together from their cities, and came to battle}* To oppose David and his men, and to defend themselves against them.

**V. 8.** *{And when David heard of it}* This has reference both to the wrong which the king of Ammon did to David's ambassadors, and also to the preparations of the Ammonites and Syrians. These moved him the more speedily to set forth his host.

*{he sent Joab}* The general, ch. 11:6.

*{and all the host of the mighty men}* Mighty men have an especial relation to captains and commanders, such as are described ch. 12:1, etc. And it may be that all David's host consisted of mighty men.

**V. 9.** *{And the children of Ammon came out}* Out of their several cities, v. 7. Or, out of the city Medeba; or, out of their tents.

*{and put the battle in array}* Readily to fight with David.

*{before the gate of the city}* That they might the better maintain it, and keep it from the spoil which David's soldiers might otherwise make in it: yea, that also it might be a safe retiring place for the army, in case it should be put to rout in the field. See v. 15. They would not put off so great a charge to the Syrians, who were strangers, but undertook it themselves.

*{and the kings that were come}* These are said to be *Syrians, of Zoba, and of Rehob, and Ishtob, and Maacah*, 2 Sam. 10:8. These two places compared show that both those Syrian kings and their armies were together.

*{were by themselves in the field}* The Ammonites were by themselves, and the Syrians by themselves. So they made two great armies. See v. 12.

**V. 10.** *{Now when Joab saw that the battle}* Heb. *the face of the battle*.

*{was set against him before and behind}* The Ammonites being on one side, and the Syrians on the other.

*{he chose out of all the choice of Israel}* Or, *young men of Israel*. For they oft are most forward to set on an enemy.

*{and put them in array against the Syrians}* Joab herein dealt very politicly. He supposed that strangers, who were hired to the war, would not stand to it so stoutly and constantly as the Ammonites, who had done the wrong to Israel; therefore he makes the first assault against them, and that a fierce one by the most valiant of his army, to put them to rout; that upon their flight the Ammonites might be dismayed, and fly too. This his policy prevailed, v. 14, 15.

**V. 11.** *{And the rest of the people}* Namely, those soldiers which David sent out: who though they were not such choice men as the others, yet were mighty men, v. 8.

*{he delivered into the hand of Abishai}* Heb. *Abshai*. See ch. 11:20.

*{his brother}* See ch. 2:16.

*{and they set themselves in array against the children of Ammon}* As the enemy divided their army into two bodies, so did Joab his army: thus was body against body; whereby the enemies were kept from helping one another; but the Israelites had liberty to afford succor one to another, v. 12.

**V. 12.** *{And he said, If the Syrians be too strong for me, then thou shalt help me}* For they who assaulted had more liberty to leave their standing without danger than they who were assaulted.

*{but if the children of Ammon be too strong for thee, then I will help thee}* He promises his help to encourage his brother the more, and to move him to help him, in case he should need.

**V. 13.** *{Be of good courage}* This very phrase of encouragement is used by God himself to Joshua three times in one discourse, Josh. 1:6, 7, 9, and by Moses, Deut. 31:7. Yea, the Philistims used it to stir up their own side, 1 Sam. 4:9. Such exhortations put great life into soldiers.

*{and let us behave ourselves valiantly for our people}* He means hereby their country, the church and commonwealth therein. The heathen out of zeal to the welfare of their country would venture far themselves, and incite one another very much: well therefore might Joab use this motive.

*{and for the cities of our God}* Those which God as an inheritance had given unto them, and by the inhabitants whereof God was acknowledged, worshiped and glorified.

*{and let the LORD do that which is good in his sight}* Or, *that which pleaseth him*. This was a pious submission, to leave off the issue of the war to God's good pleasure.

**V. 14.** *{So Joab and the people that were with him}* See v. 10.

*{drew nigh before the Syrians, unto the battle}* They did not tarry till the enemy should set upon them; but like valiant men, lion-like, they first set on the enemy, and at the first onset daunted them.

*{and they fled before him}* When men's spirits are daunted, they soon fly.

**V. 15.** *{And when the children of Ammon saw that the Syrians were fled}* Either both companies were in sight one of another, or they had scouts that soon brought them those ill tidings.

*{they likewise fled before Abishai his brother}* The Ammonites being much daunted at the foresaid tidings, dare not stand out any farther, but fly also.

*{and entered into the city}* See v. 9.

*{Then Joab came to Jerusalem}* The main work for which he drew out the soldiery into the field being finished, which was a complete victory over the enemy, he returns to the city whence they came, till there should be another occasion to draw them out.

**V. 16.** *{And when the Syrians saw that they were put to the worse before Israel}* The Hebrew word translated *put to the worst* signifies *to be smitten unto death*: and questionless many of them were left dead upon the ground.

*{they sent messengers and drew forth the Syrians that were beyond the river}* I. e. Euphrates. See Gen. 2:14 and 15:18. There were Syrians on both sides Euphrates. See ch. 18:3. The Syrians having before this been overthrown by the Israelites, and made tributaries, ch. 18:5, 6, feared lest David should now utterly ruinate them, for taking part with his enemies. To prevent therefore what they feared, they make all the strength they can, and send up and down to the utmost coasts of their dominion, to procure help against the Israelites. It is said, 2 Sam. 10:15, *they gathered themselves together*; namely, as many as remained of the former army which fled, and as many as were on this side the river able to bear arms. It is further said, *they came to Helam*, 2 Sam. 10:16. The Syrians that were beyond the river came thither. Helam is not in any other place of Scripture mentioned; but it is supposed to be a city among the Arabians, called Alamsham by geographers.

*{and Shophach}* Or, *Shobach*, 2 Sam. 10:16.

*{the captain of the host of Hadarezer went before them}* It may be that a king led the army of the Syrians before: see v. 7. But now supposing that an experienced general might better order the battle than a king, they choose Shophach to lead the army. See 1 King. 20:24.

**V. 17.** *{And it was told David}* David, being a prudent prince, had his intelligencers in all countries round about him, to give him notice of what was done in several kingdoms, especially amongst his enemies.

*{and he gathered all Israel}* David being himself a valiant man, 2 Sam. 17:8, hearing what great preparations the Syrians made, gathered a great host together: it may be supposed that it was greater than the former with which Joab went out, v. 8. He also himself went in his own person, the more to encourage his soldiers.

*{and passed over Jordan}* That so he might meet them in their own land, and make it the seat of the war. It is added 2 Sam. 10:17, *and came to Helam*, see v. 16.

*{and came upon them}* This phrase implies that he with his army came sooner than they were expected.

*{and set the battle in array against them}* David did as Joab had done before, v. 14, he first set upon the enemy.

*{so when David had put the battle in array against the Syrians, they fought with him}* They were forced so to do, unless without fighting they would yield.

**V. 18.** *{But the Syrians fled before Israel}* As before, v. 14.

*{and David slew the Syrians seven thousand men which fought in chariots}* This is thus expressed 2 Sam. 10:18, *David slew the men of seven hundred chariots of the Syrians*. The Annotations on our English translation at Geneva say that the chiefest and most principal are set down in 2 Sam. 10:18, and the whole number here: or that the soldiers which were in the seven hundred chariots are here mentioned. Or the seven hundred chariots may be taken for so many ranks of chariots, whereof there being ten in every rank, they make up seven thousand. See on 2 Sam. 10:18.

*{and forty thousand footmen}* In 2 Sam. 10:18 it is *forty thousand horsemen*. There is no contradiction. David might slay both forty thousand footmen, and also *forty thousand horsemen*. Hereby is amplified the greatness both of the Syrians' army and of David's success. See 1 King. 11:23.

*{and killed Shophach the captain of the host}* Or, *smote Shobach the captain of the host*, who died there, 2 Sam. 10:18. It seems the Syrians stood stoutly to it, in that so many chariot-men, horsemen, and footmen, together with their general, were slain. They thought that if they were overthrown, they should be made slaves, and therefore stood it out to the uttermost.

**V. 19.** *{And when the servants of Hadarezer}* Or, *when all the kings that were servants to Hadarezer*, 2 Sam. 10:19. Such as he had hired, and brought from beyond Euphrates. See v. 16.

*{saw that they were put to the worse before Israel}* See v. 15. Observing how oft the Syrians had been vanquished by the Israelites, they were not willing any more to stand against them.

*{they made peace with David}* Or, *with Israel*, 2 Sam. 10:19. They so submitted themselves to David as they yielded to such conditions of peace as were put upon them.

*{and became his servants}* See ch. 18:6. Or, *they served them*, 2 Sam. 10:19. The Syrians served the Israelites.

*{neither would the Syrians help the children of Ammon any more}* Against the Israelites. They feared so to do, 2 Sam. 10:19.

## CHAPTER 20

**V. 1.** *{And it came to pass, that after the year was expired}* Heb. *at the return of the year*. This is not to be taken for a whole year, but to be reckoned from that time that David had overcome the Ammonites and Syrians; which might be about the beginning of winter.

*{at the time that Kings use to go out to battle}* They that had enemies, and either stood in fear of them, or sought to annoy them, used to go into the field when they might have hope both of temperate weather, and also of sufficient provision for themselves and their beasts; i.e. about the springtime, when the days begin to be somewhat long, and the earth to bring forth all store of fruit. See 1 Kings 20:22.

*{Joab led forth the power of the army}* It is said, 2 Sam. 11:1, that *David sent Joab and his servants with him, and all Israel*. By servants are meant David’s subjects; by all Israel, such out of every tribe as were fit to go forth to war. By these persons is declared the meaning of this phrase, the power of the army, even such soldiers as were mighty men (see ch. 10:8), and a great number of these.

*{and wasted the country of the children of Ammon}* Or, *they destroyed the children of Ammon*, 2 Sam. 11:1. These two phrases show that they both destroyed the persons of their enemies and also demolished their towns and cities, and did other spoil in the land.

*{and came and besieged Rabbah}* An ancient and principal city of the Ammonites, Deut. 3:11. When judgments were denounced by prophets against the Ammonites, they were wont to be directed in particular against Rabbah; as Jer. 49:2, 3; Ezek. 25:5; Amos 1:14. Rabbah was to the Ammonites as Jerusalem to Judah, Ezek. 20:20. It seems the Israelites had much prevailed against the Ammonites, in that they came so far into their land with their whole army as to besiege the chief city of the kingdom.

*{but David tarried at Jerusalem}* David’s tarrying at Jerusalem proved of very sad consequence. What fell out betwixt the besieging of Rabbah and taking it is distinctly set down from 2 Sam. 11:2 to 2 Sam. 12:26.

*{and Joab smote Rabbah}* Before this was done, Joab having taken part of the city, sent to inform David how far he had prevailed, and withal to advise him to come with a greater army, and to take the city, that the glory of taking it might be the king’s, and not his. Herein as in many other particulars, Joab showed himself faithful to his sovereign, and careful of his honor. See 2 Sam. 12:26, &c.

*{and destroyed it}* Though it be said 2 Sam. 12:29, that David, with all the people, fought against Rabbah and took it, yet Joab having long besieged it, might with his army be the principal in smiting and destroying it. To David this is ascribed, because he was the chief commander and there present; to Joab because he was the chief actor, and ventured his person furthest.

**V. 2.** *{And David took the crown of their King from off his head}* In sign of an absolute conquest the king of Ammon might be brought before David, and the crown put upon his head, and then taken off from his head, and put upon David’s.

*{and found it to weigh}* Heb. *the weight of*.

*{a talent of gold}* i.e. According to the weight of the sanctuary an hundred twenty-five pound, after twelve ounces the pound, and three thousand seven hundred and fifty pound sterling in worth. See ch. 22:14. The common account was but half so much. But according to it this crown was too massive for a king to bear long on his head, it weighing sixty-two pound and an half.

*{and there were precious stones in it}* Of what kind and how many, is not expressed. But it is very probable that they were of the choicest sort, and not few in number; so as this adds exceeding much to the worth of the crown, though to the weight but little, if anything at all. It is said, 2 Sam. 12:30, *the weight was a talent of gold, with the precious stones*. The gold and precious stones together made up the weight of a talent.

*{and it was set upon Davids head}* Or at least held a little over his head by others, as a sign that the royalty of the king of Ammon was now transferred upon David.

*{and he brought also exceeding much spoil out of the city}* They plundered the whole city which was a fair and rich one.

**V. 3.** *{And he brought out the people that were in it}* He brought them as prisoners and captives out of the city, at least so many of them as he intended to make examples and spectacles of his just revenge. And it is to be supposed that they were such as he had learned had been counselors, executioners or abettors in that shameful act, ch. 19:4.

*{and cut them with saws}* Or, *sawed them asunder*; which could not be but a very painful kind of death. So were some professors of the truth used for their professions’ sake, Heb. 11:37.

*{and with harrows of iron}* It is said, 2 Sam. 12:31, *They were put under harrows of iron*. Whence we may gather that they were laid naked upon the ground, and heavy harrows with sharp pikes of iron were dragged over them, to tear their flesh therewith.

*{and with axes}* Or, *under axes of iron*, 2 Sam. 12:31. This implies that they were hewn in pieces with axes, as Agag was, 1 Sam. 15:33. It is further added 2 Sam. 12:31, *That he made them pass through the brick-kiln*. This was an hot burning furnace, such an one as the midst of a brick or tile-kiln, when they burn their bricks or tiles, used to be. It might be like that whereinto Nebuchadnezzar cast the three companions of Daniel, Dan. 3:20. Some from the notation of the word *מלכן* *malchen* take it for the place where they used to burn their children to Molech, and translate it *the furnace of Molech*: for Molech was the idol of the Ammonites, 1 Kings 11:7, and children used to be offered up in the fire to that idol, Lev. 18:21. See 2 Kings 16:3, and 23:10. These kinds of handling enemies may seem very cruel and inhumane: But sundry reasons may be given to justify David; as I. It is to be supposed that he had some warrant from God thus to deal with them, either by inward instinct, or by some prophet. II. Their villainous fact deserved as much, being against the law of nations, and the rule of amity. See ch. 19:2, 4. III. The Ammonites had exercised great cruelty upon the Israelites, Amos 1:13. IV. They were most barbarously cruel to their own children, in sacrificing them to Molech.

*{even so dealt David with all the cities of the children of Ammon}* I suppose this has special reference to the latter end of v. 2, where it is said that David brought much spoil out of the city. There is no mention made of that estate wherein David left them; but it is probable that he made them tributaries, and put garrisons over them; as he had dealt with the Moabites and Syrians, ch. 18:2, 6. We read after this, that Shobi the son of Nahash showed great courtesy to David when he fled from Absalom, 2 Sam. 17:27. Whence we infer that David took all kind of government from Hanun, ch. 19:2, and made his brother Shobi governor over the Ammonites, but tributary to Israel.

*{And David and all the people returned to Jerusalem}* See ch. 19:15.

**V. 4.** *{And it came to pass after this}* Another battle against the Philistims wherein a giant was slain by Abishai, is set before this, 2 Sam. 21:15, &c.

*{that there arose}* Or, *continued*. Heb. *stood*. This phrase implies other wars, and that with the persons here mentioned.

*{war at Gezer}* A fair city of Ephraim, given to the Levites, Josh. 21:21. See 1 Kings 9:15. It is said, 2 Sam. 21:18, that this battle was at Gob; now Gob bordered upon Gezer.

*{with the Philistines}* See ch. 14:8.

*{at which time Sibbechai the Hushathite}* Of whom see ch. 11:29.

*{slew Sippai}* Or Saph, 2 Sam. 21:18.

*{that was of the children of the giant}* Or, *Rapha*, which signifies *a giant*, as Deut. 3:11, 13. Philistims had sundry giants, and oft brought them into the war. See 1 Sam. 17:4, but they were made objects of the valor of David and his worthies, by whom they were slain.

*{and they were subdued}* For when their giant, in whom they trusted, was slain, they presently fled, 1 Sam. 17:51.

**V. 5.** *{And there was war again with the Philistines}* See v. 4.

*{and Elhanan}* This also was one of David’s worthies, ch. 11:26.

*{the son of Jair}* Called also Jaare-oregim, 2 Sam. 21:19. He is there said to be a Bethlehemite, and he is said to be son of Dodo, ch. 11:26.

*{slew Lahmi, the brother of Goliath the Gittite}* He is called the Gittite, because he was of Gath. This Goliath here mentioned, was he whom David slew, 1 Sam. 17:50.

*{whose spear-staff was like a weavers beam}* See ch. 11:23.

**V. 6.** *{And yet again there was war at Gath}* Gath was one of the principalities of the Philistims, 1 Sam. 6:17.

*{where was a man of great stature}* Heb. *a man of measure*. See ch. 11:23.

*{whose fingers and toes were four and twenty, six on each hand, and six on each foot}* See on 2 Sam. 21:20.

*{and he also was the son of the giant}* Heb. *born to the giant, or Rapha*. See v. 4.

**V. 7.** *{But when he defied Israel}* Or, *reproached Israel*. He upbraided them with their impotency and pusillanimity, as if there were none that dared combat with him. So did Goliath, 1 Sam. 17:8, &c.

*{Jonathan the son of Shimea}* Called Shammah, 1 Sam. 16:9, and Jonathan is called Jonadab, 2 Sam. 13:3.

*{Davids brother, slew him}* Shimea was David’s brother, 1 Sam. 16:9, and David uncle to this Jonathan; but there was another Jonathan that was David’s uncle, ch. 27:32.

**V. 8.** *{These were born unto the giant}* Heb. *Rapha*. Rapha may here be taken for the proper name of the father of five giants; The first, Goliath, whom David slew, 1 Sam. 17:50. brother to Lahmi, v. 5; the second, Ishbi-benob, 2 Sam. 21:16; the third, Sippai, v. 4; the fourth, Lahmi; the fifth, the monster described v. 6.

*{in Gath}* See v. 6.

*{and they fell by the hand of David}* The first only was slain by David himself, 1 Sam. 17:50. The slaughter of the other is attributed unto him as the chief commander.

*{and by the hand of his servants}* Such as were before mentioned.

## CHAPTER 21

**V. 1.** *{And Satan}* Heb. *the adversary*; so it is translated, Num. 22:22. It is most frequently put for the devil, Job 1:6; Ps. 109:6; Zech. 3:1, and is in all languages used for one of the devil’s names: And it is a name fitting his malicious disposition; for he is an adversary to God and man.

*{stood up against Israel}* Though he tempted David only, yet he is justly said to stand up against Israel, in regard both of his own intent, and also of the event. He well knew the sin of the king would bring judgment upon the people: and so it fell out, v. 14.

*{and provoked David to number Israel}* This must needs be a sinful act, because the devil provoked David thereto: for all his temptations are to sin. But simply to number the people of a land is not sinful. God commands it, Num. 1:3, and 26:1, 2, and governors have done it without sin, 2 Chron. 3:17, and 25:5. It was pride and boasting in the arm of flesh whereunto Satan tempted David, and wherein he prevailed. It is said, 2 Sam. 24:1, *And again the anger of the Lord was kindled against Israel, and he moved David against them, to say, Go, number Israel and Judah*. Of the anger of the Lord see 2 Kings 13:3. This particle again has reference to former anger of God against Israel; as when there were slain twenty thousand of them who conspired with Absalom, 2 Sam. 18:7, and when a famine continued for three years, 2 Sam. 21:1. Though the particular sin for which God was now angry be not expressed; yet by the inference of this history upon what goes before it may more than probably be collected. In 2 Sam. 23:8, &c. the catalogue of David’s worthies is set down: and sundry victories which the Israelites had are recorded ch. 20. Now upon these victories the people might grow too insolent and secure, or be overproud of, and confident in those worthies, and thereupon the Lord be angry with them. That which is here attributed to Satan, is in 2 Sam. 24:1 attributed unto God, namely, that he moved David. Some apply that relative he to Satan, *q. d.* *The anger of the Lord was kindled against Israel, and Satan moved David against them*: but there is nothing in 2 Sam. 24:1 that can give an hint of such a reference; therefore it is to be taken for truth, that both God and Satan moved David; God, as a just judge, using Satan as his minister therein, that he might punish both David and his people for other sins; (see 1 Kings 22:22) and Satan on a mischievous intent putting on David to such a sin as might bring vengeance on the people. This phrase, *He moved David against Israel*, shows that sins of kings prove prejudicial to their people. Besides David’s own sins, the people’s sins moved God to give him over to Satan; and for his sin were the people punished, v. 14.

**V. 2.** *{And David said to Joab}* Joab was commander in chief over all the men of Israel that went to war. So much is expressed 2 Sam. 24:2. *The King said to Joab the Captain of the host*.

*{and to the Rulers of the people}* Such as are set down ch. 27:16, &c. These were, every of them in their several tribes, to number the people, and Joab to take care of having it done faithfully. It is added, 2 Sam. 24:2, *The host which was with him*, namely, with Joab: Part of the host was attending on the king, part with Joab; and the rest in garrisons. That part which was with Joab was in this part to be assistant to him.

*{Go, number Israel and Judah}* 2 Sam. 24:1. There was at this time no just occasion to require this, all enemies being subdued, and the land enjoying peace. To say the least, it was overmuch curiosity in him, to know the number of his people fit for war: But with curiosity there was also without question much arrogancy and pride mixed. Under the word Israel such as were fit to go to war are comprised: for they are said to be men that drew sword, v. 5. It is added 2 Sam. 24:2. *Thorow all the tribes of Israel*. For all the tribes were in subjection to David; he reigned over them all.

*{from Beer-sheba even to Dan}* See 1 Kings 4:25, and 19:3.

*{and bring the number of them to me, that I may know it}* Or, *number the people, that I may know the number of the people*, 2 Sam. 24:2. This end of numbering the people argues the pride of David’s heart herein.

**V. 3.** *{And Joab answered}* 2 Sam. 24:3. *And Joab said unto the King*.

*{The LORD}* Or, *Now the Lord thy God*, 2 Sam. 24:3. This phrase of relation, *thy God* carries an argument in it to dissuade David from persisting in his purpose. To sin against the Lord, who was in special manner his God, was a great aggravation.

*{make his people an hundred times so many more as they be}* Or, *add unto the people (how many soever they be*) an hundred fold, 2 Sam. 24:3. This Joab premises, to show that his unwillingness to number the people was not from any envy, because they were so many; for he could well wish them very many more. Besides, it intimates a second argument to dissuade David from his purpose; because whether they were many or few, the Lord could much increase them. It is added, 2 Sam. 24:3. *And that the eyes of my Lord the King may see it*; namely if there should be any need of a great multitude, or occasion to use them.

*{but, my lord the king}* He speaks thus sweetly and submissively unto him, the more to work upon him.

*{are they not all my Lords servants?}* This is a third argument, by which Joab implies, that all the people being faithful servants to David, there was no need of numbering them, there being no fear of any unfaithfulness. For when soldiers are numbered, some new oath or bond uses to be imposed upon them.

*{Why then doth my lord require this thing}* Or, *delight in this thing*. By this it appears that David was somewhat tickled in his mind with an high conceit of the multitude of his people.

*{Why will he be a cause of trespass to Israel?}* Here Joab shows that he apprehended David’s desire to be sinful, and that it would prove prejudicial to his people.

**V. 4.** *{Nevertheless the kings word prevailed against Joab}* He was no whit moved with Joab’s arguments, but was peremptory in his resolution, and would have Joab do what he commanded him. This much aggravated David’s sin: for the good counsel of a servant is not to be despised, Job 31:13. It is added 2 Sam. 24:4. *And against the captains of the host*. Whereby it appears that other captains besides Joab, dissuaded the king from prosecuting his purpose; but he would hearken to none of them.

*{wherefore Joab}* And the captains of the host, 2 Sam. 24:4. Joab and they all agreed, both to dissuade the king, and also to obey the king when they saw he would not be removed from his purpose.

*{departed}* Or, *went out from the presence of the king to number the people of Israel*, 2 Sam. 24:4. When they saw they could not prevail with David, they tarried no longer with him, but went to execute what he had given them in charge: A question is here moved, Whether they did well, or no, in obeying the king. They who hold they did not well, allege these reasons: 1. It is unlawful to be accessory to sin. 2. They did it against their conscience. 3. They themselves judged some vengeance would follow thereupon. But a general answer may be given to all these, That the act required of them which was to number the people was not simply sinful, see v. 1. The sin was in David’s mind, and in the end he propounded to himself, to which Joab and the captains were not accessory. Neither can it be said they sinned against their conscience: For howsoever they might think David did not well in pressing the thing so far as he did, yet in their conscience they might be persuaded that they ought rather to obey their king in his resolute command than incur his displeasure by an utter refusal. As for the vengeance they might judge would follow, they were not sure of it, but only feared such a thing might fall out. If they obeyed merely out of a slavish disposition to do anything, though sinful, upon their sovereign’s command, questionless they sinned. Thus Joab sinned, when upon David’s letter he put Uriah in the forefront of the battle, 2 Sam. 11:15, &c.

*{and went throughout all Israel}* From one tribe to another, till they had taken the number of the people in the several tribes. In 2 Sam. 24:5, 6, 7, the particular places where they began, proceeded, ended, are expressly set down.

*{and came to Jerusalem}* When they had gone through all the land, they came to Jerusalem at the end of nine moneths and twenty dayes, 2 Sam. 24:8. It appears it was a great task that was imposed on Joab, both by the great circuit he was to compass, and the long time he was forced to spend therein: The king’s royal palace was at Jerusalem: thence they went to begin their work, and thither they returned when it was ended.

**V. 5.** *{And Joab gave the sum of the number of the people unto David}* Or, *unto the king*, 2 Sam. 24:9. This he did to testify his faithfulness, in obeying his command; and that David, if he pleased, might make a public record of them.

*{And all they of Israel}* Under Israel here are meant the ten tribes; for mention is afterwards made of Judah and Benjamin, in this and the next verse.

*{were a thousand thousand, and an hundred thousand}* There are but eight hundred thousand mentioned 2 Sam. 24:9. For reconciling the difference it is said there were eleven hundred thousand numbered by the rulers that went into several tribes; but because the matter seemed abominable to Joab, v. 6, he was not careful to put all into the public records, but eight hundred thousand only. This Joab might do partly through passion, and partly because he had intelligence of the beginning of the plague, which made him leave off before he had finished all. See ch. 27:24. But the penman of the Chronicles finding other records, put the total sum together in his sacred history. Others answer, that the rulers numbered all the males, whether fit for war or no, which amounted to eleven hundred thousand; and that Joab registered none but valiant men, which were but eight hundred thousand. But it is said that the eleven hundred thousand were men that drew sword, and in that respect fit for war; so as this answer will not well reconcile the case. A third is, That the rulers brought in the number of all that were fit to bear arms, whether before enrolled or no; and that Joab left out the trained bands, which were before enrolled, and did their service in Jerusalem every month. Of these there were four and twenty thousand for each month, in all, two hundred fourscore and eight thousand: And over them there might be twelve thousand commanders and officers, which make up in all three hundred thousand; which being added to the eight hundred thousand, 2 Sam. 24:9, make up the number mentioned in this verse.

*{men that drew sword}* See 2 Kings 3:26. Or, *valiant men that drew sword*, 2 Sam. 24:9. That epithet valiant shows they were men of courage, and had been before tried.

*{and Judah was four hundred threescore and ten thousand, &c.}* 2 Sam. 24:9, five hundred thousand; thirty thousand more than are set down in this place. These thirty thousand might be of the tribe of Benjamin, which are not here set down, v. 6, but were numbered with the men of Judah, 2 Sam. 24:9. Or they might be such as were under the command of those worthies ch. 11:10, &c. and are here omitted because they were before enrolled in a public record.

*{men that drew sword}* The total of all was sixteen hundred thousand men. Though the whole kingdom of David were but small in comparison of other kingdoms, yet I suppose scarce a kingdom in the world yielded so many valiant exercised soldiers.

**V. 6.** *{But Levi and Benjamin counted he not among them}* Some thus interpret these words, that Joab did not put those that were of Levi and Benjamin into the king’s records; whereupon there be but eight hundred thousand in the account, 2 Sam. 24:9. Whereas if all the Levites and Benjamites had been added, they would have made up eleven hundred thousand. But the reason that follows in this verse, and these express words, ch. 27:24, *Joab began to number, but he finished not, because there fell wrath for it against Israel*, imply that these two tribes were not numbered at all. He might forbear to number Levi, because they were to attend the service of the tabernacle; and Benjamin, because the plague was begun there before he returned to that tribe, ch. 27:24.

*{for the Kings word was abominable to Joab}* His heart and mind was utterly against it. What he did, he did against his own inclination and will, being in a manner forced thereunto by the king’s unalterable resolution.

**V. 7.** *{And God was displeased with this thing}* Heb. *and it was evil in the eyes of the Lord concerning this thing*. See 1 Kings 16:25, and 22:52; 2 Kings 3:2. Confidence in the arm of flesh and pride in the multitude of men God cannot endure.

*{therefore he smote Israel}* With pestilence. This is set down by way of anticipation; for the plague began after this, v. 14. Or here in general it being said that God smote them, the order of his smiting them is afterwards particularly set down.

**V. 8.** *{And David said unto God}* Before this it is premised, 2 Sam. 24:10. *And Davids heart smote him after that he had numbred the people*. This smiting of the heart implies tenderness of conscience, when a man upon consideration of some evil he has done checks himself for doing it. This phrase is once before applied to David, 1 Sam. 24:5. And we read it not applied to any other throughout the whole Scripture, though there were many of very tender consciences. It was the sin itself that now troubled him; and when he saw judgment inflicted for the sin, he was again troubled, v. 16, 17, yet his heart continued hardened all the time that Joab was numbering the people, which was nine months and twenty days, 2 Sam. 25:8. While sin is in committing, man uses to have little remorse; but when it is finished, then the conscience is wont to be troubled.

*{I have sinned greatly}* His tender conscience makes him aggravate his sin, but not beyond his own just apprehension of the greatness thereof. For he was conscious to his own inward intention.

*{because I have done this thing}* Joab intimated as much to him before, v. 3, but a touched conscience works more upon a man than all the arguments that other men can use.

*{but now I beseech thee}* *O Lord*, is added, 2 Sam. 24:10. His guilty conscience makes him humble and earnest in his prayers.

*{do away the iniquity}* Or, *pardon the sin*. This is that an humble pious heart more desires than preventing of judgment.

*{of thy servant}* He mentions this relation betwixt God and himself both to aggravate his sin, in that a servant of God should so transgress; and also to strengthen his faith and hope of pardon.

*{for I have done very foolishly}* He condemns himself for attempting such a matter so rashly, himself not well advising about it, nor hearkening to the good advice of his servants.

**V. 9.** *{And the LORD spake unto Gad}* In 2 Sam. 24:11 this clause is premised, *For when David was up in the morning*. Whence some infer that the reason why David’s heart smote him was, because Gad told him of God’s displeasure against him *for* his sin. But that causal particle for is not in the original. It is only a copulative particle, *And David rose*. This was questionless the next morning after Joab had given up the number of the people to him, and he had deprecated pardon. Though confession of sin, and begging of pardon be acceptable to God, yet he may inflict judgment for it, as 2 Sam. 12:13, 14, and that to make the sinner more wary of doing the like again, to show his indignation against the sin itself, to make others fear, and to vindicate his justice.

*{Davids Seer, saying}* 2 Sam. 24:11. the word of the Lord came unto the Prophet Gad, Davids Seer, saying. Here we see the same man may be both a prophet and a seer. Of the difference betwixt them see 2 Kings 17:13.

**V. 10.** *{Go and tell David, saying, Thus saith the LORD}* See 1 Kings 12:24.

*{I offer thee three things}* Heb. *I stretch out over thee*. For the holding forth of a thing to another is an outward offering of it. God might have set before David many judgments, more in number, and terribler in kind, but this restraining them to three is an evidence of his fatherly indulgence to David; yet so as that hereby he shows that he had deserved all those three judgments.

*{choose thee one of them}* This further amplifies God’s tenderness to David: He would not inflict all the three judgments on him, but only one, and that one to be chosen by himself. Known unto God are all his works from the beginning of the world, Acts 15:18, and he does nothing in time that he has not decreed before all time; yet he puts this to David’s choice, to show that he overrules the free and voluntary intentions of men, Prov. 16:1, 9, and that man’s choosing this or that does not cross the decree of God, nor God’s decree take away freedom of will or election of this or that from man.

*{that I may do it unto thee}* This shows God’s resolved purpose to punish David’s sin, notwithstanding his penitency v. 8. But it was only with a temporary punishment, to make him the more watchful for the future; to warn others also, and make them fear; and to show that God will not bolster up his dearest children in sin.

**V. 11.** *{So Gad came to David}* Though it were a very sad message that he brought to the king, yet this faithful prophet being sent of God came to David.

*{and said unto him, Thus saith the LORD}* See v. 10.

*{Choose thee}* Heb. *Take to thee*. This fitly answers the former Hebraism, v. 10. He that offers, stretches, or holds out; he that chooses, takes to himself.

**V. 12.** *{Either three years famine}* This in 2 Sam. 24:13 is thus set down, *Shall seven years of famine come unto thee in the land?* The difference may thus be reconciled. This was the fourth year from the beginning of that three years famine, 2 Sam. 21:1, so that if there had been three years famine more, it would have been accounted a famine of seven years. In this place the Holy Ghost has reference only to the time to come, and therefore mentions but three years; in the other he has reference both to the time past and also to the time to come, and in that respect extends it to seven.

*{or three months to be destroyed before thy foes}* Or, *or wilt thou flye three moneths before thine enemies*, 2 Sam. 24:13. If men fly before their enemies, many of them must needs be destroyed; for enemies use to be fierce and cruel when they pursue the adverse party flying before them. Therefore these two phrases, fly, and be destroyed, do very well cohere and agree together, as the cause and the effect. And for an enemy to be three months pursuing, must needs infer a great destruction of them that are pursued.

*{while the sword of thine enemies overtaketh thee}* Or, *while they pursue thee*, 2 Sam. 24:13. To be overtaken by the sword of an enemy, is to be wounded and slain thereby; which destruction they that fly before an enemy, and are pursued by him, can hardly avoid.

*{or else three days the sword of the LORD, even the pestilence in the land}* This is thus plainly set down 2 Sam. 24:13. *Or that there be three dayes pestilence in thy land*. The pestilence is called the sword of the Lord, in opposition to war, wherein a material sword by the hand of man is used. The pestilence is a more immediate stroke of God than war or famine: For in war men are destroyed by the hand of man, namely by such instruments as man uses: Famine also uses to happen by the means of other creatures, as extreme heat and drought, 1 Kings 17:1. Over-great violence of waters, Prov. 28:3. Caterpillars, or other like fruit-devouring creatures, Joel 1:4. But the pestilence oft comes so as no man can discern any external cause thereof; and many times some are so destroyed, and others so preserved, as no man can give any outward reason of the difference: so as this kind of judgment is more immediately ordered by God than either famine or sword.

*{and the Angel of the LORD destroying}* Great question is made whether this were properly one of the angels or no? and if an angel whether a good or a bad one? It is without all question, that a true proper angel may in the space of three days, and less, go through a whole land; and destroy as many as were here destroyed. For an angel is a spirit, and therefore not hindered in his motion by any bodily obstacle. And divine vengeance being an effect of justice, a good angel may execute it. God also may use an evil angel to execute his judgments. But whatsoever it be that he employs in the performing of his will, may in that respect be styled an angel, because it is sent of God. For the word angel, according to its proper notation, signifies *such an one as is sent*, or *a messenger*, Mal. 3:1.

*{throughout all the coasts of Israel}* It is said, 2 Sam. 24:1, 2, *That the anger of the Lord was kindled against Israel*, and that David bid Joab go through all the tribes of Israel: justly therefore is this destroyer commanded to go *throughout all the coasts of Israel*. The judgment is answerable to the sin.

*{now therefore advise thy self}* Answers to the Lord must not rashly, but with good counsel and advice, be returned.

*{what word I shall bring again to him that sent me}* Or, *see what answer I shall return, &c*., 2 Sam. 24:13. Hereby the prophet implies that God will not be mocked or dallied with by children of men. They whom he sends, or to whom he sends, must be careful to satisfy his expectation.

**V. 13.** *{And David said unto Gad, I am in a great strait}* Every of the judgments was so terrible as he scarce knew what to say. He well discerned that the Lord was resolved to execute one of them, so as he could not escape them all; and for him to choose any of them was a kind of terror unto him; and this brought him into a great strait.

*{let me fall now}* Or, *let us fall now*, 2 Sam. 24:14. Of such a kind was every of the judgments as none of them could fall upon him alone; and therefore he says in the plural number, *let us fall*. Besides, he was the principal cause of the judgment, and he himself was in danger thereof; and therefore he says also in the singular number, *let me fall*.

*{into the hand of the LORD}* He submits himself and his people to that kind of judgment which was most immediately ordered by the Lord: This was the pestilence. See v. 12.

*{for very great are his mercies}* Or, *many are his mercies*. This is added as the reason why he chose that judgment which God most immediately ordered: He knew that God was pitiful and merciful; and that if, in the midst of judgments, he saw a people penitently humbled, he would cease to smite, and spare his people.

*{but let me not fall into the hand of man}* By this phrase he deprecates the other two judgments, war, and famine, and prays that neither of them may fall upon him, or his people. It was showed before, that man has his hand in war; man smites with the sword, casts the javelin, shoots the arrow or the bullet, orders all warlike instruments; yea, he orders the beasts that are employed: So as according to the mind of man greater or less cruelty is used in war. But men for the most part are very cruel against their enemies. Of them thus says God, *I was but a little displeased, and they helped forward the affliction*, Zech. 1:15; Isa. 47:6; 2 Chron. 28:9. Something also may be attributed to man about famine, in that the rich use by hoarding and heaping up for the future to make the famine more grievous to those that have nothing but for the very present. David, by choosing pestilence rather than famine or war, gives evidence both of his piety and charity. Of piety, in that he would have God only sought unto and praised for deliverance: Both which would be done more in a plague, than in a famine or war. Of charity, in that he chooses such a kind of judgment as would oppress the poor no more than the rich, and subjects no more than princes. All persons are alike subject to the pestilence: But in famine the wealthier may better provide for themselves than the meaner sort: In war also great persons may get castles and other places of strength to defend themselves in, or may give ransoms for themselves, when the poor have no means at all to help themselves against the enemy’s rage.

**V. 14.** *{So the LORD sent pestilence upon Israel}* The pestilence is a kind of venomous disease, than which there is none more mortal to the person infected, or more infectious to others.

*{and there fell of Israel seventy thousand men}* Here was a wonderful slaughter on a sudden. We read of fourteen thousand and seven hundred destroyed by a plague, on such a sudden as Aaron could not, time enough, prepare incense to make an atonement for them, Num. 16:47, 49. There died twenty-four thousand at another time in the wilderness, and on a sudden, Num. 25:9. In one night such an angel as this here mentioned destroyed an hundred fourscore and five thousand, 2 Kings 19:35. These great destructions show that God had an especial hand therein. God is said to do this, 2 Sam. 24:15, *from the morning even to the time appointed*, i.e. from the morning after that Gad had brought David’s answer to the Lord, till the end of the three days appointed v. 12. It is further added 2 Sam. 24:15, that *there died of the people, even from Dan to Beer-sheba*, i.e. from one end of the land to the other. See v. 2. Hereby is verified the extent of the threatening, v. 12, *throughout all the coasts of Israel*.

**V. 15.** *{And God sent an Angel unto Jerusalem to destroy it}* This was the angel mentioned, v. 12, namely, the pestilence. Though the commandment for numbering the people were given in Jerusalem, yet the execution of that command in Jerusalem was put off to the last, v. 4. And in Jerusalem was the ark of God’s covenant, ch. 15:29, and there David humbled himself, v. 8. The execution therefore of the judgment on Jerusalem was also put off to the last.

*{and as he was destroying}* Or, *when the Angel stretched out his hand upon Jerusalem to destroy it*. The citizens of Jerusalem had deserved this judgment as well as the people in other places of the land; and therefore the rod was shaken over them.

*{the LORD beheld}* This is spoken after the manner of man. It shows that when God sends a messenger of his wrath to punish man, he gives not over the whole power to him, but observes how he uses the power given him, and restrains or puts him on, as seems good to himself.

*{and he repented him}* This also is applied to God after the manner of men. For *God is not as man, that he should repent*, 1 Sam. 15:29. But when he leaves off to do what he has begun, he is said to repent, Gen. 6:6; Exod. 32:14; Judg. 2:18. God may alter a course which he has begun, but he never alters his mind. He meant in the beginning to spare Jerusalem.

*{of the evil}* By evil is meant the judgment inflicted; not because it was evil in the nature of it, (for it was an execution of justice, and in that respect good) but in regard of the pain and loss which men felt thereby it seemed to them evil. Thus God is said to create evil, Isa. 45:7.

*{and said to the Angel that destroyed}* *the people*, is added 2 Sam. 24:16. Destroyers of men are at God’s disposing.

*{It is enough}* The Lord well knows what kind, manner, and measure of affliction is fit for his people.

*{stay now thine hand}* God will not suffer his to be punished above that that is meet and sufficient. This restraint was questionless after David’s prayer, v. 17. It is usual in Scripture first to set down the thing itself, and then the means or occasion of effecting it. See v. 7.

*{And the Angel of the LORD stood by the threshing-floor of Ornan the Jebusite}* Or, *Araunah*, 2 Sam. 24:16. Ornan is a name contracted of Araunah: the same radical letters are in both names. The reason why the angel here stood, is because the Lord intended this threshing-floor for the place of his holy service, v. 18, 28; 2 Chron. 3:1.

**V. 16.** *{And David lift up his eyes, and saw the Angel of the LORD}* It seems the messenger whom God sent to destroy Israel appeared in a visible shape; else how could David see him with the eyes of his body? and it is probable that he appeared like a man, as angels at other times used to do, Gen. 19:1, 5. But whether this angel in such a shape went through the whole land or no, is questionable.

*{stand between the earth and the heaven}* Thus he stood in the air, and was thereby the more visible, and the more terrible, and had power to destroy the more.

*{having a drawn sword in his hand}* A sword is an usual instrument of destruction; by drawing it an intention to destroy is implied. Hereupon this destroyer is thus presented to David’s eyes. See on Gen. 3:24.

*{stretched out over Jerusalem}* This implies a threatening of destruction to Jerusalem.

*{Then David}* when he saw the Angel had smote the people, 2 Sam. 24:17.

*{and the Elders of Israel}* See 1 Kings 8:1. These elders either saw the angel, as well as David, or else were moved with the judgment on other places, or were by David commanded to do what they did.

*{who were clothed in sackcloth}* This circumstance apparently shows that the judgment on other places of the land wrought, at least, upon David: For they went clothed with sackcloth when they saw the angel, they did not then first put it on. See 1 Kings 21:27.

*{fell upon their faces}* A gesture testifying deep humiliation, shame, and grief for sin, Gen. 17:3, 17; Lev. 9:24; Num. 14:5, and 16:22; Josh. 5:14; Judg. 13:20.

**V. 17.** *{And David said unto God}* David, as a king, as a prophet, and as the principal in this sin, does most of all deprecate the judgment, and pray that it may be removed.

*{Is it not I that commanded the people to be numbered?}* He prudently takes notice of and confesses that particular sin which was the immediate cause of the judgment, and patently acknowledges himself the chief actor therein.

*{even I it is that have sinned and done evil indeed}* Or, *Lo, I have sinned, and I have done wickedly*, 2 Sam. 24:17. This pressing upon himself the sin again and again, I, even I, Lo I; multiplying of phrases, sinned, done evil, done wickedly; ratifying all by notes of asseveration, Lo, indeed; setting down the points interrogatively, Is it not I? &c. do all amplify a deep apprehension of his own special guiltiness, and lay the heavier burden upon his soul. This is an evidence of true penitency, and a means to move God to pity a distressed soul. For God uses to ease those who load themselves, and to load those who seek unduly to ease themselves.

*{but as for these sheep}* He styles his people sheep; I. In relation to that charge he had over them. For a king is as a shepherd to his people, 1 Kings 22:17; Isa. 44:28. II. In regard of the great slaughter made amongst them. For many sheep were wont to be slain together for sacrifice, 1 Kings 8:63; 2 Chron. 15:11, and 29:33, and 30:24, and when wolves enter into a flock of sheep, they use to devour many. Sundry proverbial phrases are raised from the destruction of sheep; as, Sheep appointed for meat, Ps. 44:11. Like sheep they are laid in the grave, Ps. 49:14. Like sheep for the slaughter, Jer. 12:3; Ps. 44:22. III. In regard of their innocency in this particular. For sheep are counted innocent creatures.

*{what have they done?}* David does not acquit them of all sin, but only of that for which the plague was sent, namely numbering of the people.

*{Let thine hand, I pray thee, O LORD my God, be on me}* Or, *against me*, 2 Sam. 24:17. He does not simply pray for vengeance on himself, but comparatively, rather upon himself who had committed that sin, than upon the people.

*{and on my fathers house}* By his father’s house he means his kindred, his household-servants, his courtiers, his counselors, such as by any special bond of relation were knit unto him; who haply might some way or other be accessory to his sin: at least, by their near relation to him the punishment would more appear to be on him.

*{but not on thy people}* This is the main thing he aims at, that God would spare the people. In this pronoun thy an argument is couched, namely, That God would therefore spare them because they professed his name.

*{that they should be plagued}* Or, *that they should dye of the plague*. That destruction of seventy thousand v. 14 so moved his bowels, as he wished himself rather might die of the plague than any more of the people.

**V. 18.** *{Then the Angel of the LORD}* That angel which was before an executioner of God’s vengeance, is now a messenger to direct David how to pacify God’s wrath.

*{commanded Gad to say to David}* God so far honors sons of men, that are his prophets, and ministers of his word, as to send his angels to instruct them in what they are to direct his people, rather than to make angels themselves immediate instructors, Acts 10:5, 6.

*{that David should go up}* The place whereunto he was directed, was on mount Moriah, 2 Chron. 3:1. Therefore he was to go up to it.

*{and set up an Altar unto the LORD}* This was a means of pacifying God’s wrath and removing the plague: for on that altar sacrifices were to be offered, v. 22, 23, 26, and by it the sacrifices were sanctified, Matt. 23:10.

*{in the threshing-floor of Ornan the Jebusite}* In that place Ornan used to thresh his wheat, v. 20, therefore it was called his threshing-floor. There stood the angel with his sword drawn when he was about to destroy Jerusalem, v. 20, therefore there must the means of atonement be erected, that so the plague might be stayed, and spread no farther. It is noted, 2 Sam. 24:18, that Gad came that day to David, and said to him, *Go up*, &c. Gad like a true prophet, delivers his message to David in the very same words in which he had received it from the angel: 1 Cor. 11:23.

**V. 19.** *{And David went up at the saying of Gad}* Or, *according to the saying of Gad*. David was as conscionable in observing the direction, as Gad was in delivering it. For he went up as the Lord commanded, 2 Sam. 24:19.

*{which he spake in the name of the LORD}* It was Gad who thus spoke: and he spoke by warrant from the Lord; with power and authority, as from the Lord; and that very message which he had received from the Lord: All these are implied under this phrase, *In the name of the Lord*.

**V. 20.** *{And Ornan turned back, and saw the Angel; and his four sons with him hid themselves}* Or, *when Ornan turned back and saw the Angel, then he and his four sons with him hid themselves*. Here is a further evidence that the angel appeared in a visible shape. See v. 16. Questionless both Ornan and his four sons were in their calling, threshing together, and expected no such sight: But on a sudden they saw it, which made it the more terrible unto them: and thereupon they all hid themselves, Prov. 22:3.

*{Now Ornan was threshing wheat}* Ornan had been a king, 2 Sam. 24:23, but being subdued and brought under by David, he was content to perform a mean subject’s part, and thresh his wheat; or at least he was there overseeing his sons while they did it.

**V. 21.** *{And as David came to Ornan}* For Ornan was at the place whither Gad directed David.

*{Ornan looked and saw David}* It is added 2 Sam. 24:20. *The King and his servants coming on towards him*. For David came as a king, with his attendants.

*{and went out of the threshing-floor}* He would not tarry till the king should come to him, but in a good and dutiful respect went out to meet him.

*{and bowed himself to David with his face to the ground}* See 1 Kings 1:23.

**V. 22.** *{Then David said to Ornan}* Before this it is noted, 2 Sam. 24:21, that Araunah said, *Wherefore is my lord the king come to his servant?* As Araunah’s gesture, so his words manifest a very great respect to David; for he acknowledges him to be not only a king, but also his sovereign, and himself to be his servant. And that he might the better know wherein to testify further respect, he diligently inquires after the occasion of his coming to him.

*{Grant me}* Heb. *give me*. He did not require what he asked upon free gift, but only desired a readiness in Ornan to let him have it upon a valuable price. Thus is the word give used, 1 Kings 21:2.

*{the place of this threshing-floor}* 2 Sam. 24:21 this is set down as the end of David’s coming to Araunah, thus, *And David said, To buy the threshing-floor of thee*. Both may well stand together: David might first tell him why he came, and afterwards make the motion.

*{That I may build an Altar therein unto the LORD}* See the reason, v. 18.

*{thou shalt grant it me for the full price}* He desired not the place gratis, nor yet for a small matter, but for a valuable and full consideration.

*{that the plague may be stayed from the people}* This was the main end of building that altar. See v. 18.

**V. 23.** *{And Ornan said unto David, Take it to thee}* His mind was, that he should have it gratis.

*{and let my LORD the King do that which is good in his eyes}* 2 Sam. 24:22, take and offer up what seemeth good unto him. Ornan was content that what himself had by him should be offered, or what else David pleased.

*{Lo, I give thee the oxen also}* He means those with which he was threshing: for they were wont to tread out their corn with oxen, Deut. 25:4.

*{for burnt offerings}* Oxen were an especial kind of beasts by the law required for burnt-offerings, Exod. 20:24; Num. 7:87.

*{and the threshing-instruments}* i.e. Whatsoever was used to thresh or beat out his corn.

*{for wood}* To be laid on the altar to burn the sacrifices withal, Lev. 1:7, 8.

*{and the wheat for the meat offering}* See 1 Kings 3:15; Num. 18:12.

*{I give it all}* He offered all as a mere free gift, both to testify his good respect to David, and also to contribute what he could towards the removing of the plague, whereof he and his sons stood in great fear, v. 20. It is added 2 Sam. 24:23. *All these things did Araunah, as a King, give unto the King: and Araunah said unto the King, The Lord thy God accept thee*. In the former part of which verse it is implied that Araunah was a king: For that particle of resemblance, as, is not in the Hebrew; but it is said, *Araunah a King gave*. And he might be king of those Jebusites that held the fort of Zion, ch. 11:4, 5, and after the taking of it become a subject unto David, and by conversing among the Israelites turn proselyte, and thereupon freely enjoy a possession among them. And fearing God in truth of heart, he might love and honor David, yea, and all Israel too, and be ready to promote the worship of God in what he could, and to do what lay in his power for averting his wrath. Yet I will not deny but the epithet king may here be applied unto him in regard of his royal disposition, and thus the particle as be fitly supplied. The last clause of that verse, *The Lord thy God accept thee*, gives further evidence, that both fear of God and also faith in God possessed the soul of this man: And it shows that he earnestly desired the preservation of David, and of the remnant of Israel, as well as his own; and that he believed the preservation of them all depended upon God’s gracious acceptation of David’s offering, which was a type of Christ. He says of the Lord, in relation to David, *thy God*, because he knew that David was a faithful servant of the Lord, and that the Lord was in special manner the God of Israel: yea further, because he himself was a stranger by birth, though now incorporated into the church, he says, *thy God*. Araunah was a sign of the calling of the Gentiles; that God would be worshiped where they were, as he was worshiped in the threshing-floor of Araunah; and that, as Araunah did, they would offer what they had to the service of God.

**V. 24.** *{And King David said to Ornan, Nay}* David would not receive so great a gift freely at the hand of Ornan, because being a great and wealthy king, he could better pay for it than Ornan part with it for nothing. Besides, he would not render services and offer sacrifices to God upon another man’s cost, lest such offerings should rather be accounted his that gave the things to be offered, than his that offered the same. It may be also that David was not willing to be beholding to Ornan for so great a courtesy. See Gen. 14:21, 22, 23, and 23:11, 12, 13.

*{but I will verily buy it for the full price}* David was so far from accepting it gratis, as he would not purchase it at an under rate, but would give the full worth for it.

*{for I will not take that which is thine for the LORD}* He desired not that place for his own profit or pleasure, as Ahab coveted Naboth’s vineyard, 1 Kings 21:2, but for the Lord’s service; and therefore he would not have it at another man’s cost.

*{nor offer burnt offerings without cost}* He means without his own cost, or his own paying for it. He was not like that man who took a poor man’s sheep to entertain his friend therewith, 2 Sam. 12:2, 3, 4. See 1 Kings 3:15.

**V. 25.** *{So David gave to Ornan for the place}* i.e. For all that building where the threshing-floor was, and all the land about it that belonged to Ornan: For the temple was there afterwards built. So as Ornan after this possessed neither houses nor land thereabouts. By this large signification of the word place the difference 2 Sam. 24:24 may be easily reconciled.

*{six hundred shekels of gold by weight}* 2 Sam. 24:24 it is, *So David bought the threshing-floor and the oxen for fifty shekels of silver*. There is a great difference between these two prices. For fifty shekels of silver amount but to six pounds five shillings sterling; but six hundred shekels of gold, to seven hundred and fifty pounds. See 1 Kings 10:16. The less price was paid only for the oxen, instruments and threshing-floor; but the greater for all Ornan’s land and houses thereabouts. Or it may be that Ornan priced all that David desired only at fifty shekels of silver, but David of his royal bounty would give him six hundred shekels of gold by weight. It is said of Solomon, 1 Kings 10:13, that *he gave unto the Queen of Sheba all her desire whatsoever she asked, besides that which Solomon gave her of his royal bounty*: And so might David here do to Ornan.

**V. 26.** *{And David built there an altar unto the LORD}* This he did upon the direction of the angel, v. 18.

*{and offered burnt offerings and peace offerings}* See 1 Kings 3:15. He added these offerings to his confession of sin and craving of pardon, v. 8, to show that all we can do is nothing without that satisfaction which Christ has made for our sins.

*{and called upon the LORD}* Prayer must be joined with all other warrantable services; for by it a blessing on all is obtained from the Lord. Therefore it is said, 2 Sam. 24:23, that *the Lord was entreated for the land*.

*{and he answered him from heaven by fire, &c.}* See 1 Kings 18:24; 2 Chron. 7:1. The fire wherewith sacrifices were to be burned was at Gibeon, where the altar for burnt-offerings then was, ch. 16:40. But thither none dared go because of the plague, v. 30, and with no ordinary fire might a sacrifice be offered, Lev. 10:1, 2, therefore David prayed for fire from heaven, and the Lord sent it: in which respect it is said that *he answered him from heaven by fire*.

**V. 27.** *{And the LORD commanded the Angel}* That angel mentioned v. 16. The Lord has a command over destroyers, to put them on, or to restrain them, as he pleases.

*{and he put up his sword again into the sheath thereof}* This has relation to the drawn sword, v. 16. As that phrase set out destruction by the plague, so this imports a ceasing to destroy. And thereupon it is said, 2 Sam. 24:25, *the plague was staied from Israel*. This shows that the restraint noted v. 15 is to be referred to this place, and that it followed upon David’s offering and calling upon the Lord.

**V. 28.** *{At that time}* Or, *after that time*. For this has reference to further actions of David.

*{when David saw that the LORD had answered him}* V. 26.

*{in the threshing-floor of Ornan the Jebusite}* See v. 18.

*{then he sacrificed there}* He did not only offer up those solemn burnt-offerings, v. 26, upon that extraordinary occasion, but also afterwards on other occasions he there sacrificed. This was a place sanctified of old by Abraham’s building an altar there, Gen. 22:2, 9, and afterwards, 2 Chron. 3:1.

**V. 29.** *{For the Tabernacle of the LORD which Moses made in the wilderness}* See Exod. 36:8, &c.

*{and the Altar of the burnt offering}* See Exod. 38:1, &c.

*{were at that season}* When this plague was inflicted.

*{in the high place at Gibeon}* See ch. 16:39; 1 Kings 3:4.

**V. 30.** *{But David could not go before it to enquire of God}* The tabernacle was the place where the priests attended; and by the priests both the king and others used to inquire of God: but the tabernacle being at Gibeon, David could not go thither.

*{for he was afraid}* Even as Ornan and his sons were, v. 20. For the plague when it rages makes all of all sorts afraid.

*{because of the sword of the Angel of the LORD}* i.e. The plague. See v. 14, 15, 16. It appears by this that many fell by the plague at Gibeon.

## CHAPTER 22

**V. 1.** *{Then David said}* When God had testified his acceptation of his sacrifice, both by fire from heaven, and also by removing the plague, ch. 21:26, 27.

*{This is the house of the LORD God}* i.e. The place where the temple is to be built.
